Motivation: The standard RLS algorithm offers fast convergence for adaptive filtering but suffers from high computational complexity that limits practical deployment for long filters.

Method: Approximates the inverse covariance matrix as a block-diagonal structure to enable independent parallel computations of sub-blocks, and applies Tikhonov regularization to each sub-block for numerical stability.

Result: RBD-RLS maintains good convergence while significantly reducing computational complexity and exhibits relative robustness in real-world scenarios.

Conclusion: The proposed RBD-RLS algorithm successfully balances convergence performance and computational efficiency, making it more practical for real-world acoustic echo cancellation applications.

Abstract: While the recursive least square (RLS) algorithm is widely used in adaptive filtering applications like acoustic echo cancellation (AEC) due to its fast convergence rate, its high computational complexity severely limit its practical deployment for long filters. In this paper, a regularized block-diagonal RLS (RBD-RLS) algorithm is proposed to address these challenges. By approximating the inverse covariance matrix as a block-diagonal structure, RBD-RLS simplifies the update process into independent parallel computations of sub-blocks, effectively reducing the computational complexity. Additionally, Tikhonov regularization is applied to each sub-blocks for enhance numerical stability. A series of experimental results demonstrate that RBD-RLS maintains good convergence while significantly reducing computational complexity. Moreover, it still exhibits relative robustness in real-world scenarios.

</details>


### [89] [Do SpeechLMs Hear Their Own Opinions? Diagnosing and Mitigating Previous-Belief Contamination in Streaming Emotion Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20769)
*Haoyue Liu,Zhichao Wang,Ye Chen,Haonan Deng,Xiaoying Tang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper identifies and addresses "Previous-Belief Contamination (PBC)" in streaming emotion understanding, where a model's previous predictions contaminate current audio perception. The proposed EmoUpdate framework uses three components (prior-blind acoustic firewall, evidence-shrunk causal belief filter, and closed-form decontamination operator) to separate current-audio perception from historical state revision, achieving significant improvements across four SpeechLMs and two benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Streaming emotion understanding systems use historical state as context, feeding previous predictions back. This study reveals that this history conditioning severely distorts current perception, causing accuracy drops from 72.50% to 30.42% and prediction flips in 65.69% of cases when only the previous emotion label is changed. This represents a fundamental failure mode in current systems.

Method: The authors introduce EmoUpdate, a training-free framework with three components: (1) A prior-blind acoustic firewall that prevents historical state from entering the perception module; (2) An evidence-shrunk causal belief filter that introduces history only after observation formation and retains label-asymmetric transition structure only when supported by evidence; (3) A closed-form decontamination operator derived from counterfactual measurements for cases where firewalling is unavailable.

Result: EmoUpdate achieves the best step accuracy and state-balanced accuracy across all eight model-benchmark settings (four SpeechLMs × two benchmarks). Improvements include up to 69.71 points in S-BAcc and up to 38.41 points in step accuracy over the strongest controlled baselines. The method reveals label-asymmetric prior pull effects ranging from 4.76% to 98.20%.

Conclusion: Previous-belief contamination (PBC) is a significant failure in streaming emotion understanding systems. EmoUpdate provides an effective training-free solution that separates current-audio perception from historical state revision, successfully addressing PBC across multiple models and benchmarks with substantial performance gains.

Abstract: Streaming emotion understanding uses historical state while continuously interpreting current audio, often feeding the model's previous prediction back as context. We show that this history conditioning can distort current perception. On a balanced CREMA-D-Stream counterfactual diagnostic, changing only the injected previous emotion label while holding the audio fixed reduces current-audio accuracy from 72.50% to 30.42% and flips 65.69% of predictions. The effect is strongly label-asymmetric, with prior pull ranging from 4.76% to 98.20%, revealing a failure we call previous-belief contamination (PBC). To address PBC, we introduce EmoUpdate, a training-free framework that separates current-audio perception from historical state revision through three components: (1) a prior-blind acoustic firewall that prevents historical state from entering perception; (2) an evidence-shrunk causal belief filter that introduces history only after observation formation and retains label-asymmetric transition structure only when supported by observed evidence; and (3) a closed-form decontamination operator derived from the same counterfactual measurements for serving stacks where firewalling is unavailable. Across four SpeechLMs and two streaming emotion benchmarks, EmoUpdate achieves the best step accuracy and state-balanced accuracy in all eight model--benchmark settings, improving S-BAcc by up to 69.71 points and step accuracy by up to 38.41 points over the strongest controlled baselines.

</details>


### [90] [AudioWorldSim: Realistic Binaural Audio Datasets For World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21075)
*Luis Vitor Zerkowski,Luiz Velho*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: AudioWorldSim is an open-source platform for generating realistic binaural audio datasets to advance audio-based machine learning and world models, built as an extension of Meta's SoundSpaces 2.0 with automatic random agent navigation rollout and fixes to continuous sound composition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses the need for realistic binaural audio datasets to advance audio-based machine learning, particularly world models. Existing platforms lacked automatic rollout capabilities and had issues with continuous sound composition.

Method: Built as a custom extension of Meta's SoundSpaces 2.0 platform, leveraging its comprehensive acoustics framework. Implements automatic rollout of random agent navigations and fixes to continuous sound composition. Made publicly available on GitHub for reproducibility.

Result: A functional open-source platform called AudioWorldSim that generates realistic binaural audio datasets with automatic agent navigation capabilities and corrected continuous sound composition.

Conclusion: AudioWorldSim provides the research community with a reproducible tool for generating binaural audio datasets, which should facilitate advancement in audio-based machine learning and world model research.

Abstract: This technical report presents AudioWorldSim, an open-source platform designed to generate realistic binaural audio datasets and advance research in audio-based machine learning, particularly world models. Built as a custom extension of Meta's SoundSpaces 2.0 platform, AudioWorldSim leverages their comprehensive acoustics framework, but focuses on the automatic rollout of random agent navigations, as well as implements crucial fixes to how continuous sound is composed. AudioWorldSim is made publicly available to the research community at https://github.com/Luizerko/AudioWorldSim to facilitate reproducibility.

</details>


### [91] [DAMOS: Learning Distortion-Aware Speech Quality Assessment through Explicit Distortion Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21176)
*Naiyuan Li,Li Dong,Diqun Yan*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper introduces DAMOS, a distortion-aware speech quality assessment framework that uses explicit distortion localization as auxiliary knowledge to improve MOS prediction accuracy, addressing the limitation of existing methods that only use coarse-grained utterance-level supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing speech quality assessment methods are optimized only with utterance-level MOS, which provides coarse-grained supervision and doesn't indicate where perceptually important distortions occur. Since distortions in speech signals often occur locally and overall quality is dominated by a small number of salient distortion regions, there is a need for explicit distortion localization to improve prediction accuracy.

Method: The authors construct the first partially distorted speech dataset with frame-level distortion annotations, train a localization model to generate distortion cues, and integrate these cues into the MOS prediction pipeline through the DAMOS framework. This combines distortion localization information with quality assessment in an integrated model.

Result: DAMOS consistently outperforms existing methods on multiple public benchmarks and exhibits strong cross-dataset generalization, validating the effectiveness of explicit distortion localization for speech quality assessment.

Conclusion: Explicit distortion localization provides valuable auxiliary knowledge for speech quality assessment. By identifying where perceptually important distortions occur, the model can better predict overall MOS scores. The approach demonstrates both improved performance and good generalization across datasets.

Abstract: Automatic speech quality assessment aims to predict Mean Opinion Scores (MOS) consistent with human subjective perception and is essential for evaluating speech generation, enhancement, and communication systems. For speech signals, especially synthetic speech, distortions often occur locally, and overall perceptual quality is usually dominated by a small number of perceptually salient distortion regions. However, most existing methods are primarily optimized with utterance-level MOS, which provides only coarse-grained supervision and offer no explicit indication of where perceptually important distortions occur. To address this limitation, we introduce explicit distortion localization as auxiliary knowledge for speech quality assessment. We construct the first partially distorted speech dataset with frame-level distortion annotations and train a localization model to generate distortion cues. Building on these cues, we propose DAMOS, a distortion-aware speech quality assessment framework that integrates localization information into the MOS prediction pipeline. Experiments on multiple public benchmarks demonstrate that DAMOS consistently outperforms existing methods and exhibits strong cross-dataset generalization, validating the effectiveness of explicit distortion localization for speech quality assessment.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [92] [Vibe Coding: Practice, Performance, Productivity, and Risk -A State-of-the-Art Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20446)
*Dominik L. Michels,Mutaz Abu Ghazaleh,Francois Lazzari,Nabil Kassem,Jonathan Klein*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A state-of-the-art review assembling the first 17 months of empirical evidence on "vibe coding" (AI-assisted development where developers describe intent in natural language and validate by running rather than reading code). The review spans software engineering, HCI, labor economics, security, governance, and education, finding reliable code generation but weak fault detection, contradictory productivity metrics that can be reconciled through measurement methodology, and documented security failures, code-quality degradation, and skill atrophy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To synthesize the early empirical evidence on vibe coding within 17 months of its naming by Andrej Karpathy, addressing a cross-disciplinary corpus to understand the phenomenon's impacts across multiple domains.

Method: Cross-disciplinary survey examining the model landscape, tool ecosystem, and performance record by task type, with analysis of productivity metrics across different measurement approaches.

Result: Reliable code generation with weak fault detection and hard-to-audit documentation; productivity findings appear contradictory (+26% tasks/week in peer-reviewed experiments, -19% slowdown in randomized trials, +441% code-review time in team telemetry) but are reconcilable when measurement method, scope, and time horizon are held constant; security failures, code-quality degradation, copyright exposure, and skill atrophy documented.

Conclusion: Six patterns explain the dispersion in findings: effect-shrinkage under broader measurement, self-report diverging from independent measurement, output volume conflated with productivity, and bold claims walked back over longer horizons. The author proposes a falsifiable conjecture: gains are real on new code but shrink or reverse on mature codebases, which would account for most disagreement in the record.

Abstract: Vibe coding - AI-assisted software development in which the developer describes intent in natural language and validates results by running rather than reading the generated code - was named by Andrej Karpathy in February 2025 and produced its first body of empirical evidence within seventeen months. This state-of-the-art review assembles that evidence across a cross-disciplinary corpus spanning software engineering, human-computer interaction, labour economics, security research, governance, and education. We survey the model landscape, the tool ecosystem, and the performance record by task type, finding the early benchmarks saturated but task-level capability uneven: reliable code generation alongside weak fault detection and hard-to-audit documentation. The productivity record is at first contradictory: peer-reviewed field experiments report +26% more tasks per week, independent randomised trials measure a 19% slowdown, and team-level telemetry shows code-review time up +441%. We argue these readings are consistent once measurement method, scope, and time horizon are held constant, and identify six patterns behind the dispersion, among them effect-shrinkage under broader measurement, self-report diverging from independent measurement, output volume conflated with productivity, and bold claims walked back once tested over longer horizons. We further document security failures in deployed applications, code-quality degradation visible in large-scale code and developer telemetry, unsettled copyright exposure, and evidence of skill atrophy. The review closes with the open research questions and one falsifiable conjecture: that the gains are real on new code and shrink or reverse on mature codebases, which would account for most of the disagreement in the record.

</details>


### [93] [Making Deployments Safe at Meta: Health Checks for Continuous Change-Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20513)
*Prakash KL,Anton Korenkov,Uttam Thakore,Christopher Hegre*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Meta's Service Health Checker is a prevention-based distributed system that mediates the tension between release velocity and reliability in continuous deployment across thousands of heterogeneous services. It composes templated metric queries, thresholds, and workflow predicates to trigger automatic rollbacks during tiered and phased rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core tension in continuous deployment is that every change is a potential reliability incident, yet every delay is a missed opportunity. The authors aimed to build a system that enables fast releases while automatically preventing regressions from reaching production.

Method: The paper describes: (1) Service Health Checker's architecture for deployment-time health checks, (2) a framework for check authors to compose templated metric queries, thresholds, and workflow predicates, (3) integration with tiered and phased rollouts to trigger automatic rollback when regressions occur, and (4) a program of measurement, tooling, and improved defaults to address operational problems at scale (noise, alert fatigue, drift, and uncovered regressions).

Result: The system addresses operational challenges including noise reduction, alert fatigue mitigation, threshold drift detection, and coverage gaps for regressions. The paper shares lessons learned from years of operating deployment health checks at Meta and explores future directions including AI-assisted health check tuning.

Conclusion: Meta demonstrates that a well-designed prevention-based system can balance release velocity with reliability at scale. The key insight is that addressing operational problems (noise, drift, coverage) is as important as the initial system design. Future work points toward AIOps for automated health check tuning.

Abstract: Continuous deployment to large scale production systems creates a tension between release velocity and reliability. Every change is a potential reliability incident, yet every delay is a missed opportunity. This paper describes the deployment time health check infrastructure that Meta uses to mediate this tension across thousands of heterogeneous services. We summarize the architecture of this prevention based distributed system's service called Service Health Checker, explain how check authors compose templated metric queries, thresholds, and workflow predicates; and discuss how the system is integrated with tiered and phased rollouts so that regressions trigger automatic rollback. We then describe the operational problems that emerged at scale, such as noise, alert fatigue, drift, and uncovered regressions, and the program of measurement, tooling, and improved defaults we deployed to address them. We close with lessons learned from years of operating deployment health checks at Meta, and the directions we are exploring next, including AI assisted health check tuning. Index Terms: deployment safety, continuous deployment, monitoring, software reliability, release engineering, software reliability engineering, AIOps, anomaly detection

</details>


### [94] [Testing and Evaluation of Agentic AI Systems In Military Command and Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20597)
*Ulysse Richard,Heather Frase,Sarah Cao,Di Cooke,Sebastian Kwon,Adrianna Tan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper examines whether current Testing and Evaluation (T&E) methods can adequately assure agentic AI systems for military Command and Control (C2). Through reviewing 240 T&E practices across eight evaluation dimensions and three lifecycle stages, the authors identify eight assumptions (grouped into four clusters: specifiability, stability, composability, supervisability) that agentic AI properties undermine. The research finds that while test results may satisfy process requirements, they do not warrant inferring tested behavior will match fielded behavior. Ten assurance claims are derived, with operational consequences mapped through five C2 scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems are being procured for military C2 under commitments to rigorous testing and human oversight. This research questions whether such commitments can actually be discharged through current T&E methods, given the novel properties of agentic AI that may break assumptions underlying established testing practices.

Method: Structured review of 240 documented T&E practices spanning eight evaluation dimensions and three lifecycle stages. Assessed whether current and emerging methods can address ten derived assurance claims across four assumption clusters (specifiability, stability, composability, supervisability). Mapped operational consequences through five C2 scenarios.

Result: Agentic properties weaken all eight identified assumptions across the four clusters. This erosion affects the argument connecting evidence to claims (not the claims or evidence themselves). The documented record does not support broad claims about system-level behavior, but narrower claims remain recoverable: bounded mission envelopes, trajectory-grounded correctness, executable runtime constraints, and characterized run-to-run variance. Part of the evidentiary burden shifts into deployment, making fielding a continuing act. Where evidence cannot be generated, residual uncertainty should be governed through defined expiry conditions and assigned ownership.

Conclusion: Current T&E methods cannot adequately assure agentic AI for military C2 under existing frameworks. While narrow claims may be achievable with mature methods, the shift of evidentiary burden into deployment requires new governance approaches including expiry conditions and ownership assignment for residual uncertainty. The gap between tested and fielded behavior cannot be bridged by traditional T&E alone.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems are being procured for military command and control (C2) under public commitments to rigorous testing and human oversight. Whether such commitments can be discharged depends on their supporting assurance case, which requires three elements: claims specifying the conditions for acceptability, evidence bearing on those claims, and an argument connecting the two. Through a structured review of 240 documented Testing and Evaluation (T&E) practices, spanning eight evaluation dimensions and three lifecycle stages, we identify eight assumptions that established methods make about their test article, grouped into four clusters: system specifiability, stability, composability, and supervisability. Agentic properties weaken all eight assumptions. This erosion affects the argument connecting evidence to claims, not the claims or evidence themselves. As a result, test results may satisfy process requirements, but they do not warrant the inference from tested to fielded behavior.
  We derive ten assurance claims for the first three assumption clusters and assess whether current and emerging methods can address each, mapping operational consequences through five C2 scenarios. Supervisability is identified but not assessed here, since evidencing it depends on system stability results and human factors T&E methods beyond the present scope. The documented record does not support broad claims about system-level behavior, but narrower claims remain recoverable in principle, contingent on mature methods: bounded mission envelopes, trajectory-grounded correctness, executable runtime constraints, and characterized run-to-run variance. Part of the evidentiary burden shifts into deployment, making the determination to field a continuing act. Where evidence cannot be generated, the residual uncertainty can be governed through defined expiry conditions and assigned ownership.

</details>


### [95] [Behavior Specification-Guided Program Synthesis for Binary Deobfuscation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20628)
*Kangchen Zhu,Shangwen Wang,Zhiliang Tian,Zhouyang Jia,Xiaoling Li,Jun Ma,Jie Yu,Xiaoguang Mao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: BinMirror is a binary deobfuscation approach that reformulates the problem as behavior-specification-guided program synthesis, using dynamic execution traces and interaction snapshots to synthesize high-quality source code that is validated against runtime observations from heavily obfuscated binaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing binary deobfuscation methods rely on decompilation and structural transformations, but compilation discards high-level semantics, resulting in low-quality recovered code with limited assurance of behavior preservation. Binary-level deobfuscation remains underexplored despite its practical importance when source code is unavailable.

Method: BinMirror treats dynamic execution traces and interaction snapshots as behavioral specifications, and synthesizes high-quality source code by validating it against runtime observations collected from heavily obfuscated binaries. This paradigm shifts from structural transformation to behavior-driven synthesis.

Result: Evaluated on 1.5 million synthetically obfuscated binaries, BinMirror achieves a unit-test Pass@1 of 74.5% under extreme obfuscation, significantly outperforming state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: BinMirror demonstrates the practical utility of behavior-driven synthesis for binary deobfuscation in restoring semantic clarity for real-world security analysis, offering a promising new paradigm that preserves runtime behavior through observable execution characteristics.

Abstract: Deobfuscation is critical to reverse engineering and security analysis because it restores the readability and analyzability of obfuscated code. However, existing research primarily focuses on source-code deobfuscation, while binary-level deobfuscation remains largely underexplored despite its practical importance when source code is unavailable. Existing binary deobfuscation methods typically decompile binaries into pseudocode and then apply structural transformations. However, because compilation discards high-level semantics such as precise type information and source-level structures, this decompilation-based paradigm often produces low-quality code and provides limited assurance that the recovered code preserves the runtime behavior of the original program. To address these limitations, we propose a paradigm shift from structural transformation to behavior-driven synthesis. Our core insight is that although obfuscation distorts a program's internal structure, semantics-preserving transformations must retain its observable execution behavior. Based on this insight, we introduce BinMirror, an approach that reformulates binary deobfuscation as a behavior-specification-guided program synthesis task. By treating dynamic execution traces and interaction snapshots as behavioral specifications, BinMirror synthesizes high-quality source code and validates it against runtime observations collected from heavily obfuscated binaries. Extensive evaluations on 1.5 million synthetically obfuscated binaries show that BinMirror significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving a unit-test Pass@1 of 74.5% under extreme obfuscation. These results demonstrate the practical utility of BinMirror in restoring semantic clarity for real-world security analysis.

</details>


### [96] [Toward Understanding Operating System Defects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20643)
*Hongyao Zuo,Jiali Li,Jiajun Jiang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents the largest-scale study analyzing 1,500 OS defects from Android, Linux, and HarmonyOS across multiple classification dimensions (OS layer, functions, trigger mechanisms, severity, repair code elements) to provide actionable implications for OS defect detection and debugging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Operating systems underpin all software systems, and OS defects can cause severe consequences affecting billions of users. Understanding OS defect characteristics is fundamental for designing effective defect detection and debugging approaches to ensure OS quality.

Method: Large-scale empirical study of 1,500 defects from three representative OSes (Android, Linux, HarmonyOS) covering mobile and desktop environments. Analyzed defects across multiple dimensions: OS layer distribution, affected functions, trigger mechanisms, severity levels, and code elements involved in repair. Performed joint analysis between dimensions and cross-OS similarity comparisons.

Result: The study identifies several major findings contributing to comprehensive understanding of OS defects across systems, though specific findings are not detailed in the abstract. Provides a series of actionable implications for better OS defect detection and debugging.

Conclusion: This largest-of-its-kind study offers guidelines for future research aimed at improving OS quality assurance by providing empirical insights into OS defect characteristics across different operating systems.

Abstract: Operating systems (OS) serve as the foundation for all other software systems, and thus defects in OSes can lead to severe conquences, such as system crashes and data corruption, affecting billions of users. This broad impact underscores the necessity and importance of ensuring OS quality. Understanding the characteristics of OS defects is a fundamental step in this quality assurance task, as it facilitates the design of effective defect detection and debugging approaches. In this work, we conduct a large-scale study of 1,500 defects from three distinct and representative operating systems (Android, Linux, and HarmonyOS) spanning both mobile and desktop environments. To the best of our knowledge, this is the largest study of its kind in this domain. By analyzing the distribution of OS defects across multiple classification dimensions, including the OS layer where defects occur, the functions they affect, how they are triggered, their severity, and the code elements involved in their repair, as well as performing joint analysis between dimensions and cross-OS similarity comparisons, we summarize several major findings that contribute to a comprehensive understanding of OS defects across systems. Based on these findings, we provide a series of actionable implications for better OS defect detection and debugging, offering guidelines for future research aimed at improving OS quality assurance.

</details>


### [97] [Temporal Validity on Real Software Histories: Eliminating Stale-Fact Errors in Code-Assistant Memory over GitHub Fixes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20685)
*Neeraj Yadav*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper addresses a fundamental limitation in Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems: inability to distinguish between stale (superseded) and current information when facts change during a coding session. The authors validate a deterministic supersession memory approach on real GitHub issues, achieving 0.91 accuracy vs RAG's 0.57-0.59, while eliminating superseded value retrieval (from 36-38% to ~0%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems retrieve information based on semantic similarity but have no temporal model. When facts change (e.g., function renames, endpoint moves, dependency updates), RAG retrieves both old and new versions with similar similarity scores, 无法区分哪个是当前有效的，导致模型提供过时信息。这是一个重要的 practical problem，尤其是在软件开发中需要准确、最新的信息。

Method: The authors extracted 130 clean atomic state transitions from 707 real GitHub issues (SWE-bench Lite + Verified), where a fix changes one identifiable value from pre-fix to post-fix form. They compared MemStrata (their approach with deterministic supersession memory using subject-relation-object triples) against standard RAG and an LLM reranker baseline. Evaluation measured answer accuracy and frequency of serving superseded values.

Result: MemStrata achieves 0.91 answer accuracy vs RAG's 0.57-0.59. RAG serves the superseded value 36-38% of the time (LLM reranker doesn't improve this), while MemStrata reduces this to ~0%. MemStrata operates at RAG retrieval latency (~2.1s) compared to ~18s for the reranker. The study also discovered and fixed a real product bug (case/punctuation-insensitive value comparison) during the research.

Conclusion: The deterministic supersession memory mechanism effectively solves the temporal grounding problem for RAG in software engineering contexts, achieving high accuracy while maintaining low latency. However, the approach only applies to ~18% of real fixes (clean atomic transitions), and extracting such transitions from the remaining 82% of fixes remains an orthogonal problem for future work.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has no model of time: when a fact changes across a coding session - a function is renamed, an endpoint moves, a dependency is bumped - RAG retrieves both the old and new value with near-identical similarity and cannot tell which is current, so it serves the superseded value. Paper 1 showed, on synthetic single-value benchmarks, that a deterministic (subject, relation, object) supersession memory eliminates this failure. Here we validate it end-to-end on real software history. From 707 real GitHub issues (SWE-bench Lite + Verified) we extract 130 clean atomic state transitions, a fix that changes one identifiable value from a pre-fix to a post-fix form, and render each marker-free (the stale and current statements differ only in the value). On this set, MemStrata reaches 0.91 answer accuracy versus RAG's 0.57-0.59; and, the structural result, when forced to answer RAG serves the superseded value 36-38% of the time (an LLM reranker does not help) while MemStrata drives this to ~0, at RAG retrieval latency (~2.1 s vs ~18 s for the reranker). We are explicit about scope: only ~18% of real fixes are clean atomic transitions; Paper 2 isolates the memory mechanism on that class, and extraction coverage of the remaining fixes is the orthogonal problem we defer to follow-on work. A real product bug surfaced and was fixed during the study (a case/punctuation-insensitive value comparison), with the moat property (deterministic-supersession accuracy on clean code mutations) preserved and verified.

</details>


### [98] [An Extensive Empirical Study on Code Translation Technique](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20776)
*Ruihang Fan,Jiajun Jiang,Xinpeng Wang,Jiateng Fu,Fengjie Li,Jiasi Shen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a large-scale empirical study comparing learning-based and LLM-based code translation techniques across multiple programming languages, evaluating method-level and class-level translations. The study finds that LLM-based methods generally outperform learning-based methods, though similarity metrics don't reliably predict functional correctness. Translation direction significantly impacts performance, and class-level translation is substantially harder than method-level translation due to complexity of preserving global semantics and cross-method dependencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap in understanding the relative strengths and limitations of learning-based versus LLM-based code translation techniques, which are increasingly important for software evolution.

Method: The authors conducted a large-scale empirical study evaluating three categories of code translation methods: learning-based methods, LLM-based methods, and general-purpose LLMs. They tested these on multilingual method-level and class-level benchmarks across multiple programming languages, considering metrics including executable correctness, code similarity, translation direction, translation granularity, and failure patterns.

Result: LLMs and LLM-based methods generally outperform learning-based methods in method-level correctness. Code similarity metrics do not reliably reflect functional correctness. Translation direction substantially affects performance, especially when translating between languages with different type-system characteristics. Class-level translation is considerably more difficult than method-level translation due to the need to preserve global semantics, interfaces, member relationships, and cross-method dependencies. Static semantic errors and logical errors are the primary challenges in existing code translation systems.

Conclusion: The findings provide empirical evidence and practical guidance for developing more robust, type-aware, structure-aware, and context-aware code translation techniques. Future work should focus on addressing static semantic errors and logical errors, and developing better approaches for class-level translation that can handle global semantics and cross-method dependencies.

Abstract: Automated code translation is increasingly important for software evolution, yet the relative strengths and limitations of learning-based and large language model (LLM)-based techniques remain insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we conduct a large-scale empirical study comparing representative code translation techniques across methodological paradigms and translation granularities. We evaluate learning-based methods, LLM-based methods, and general-purpose LLMs on multilingual method-level and class-level benchmarks involving multiple programming languages. Our analysis considers executable correctness, code similarity, translation direction, translation granularity, and failure patterns. The results show that LLMs and LLM-based methods generally outperform learning-based methods in method-level correctness, although similarity metrics alone do not reliably reflect functional correctness. Translation direction substantially affects performance, particularly when translating between languages with different type-system characteristics. Class-level translation remains considerably more difficult than method-level translation because it requires preserving global semantics, interfaces, member relationships, and cross-method dependencies. Our error analysis further shows that static semantic errors and logical errors are the primary challenges in existing code translation systems. These findings provide empirical evidence and practical guidance for developing more robust, type-aware, structure-aware, and context-aware code translation techniques.

</details>


### [99] [BC-Bench: Evaluating Agentic Engineering in a Domain-Specific Language for ERP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20851)
*Haoran Sun,Klaus Marius Hansen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: BC-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating agentic engineering systems on AL, the domain-specific language for Microsoft Dynamics 365 Business Central. It contains 101 tasks from production repositories and evaluates models on bug fixing, test generation, and multimodal problem statements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic engineering systems perform well on general benchmarks but their effectiveness in enterprise resource planning (ERP) domain-specific languages remains underexplored. The AL ecosystem has unique constraints including limited public resources and complex environment provisioning.

Method: The benchmark adapts the SWE-Bench methodology to AL's specific constraints. It comprises 101 manually curated tasks from two Microsoft production repositories. Multiple frontier models are evaluated across two agent harnesses using multi-run metrics to account for nondeterminism. The benchmark evaluates functional code generation, test generation, and supports multimodal problem statements.

Result: In the Bug Fixing category, between-model differences in resolution rate are larger than differences between the two evaluated agent harnesses. Improvements reported on general-purpose benchmarks do not consistently transfer to AL, indicating that domain-specific evaluation is necessary.

Conclusion: Domain-specific evaluation is crucial as general-purpose benchmark improvements do not reliably transfer to ERP DSLs like AL. The results highlight the need for specialized benchmarks like BC-Bench for evaluating agentic systems in enterprise software contexts.

Abstract: Agentic engineering systems have shown strong performance on general-purpose benchmarks, yet their effectiveness in enterprise resource planning (ERP) domain-specific languages (DSLs) remains underexplored. We introduce BC-Bench, a benchmark designed to evaluate agentic engineering on real-world tasks in AL, the DSL for Microsoft Dynamics 365 Business Central. BC-Bench comprises 101 manually curated tasks extracted from two Microsoft-owned production repositories, reflecting authentic ERP development workflows. Adapting the SWE-Bench methodology, we address the unique constraints of the AL ecosystem---including limited public resources and complex environment provisioning. Beyond generating functional code, BC-Bench evaluates test generation and supports multimodal problem statements where visual context is commonly present. We evaluate multiple frontier models across two agent harnesses, utilizing multi-run metrics to account for nondeterminism. In the Bug Fixing category, under our evaluated settings, between-model differences in resolution rate are larger than differences between the two evaluated agent harnesses, and improvements reported on general-purpose benchmarks do not consistently transfer to AL. These results highlight the need for domain-specific evaluation.

</details>


### [100] [Beyond the Traceback: Using LLMs for Adaptive Explanations of Programming Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20896)
*Alexandru-Radu Moraru,Shreyan Biswas,Ujwal Gadiraju*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Study evaluating whether LLM-generated, skill-targeted Python error messages improve debugging performance. N=103 participants were categorized by skill level and tested with standard interpreter messages versus two LLM-generated styles (pragmatic/action-oriented and contingent/scaffolded). Results showed LLM messages improved subjective evaluations (readability, cognitive load) but did not significantly improve objective debugging metrics (fix rate, attempts, time-to-fix), revealing a human-AI complementarity gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Programming error messages are critical but difficult for novices to interpret. While LLMs can rewrite these errors into clearer explanations, it was unclear whether increased readability improves actual debugging performance or how explanation styles should align with programmer skill.

Method: Multi-stage crowdsourced study (N=103) using a custom proficiency assessment to categorize participants by skill level. Tested three message types: standard interpreter messages, pragmatic (action-oriented) LLM messages, and contingent (scaffolded explanations) LLM messages. Measured both objective metrics (fix rate, attempts, time-to-fix) and subjective perceptions (readability, cognitive load, tone).

Result: LLM-rewritten messages significantly improved subjective evaluations - pragmatic messages rated as clearer and less cognitively demanding. However, these perceived gains did NOT translate into statistically significant improvements in objective debugging performance.

Conclusion: Reveals a critical human-AI complementarity gap: explanations that feel better to users do not necessarily make them more effective debuggers. Future AI feedback systems should pivot from static skill-targeted rewriting toward dynamic adjustments based on real-time repair trajectory rather than just perceived readability improvements.

Abstract: Programming error messages are critical for software development, yet they remain difficult for novice programmers to interpret. While Large Language Models (LLMs) can rewrite these errors into clearer explanations, it remains unclear whether increased readability improves objective debugging performance or how explanation styles should align with programmer skill. We present a multi-stage crowdsourced study N=103 evaluating skill-targeted, LLM-generated Python error messages. Using a custom proficiency assessment, we categorized participants by skill level and tested standard interpreter messages against two LLM-generated styles: pragmatic (action-oriented) and contingent (scaffolded explanations). We measured both objective debugging metrics (fix rate, attempts, time-to-fix) and subjective perceptions (readability, cognitive load, tone). Our results show that while LLM-rewritten messages significantly improved subjective evaluations, with pragmatic messages rated as clearer and less cognitively demanding, these perceived gains did not translate into statistically significant improvements in objective debugging performance. This highlights a critical human-AI complementarity gap: explanations that feel better to users do not necessarily make them more effective debuggers. We discuss design implications for adaptive AI feedback systems, arguing that future tools should pivot from static skill-targeted rewriting toward dynamic adjustments based on a user's real-time repair trajectory.

</details>


### [101] [Generation of Web Apps with Agentic IDEs: An Empirical Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20903)
*Manuel Marceca,Maria Teresa Rossi,Leonardo Mariani*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper evaluates three popular agentic IDEs (Copilot, Cursor, and Windsurf) by testing their ability to generate five full-stack web applications from scratch. The study finds that these tools excel at common patterns like CRUD and authentication but struggle with complex distributed architectures like task queues. While they cannot replace developers, they shift the development role toward orchestrating LLM agents through natural language.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The evaluation of agentic IDEs in end-to-end development tasks involving complete application generation remains limited. Despite being significant innovations in software engineering, there is a lack of rigorous comparative analysis of these tools in real-world full-stack development scenarios.

Method: The authors conducted a rigorous comparative analysis of three popular agentic IDEs (Copilot, Cursor, and Windsurf) by prompting each to generate five full-stack Web applications from scratch, testing their capabilities across different architectural patterns.

Result: Agentic IDEs show high maturity in generating established patterns such as CRUD operations and authentication features. However, they produce significantly more errors when generating less common distributed architectures like task queue systems. Overall differences between the three IDEs are narrow, with each showing its own peculiarities.

Conclusion: Agentic IDEs cannot replace developers but shift their role toward building software by orchestrating LLM-based agents through natural-language instructions and iterative refinement. These tools represent a paradigm shift in software development, moving developers from direct coding to agent orchestration.

Abstract: Agentic IDEs are among the most significant innovations in software engineering, aiming to accelerate application development through LLM-based agents that can assist developers during development. However, their evaluation in end-to-end development tasks involving the generation of complete applications remains limited. To fill this gap, we propose a rigorous comparative analysis of three popular agentic IDEs (Copilot, Cursor, and Windsurf) in the generation of five full-stack Web applications from scratch.
  Results show high maturity in the generation of established patterns, such as CRUD operations and authentication features. In contrast, the generation of less common distributed architectures, such as a task queue architecture, produces significantly more errors. Overall, results show that Agentic IDEs cannot replace developers but shift their role toward building software by orchestrating LLM-based agents through natural-language instructions and iterative refinement. Yet, each agentic IDE shows its peculiarities, although differences are narrow.

</details>


### [102] [Spike-Killer: Evidence-Gated LLM Assistance for Safe Performance Diagnosis on a Real Windows Workstation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21069)
*Baocheng Zeng,Jinhao Yang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents Spike-Killer, a human-in-the-loop workflow for LLM-assisted system diagnostics on a real Windows workstation, using Counter-Strike 2 as a test case. The approach treats each diagnostic action as an evidence-gated transaction with built-in safeguards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-assisted agents can perform diagnostic tasks autonomously but pose operational risks due to imprecise actions or intrusive data collection. The authors seek to create a trustworthy, auditable diagnostic workflow with human oversight.

Method: A same-day study using a Windows workstation running Counter-Strike 2. The workflow implements evidence-gated transactions: recording target states, classifying risk, preserving snapshots, verifying postconditions, and retaining failed measurements. Methods include state snapshots, microbenchmarks, repeatability probes, live telemetry, registry repair, and Windows Performance Recorder tracing.

Result: Obtained two valid GPU traces (90.69s and 85.85s) from cs2.exe with DxgKrnl Present metadata and zero lost ETW buffers. However, no frame intervals, P99 estimates, or intervention effects were reported - the study validates trace integrity only.

Conclusion: The contribution is a pattern for auditable, human-in-the-loop agent assistance on real workstations, with explicit stop conditions when evidence is insufficient. The paper does not claim to solve the frame-time problem but demonstrates a methodology for trustworthy diagnostic workflows.

Abstract: LLM-assisted agents can synthesize system evidence, propose configuration changes, and automate diagnostic tasks, but their flexibility makes an imprecise action or an intrusive collector an operational risk. We present Spike-Killer, a human-approved workflow for diagnosing frame-time complaints on one real Windows workstation. The workflow treats each action as an evidence-gated transaction: it records the exact target state, classifies risk, preserves a snapshot, verifies a postcondition, and retains failed measurements as first-class evidence.
  This experience paper reports a completed same-day study with Counter-Strike 2 as a demanding target application. The evidence bundle contains preserved state snapshots, exploratory microbenchmarks, a ten-run same-state repeatability probe, live telemetry, a repaired over-broad registry action, incompatible presentation-capture attempts, an invalid local replay, and a system-level tracing replacement. Windows Performance Recorder produced two CS2 local-Bot GPU traces of 90.69 and 85.85 seconds; both were attributed to cs2.exe, exposed DxgKrnl Present metadata, and had zero lost ETW buffers or events. These results qualify trace integrity, not performance: the study reports no frame intervals, P99 estimate, or intervention effect. The contribution is an auditable, human-in-the-loop pattern for trustworthy agent assistance on a real workstation, including explicit stop conditions when evidence is insufficient.

</details>


### [103] [Trustworthy RAG: An Evaluation Agent for Detecting Misinformation and Knowledge Poisoning in Generative AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21095)
*Balkrishna Giri,Md Toufique Hasan,Jussi Rasku,Muhammad Waseem,Pekka Abrahamsson*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces an Evaluation Agent middleware for RAG systems that addresses knowledge poisoning attacks. The agent combines NLI-based factual verification with a five-signal poison detector and a Trust Index formula (T = 0.4F + 0.35C + 0.25(1-P)) with non-linear dampener. It achieves 91% accuracy and 100% precision on TruthfulQA with Llama 3.3 70B, 100% recall on instruction injection, and F1 92% in a secure-coding use case, though in-place edits like entity swaps remain challenging to detect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems typically trust retrieved documents without verification, creating a Security-Reliability Gap where high semantic relevance doesn't guarantee factual truth. Attackers exploit this through knowledge poisoning by inserting malicious documents to cause targeted misinformation.

Method: The proposed Evaluation Agent combines: (1) Natural Language Inference (NLI) factual verification, (2) a five-signal poison detector with relevance-weighted aggregation, and (3) a Trust Index formula T = 0.4F + 0.35C + 0.25(1-P) with a non-linear dampener for high-contamination contexts.

Result: On TruthfulQA with Llama 3.3 70B: 91% accuracy, 100% precision, 100% recall on instruction injection. ROC-AUC of 0.73-0.81 across three LLMs. In secure-coding assistant use case: F1 92% for blocking instruction injection. Limitations: in-place edits (entity swaps) and contradiction/semantic weakening remain hard to detect. Cross-dataset generalization requires domain-specific calibration.

Conclusion: The Evaluation Agent effectively detects poisoned context before generation (not LLM adoption of misinformation). Generation style matters more than model size, and per-LLM threshold calibration restores competitive accuracy. The approach is released open-source at GitHub.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) grounds Large Language Model (LLM) outputs in external knowledge, but RAG systems usually trust whatever they retrieve, creating a Security-Reliability Gap: high semantic relevance does not guarantee factual truth. Adversaries exploit this through knowledge poisoning, inserting malicious documents to cause targeted misinformation. We propose an Evaluation Agent, middleware that combines Natural Language Inference (NLI) factual verification, a five-signal poison detector with relevance-weighted aggregation, and a Trust Index T = 0.4 F + 0.35 C + 0.25 (1 - P ) with a non-linear dampener for high-contamination contexts. On TruthfulQA with Llama 3.3 70B, the agent reaches 91% accuracy and 100% precision, with 100% recall on instruction injection, while in-place edits, such as entity swaps, remain hard to detect. Across three LLMs the Trust Index stays discriminative, with a Receiver Operating Characteristic Area Under the Curve (ROC-AUC) of 0.73 to 0.81; generation style matters more than model size, and per-LLM threshold calibration restores baseline competitive accuracy, whereas a weaker FEVER result shows that cross-dataset generalization requires domain-specific calibration. In a software-engineering use case, a secure-coding assistant over guidance from the Open Worldwide Application Security Project (OWASP) Top 10 and the Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE), the agent reliably blocks instruction injection of unsafe advice (F1 92%), while contradiction and subtle semantic weakening remain hard. Throughout, the agent measures detection of poisoned context before generation, not whether the LLM adopts the injected misinformation. We release the proposed approach, attack generator, and experimental artifacts at the link: https://github.com/GPT-Laboratory/TrustworthyRAG.

</details>


### [104] [On the Time and Frequency Domain Representations of Signals for CPS Specification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21167)
*Claudio Mandrioli,Drishti Yadav,Domenico Bianculli*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes Signal-Spectrum Temporal Logic (S2TL), a novel specification language for Cyber-Physical Systems that uses time-frequency representations to specify and monitor CPS requirements, combining benefits of both time and frequency domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current specification languages for CPS verification use time-domain representation, which is unsuitable for describing signal shapes and dynamic behaviors. Frequency-domain is used in control/robotics but has limitations. Time-frequency representations could combine both domains' capabilities.

Method: 1) Analyzed existing taxonomies of CPS requirements to identify which classes benefit from time-frequency representations. 2) Derived desiderata for a specification language using time-frequency representations. 3) Proposed S2TL language enabling assertions over frequency intervals and relations between frequency components. 4) Operationalized S2TL semantics for monitoring CPS traces and implemented a monitor. 5) Defined specification templates for identified requirement classes. 6) Compared time- and time-frequency-domain formulations in terms of applicability, expression fidelity, and noise tolerance.

Result: Time-domain specifications are only applicable to input traces with step-like changes or constant interpolation. Time-frequency specifications extend evaluation to traces generated through linear interpolation, improve tolerance to offset and high-frequency noise, while achieving comparable fidelity to intended system properties.

Conclusion: Time-frequency representations (S2TL) offer significant advantages over pure time-domain approaches for CPS specification and monitoring, enabling more flexible interpolation methods and better noise tolerance while maintaining fidelity.

Abstract: Specification languages are instrumental to the Verification \& Validation of Cyber-Physical Systems (CPSs). Most state-of-the-art specification languages use the time-domain representation of signals, which is not always suitable for describing signal shapes and dynamic behaviours. Instead, fields like control and robotics use the frequency-domain representation to characterise these behaviours. Time-frequency representations combine the capabilities of both domains. We investigate the use of time-frequency representations to specify CPS requirements. We analyse existing taxonomies of CPS requirements to identify which requirement classes can benefit from time-frequency representations. We derive the desiderata for a specification language that uses time-frequency representations and propose Signal-Spectrum Temporal Logic (S2TL), a language enabling assertions over frequency intervals and relations between frequency components. We operationalise the S2TL semantics for monitoring CPS traces, and implement a monitor. We define specification templates for the identified requirement classes and compare time- and time-frequency-domain formulations in terms of applicability, expression fidelity, and noise tolerance of monitoring. We observe that, while time-domain specifications are applicable only to input traces containing step-like changes or using constant interpolation, time-frequency specifications extend their evaluation to traces generated through linear interpolation, and improve tolerance to offset and high-frequency noise, while achieving comparable fidelity to the intended system properties.

</details>


### [105] [Specification Portability Across LLM Development Agents: Cross-Agent Compatibility in Specification-Driven Software Migration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21208)
*Oleg Grynets,Oleksii Ilchuk,Dariia Zatulna,Vasyl Lyashkevych*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates cross-agent specification portability in Oracle-to-PostgreSQL migration, evaluating how specifications generated by one AI agent perform when consumed by other agents. The study tested multiple AI agents (Kiro, Gemini, Copilot, Claude Code, Cursor) on 1,802 Oracle-to-PostgreSQL migration tasks, finding that specifications are not agent-neutral and suffer significant degradation when transferred between agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses a critical gap in multi-agent software engineering: whether specifications (intermediate artifacts in software transformation) can be reliably transferred between different AI agents. As heterogeneous SDD (Specification-Driven Development) workflows emerge, understanding specification portability becomes essential for effective agent collaboration.

Method: Two experimental stages were conducted: (1) A specification-first migration pipeline evaluated on 1,006 PL/SQL files (623 successfully regenerated, 380 executed successfully in PostgreSQL 16); (2) Cross-agent experiments on 1,802 Oracle scripts with corresponding PostgreSQL implementations using Amazon Kiro, Google Gemini, GitHub Copilot, Claude Code, and Cursor. Specifications were assessed using Token F1, exact match, SQL syntax validity, AST exact match, AST mean similarity, and immediate runnability metrics.

Result: Key findings include: (1) Specification size alone does not predict implementation quality; (2) Cross-agent transfer produces substantial agent-dependent degradation; (3) The strongest degradation occurred when Gemini consumed Kiro-origin specifications (Token F1: 0.035, SQL syntax validity: 2.33%, AST mean similarity: 0.015); (4) Rewriting substantially improved Gemini's performance; (5) Compression did not provide universal benefit; (6) Retrieval-augmented ingestion was the only common strategy on per-agent Pareto frontiers for both Gemini and Copilot.

Conclusion: Specifications in heterogeneous SDD workflows should not automatically be treated as agent-neutral artifacts. The findings motivate explicit consideration of specification portability, agent-specific interpretation, and retrieval-based access in multi-agent software engineering. Practitioners should avoid naive specification transfer between agents and instead implement retrieval-based or agent-specific adaptation strategies.

Abstract: This paper investigates cross-agent specification portability using Oracle-to-PostgreSQL migration as a controlled software transformation task. The study combines two experimental stages. First, a specification-first migration pipeline was evaluated on 1,006 PL/SQL files, of which 623 were successfully regenerated and 380 generated scripts executed successfully in PostgreSQL 16. Second, cross-agent experiments were conducted on a dataset of 1,802 Oracle scripts with corresponding PostgreSQL implementations using Amazon Kiro, Google Gemini, and GitHub Copilot, with Claude Code and Cursor included in the initial single-agent evaluation. Native and foreign specifications were assessed using Token F1, exact match, SQL syntax validity, AST exact match, AST mean similarity, and immediate runnability. The results show that specification size alone does not predict implementation quality and that cross-agent transfer can produce substantial agent-dependent degradation. The strongest replicated case occurred when Gemini directly consumed a Kiro-origin specification, producing a Token F1 of 0.035, SQL syntax validity of 2.33%, and AST mean similarity of 0.015. Rewriting substantially improved Gemini in the tested configuration, compression did not provide a universal benefit, and retrieval-augmented ingestion was the only common strategy represented on the per-agent Pareto frontiers of both Gemini and Copilot. The findings suggest that specifications in heterogeneous SDD workflows should not automatically be treated as agent-neutral artifacts and motivate explicit consideration of specification portability, agent-specific interpretation, and retrieval-based access in multi-agent software engineering.

</details>


### [106] [The Substitution Escrow Threshold: When "Compatible With" Becomes Safe Enough to Buy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21221)
*Amadeus Brandes*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Substitution Escrow Threshold, a five-condition framework to evaluate whether compatibility claims in enterprise infrastructure genuinely reduce institutional risk or merely reduce first-integration cost. The framework is applied to five cases (OCI, Kubernetes, OpenTelemetry, S3, PostgreSQL) to produce actionable diagnostics for enterprise architects, platform engineers, procurement teams, and investors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise infrastructure buyers routinely evaluate compatibility claims (e.g., "S3-compatible," "PostgreSQL-compatible") as proxies for future substitution options. However, most compatibility claims do not escrow the substitution path they imply, creating a gap between promised and actual substitutability.

Method: The paper introduces a five-condition framework: (1) boundary closure, (2) executable conformance, (3) custody independence, (4) state and operations reversibility, and (5) extension quarantine. These conditions are applied to five infrastructure cases: OCI, Kubernetes, OpenTelemetry, S3, and PostgreSQL.

Result: The framework produces five distinct outcome cells from the five infrastructure cases, demonstrating how different compatibility claims map to different levels of genuine substitution risk reduction versus mere integration cost reduction.

Conclusion: The Substitution Escrow Threshold framework provides actionable diagnostics for enterprise architects, platform engineers, procurement teams, and investors evaluating compatibility-dependent infrastructure decisions. The paper identifies AI infrastructure as the framework's most urgent next application area.

Abstract: Enterprise infrastructure buyers routinely evaluate compatibility claims--"S3-compatible," "PostgreSQL-compatible," "OpenAI compatible"--as proxies for future substitution options. Yet most compatibility claims do not escrow the substitution path they imply. This paper introduces the Substitution Escrow Threshold, a five-condition framework that determines when a compatibility claim genuinely reduces institutional risk versus merely reducing first-integration cost. The five conditions--boundary closure, executable conformance, custody independence, state and operations reversibility, and extension quarantine--are applied to five infrastructure cases (OCI, Kubernetes, OpenTelemetry, S3, PostgreSQL) that populate five distinct outcome cells. The framework produces actionable diagnostics for enterprise architects, platform engineers, procurement teams, and investors evaluating compatibility-dependent infrastructure decisions, and identifies AI infrastructure as the framework's most urgent next application.

</details>


### [107] [Human-AI Collaboration in Requirements Engineering: Evidence of the Negative Effect of LLMs on Requirements Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21298)
*Giovanna Broccia,Julian Frattini,Chetan Arora,Maurice H. ter Beek,Alessandro Fantechi,Andreas Vogelsang,Alessio Ferrari*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates the impact of LLM support on human-performed requirements inspection (RI) through a controlled crossover experiment with 34 participants. Results show LLM support negatively affects smell detection accuracy but has no significant effect on classification or task duration. Learning effects are present, and LLM support from the beginning may slow skill acquisition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While requirements inspection (RI) is established for detecting defects early in software development, and LLMs have shown potential for supporting requirements engineering tasks, there is scarce empirical evidence on how LLMs affect human-performed RI. This study addresses this gap by examining the actual impact of LLM assistance on inspection effectiveness and efficiency.

Method: A controlled crossover design experiment with 34 participants who inspected textual specifications both with and without LLM support. Participants identified and classified requirements smells (as nocuous vs innocuous) while recording inspection time. Analysis used Bayesian regression models accounting for crossover design validity threats, covariates, and mediators.

Result: LLM support negatively affects smell detection accuracy. No significant effect on smell classification or task duration. A learning effect exists across experimental periods but is reduced when RI is first performed with LLM support.

Conclusion: LLM support does not necessarily improve performance and may hinder it for novice inspectors. Learning RI with LLM support from the beginning may slow down skill acquisition, implying potential threats for LLM-supported learning in requirements engineering.

Abstract: Background. Requirements inspection (RI) is a well-established practice for detecting potential defects in requirements artifacts early in the software lifecycle. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have stimulated interest in their potential to support requirements engineering (RE) tasks. However, empirical evidence on the effects of LLMs when used as collaborative assistants in human-performed RI remains scarce. Aims. We aim to investigate the impact of LLM support on human-performed RI, considering inspection effectiveness in terms of smell identification and severity classification (i.e., nocuous vs innocuous), as well as inspection duration. Method. We conducted a controlled crossover design experiment with 34 participants, who inspected textual specifications with and without LLM support, identifying and classifying requirements smells while recording inspection time. We analyzed the data using one Bayesian regression model per outcome variable, accounting for validity threats induced by the crossover design as well as covariates and mediators. Results. Results show that LLM support negatively affects smell detection accuracy but has no significant effect on smell classification or task duration. A learning effect is present across experimental periods, but reduced when RI is first performed with LLM support. Conclusions. Our findings provide empirical evidence that LLM support does not necessarily improve performance and may, instead, hinder it for novice inspectors. Moreover, the results suggest that learning RI with LLM-support from the beginning may slow down the skill acquisition process, implying threats for LLM-supported learning.

</details>


### [108] [Beyond Fault Localization: A Trajectory-Level Study of LLM Agents for Microservice Root Cause Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21310)
*Qisheng Lu,Aoyang Fang,Junjielong Xu,Jin'ao Shang,Songhan Zhang,Yifan Yang,Xiaochuan Yan,Pinjia He*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper addresses a critical gap in evaluating automated Root Cause Analysis (RCA) for microservices: current metrics only measure endpoint correctness (whether the correct service is identified) but ignore the diagnostic process itself. The authors propose a trajectory-level evaluation framework that assesses how agents investigate faults, use telemetry, and reconstruct fault propagation paths. They also introduce DiagGuard, a two-stage defense system that improves diagnostic accuracy by grounding observations before localization and verifying diagnoses against evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RCA evaluations focus solely on final answer correctness, which fails to reveal: (1) the evidentiary basis of a diagnosis, and (2) the fault-propagation route connecting the source to observed symptoms. On-call SREs need this diagnostic process information to judge whether action is warranted. The paper treats RCA as an observable diagnostic process rather than just a localization task.

Method: 1. Developed a trajectory-level framework that evaluates agent executions against manually curated service-level fault-propagation paths
2. Applied to a public microservice RCA benchmark, analyzing 3,500 diagnostic trajectories
3. Characterized agent investigation patterns and evidence usage
4. Operationalized findings as DiagGuard - a two-stage defense-in-depth architecture:
   - Grounding: surveys available observations before localization
   - Verification: audits diagnosis against available evidence
5. Tested in independent setting with different model, benchmark, and service topology

Result: 1. Disconnect between answer correctness and diagnostic quality: agents may localize fault source yet fail to reconstruct propagation
2. Successful investigations: stay on fault-impact surface, act on retrieved evidence, broaden query repertoire as search deepens
3. Failures arise from: (a) decisive evidence omitted, (b) retrieved evidence misinterpreted, (c) unsupported inference substituting for missing evidence
4. DiagGuard raised Acc@1 from 43.5% to 52.5% in independent evaluation

Conclusion: Trajectory-level evaluation exposes limitations hidden by final-answer metrics and provides actionable guidance for improving automated RCA. The disconnect between correctness and diagnostic quality suggests that evaluating only endpoint accuracy is insufficient. DiagGuard demonstrates that incorporating grounding and verification stages significantly improves diagnostic performance, suggesting that process-oriented evaluation and intervention can substantially enhance RCA systems.

Abstract: Existing evaluations of automated root cause analysis (RCA) for microservices assess diagnostic performance mainly by endpoint correctness: whether a method localizes the responsible service. This criterion enables comparison but does not reveal the evidentiary basis of a diagnosis or the fault-propagation route connecting the source to observed symptoms, both of which an on-call site reliability engineer needs to judge whether action is warranted. We therefore treat RCA as an observable diagnostic process. Our trajectory-level framework evaluates agent executions against manually curated service-level fault-propagation paths. Applied to a public microservice RCA benchmark, it analyzes 3,500 diagnostic trajectories, characterizing where agents investigate and how they use retrieved telemetry. We find a disconnect between answer correctness and diagnostic quality: an agent may localize the fault source yet fail to reconstruct its propagation. Successful investigations stay on the fault-impact surface, act on retrieved evidence, and broaden their query repertoire as the search deepens. Failures arise when decisive evidence is omitted, retrieved evidence is misinterpreted, or unsupported inference substitutes for missing evidence. We operationalize this taxonomy as DiagGuard, a two-stage defense-in-depth architecture in which grounding surveys available observations before localization and verification audits the diagnosis against them. In an independent setting with a different model, benchmark, and service topology, DiagGuard raises Acc@1 from 43.5% to 52.5%. These results show that trajectory-level evaluation exposes limitations hidden by final-answer metrics and provides actionable guidance for improving automated RCA.

</details>


### [109] [AI-to-AI Code Reviews of GitHub Pull Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21311)
*Niruthiha Selvanayagam,Taher A. Ghaleb*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates AI-to-AI code review, where AI agents serve as both PR authors and reviewers. Using the CodAGE dataset, researchers analyzed 248,641 AI-attributed PRs receiving AI reviews, finding cross-product review in ~1.6% of cases but growing rapidly. Reviewer output varies significantly across author-reviewer configurations, with CodeRabbit labeling 35% of comments on Claude Code PRs as refactors vs 10.5% on Copilot PRs. Same-product reviews show 58-65% higher comment volume for most dual-role reviewers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid adoption of AI coding agents in software development has created a closed-loop system where AI agents review AI-authored code contributions. This phenomenon raises important questions about review quality, consistency, and potential systematic biases in AI-to-AI interactions. However, the scale and characteristics of this phenomenon had not been systematically studied, motivating the construction of a large-scale dataset to analyze AI-to-AI code review patterns.

Method: The researchers linked AI-attributed PRs with AI-attributed review events from CodAGE, a public dataset of coding-agent-generated GitHub events. They categorized reviews as cross-product (different authoring and reviewing agents) or same-product (same ecosystem), and analyzed reviewer output patterns, comment volumes, and latency across different author-reviewer configurations.

Result: The dataset contains 248,641 unique AI-attributed PRs with AI reviews: 45,269 cross-product, 208,145 same-product, with 4,773 receiving both. Cross-product review represents ~1.6% of agent-authored PRs but grew >100x from 2025-Q1 to Q3. CodeRabbit showed markedly different comment patterns: 35% refactor comments on Claude Code PRs vs 10.5% on Copilot PRs. For dual-role reviewers, mean comments per PR were 58-65% higher in same-product configurations, though concentrated in upper tail. Median latency was 1.2 min for cross-product vs 4.7 min for same-product pairs.

Conclusion: Closed-loop AI-to-AI review is increasing but remains a minority of identified agent activity. Review output varies substantially across authoring-agent groups and product configurations, which may reflect either reviewer behavior differences or characteristics of the PRs being reviewed. This emerging phenomenon warrants continued monitoring as AI coding agents become more prevalent in development workflows.

Abstract: AI coding agents are increasingly integrated into software development workflows, operating on both sides of the pull-request (PR) process: AI authoring agents create or modify PRs, while AI reviewers evaluate them. This creates a closed loop in which one AI coding agent reviews a contribution attributed to another. We construct a large-scale dataset of AI-to-AI code review by linking AI-attributed PRs with AI-attributed review events from CodAGE, a public dataset of coding-agent-generated GitHub events. Our dataset contains 248,641 unique AI-attributed PRs that received at least one AI-attributed review. Of these, 45,269 received cross-product review and 208,145 received same-product review; 4,773 PRs received both. Cross-product AI-to-AI review occurred in approximately 1.6% of identified agent-authored PRs but was substantial in absolute terms, and its volume increased by more than two orders of magnitude from 2025-Q1 to 2025-Q3. Reviewer output varied across author-reviewer configurations. CodeRabbit labeled 35.0% of its comments on Claude Code-authored PRs as refactor comments, compared with 10.5% on Copilot-authored PRs, although this difference may reflect characteristics of the PRs rather than the reviewer. For three of four dual-role reviewers, mean comments per PR were 58-65% higher in the same-product group, although effect sizes were small or negligible and the difference was concentrated in the upper tail. Among pairs with complete, nonnegative timestamps, the observed median latency was 1.2 minutes for cross-product pairs and 4.7 minutes for same-product pairs; differential timestamp availability and reviewer composition limit this comparison. Overall, closed-loop AI-to-AI review is increasing but remains a minority of identified agent activity, with review output varying across authoring-agent groups and product configurations.

</details>


### [110] [Natural-Language Workflows Are Not Software Yet: Artifact-Driven Compilation for Reliable Agent Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21341)
*Xiangzhe Xu,Hanxi Guo,Guangyu Shen,Siyuan Cheng,Xiangyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Natural-language workflows offer a software-like interface for agents: domain experts can write reusable procedures, and agents can execute them as instructions. This promise is not yet reliable. Workflow descriptions often leave data dependencies implicit, so the executor must infer which prior results a step should use; agents can also fail to follow long or branching instructions under context pressure. We propose Artic, an artifact-driven workflow compiler that transforms a natural-language workflow into an artifact-driven workflow in which each step declares the artifacts it reads and writes, constraints gate produced artifacts, and explicit control transfers route execution. This representation exposes the enforcement burden placed on agent execution, allowing the compiler to identify steps that depend on too much state or contain difficult control logic and refine them through constrained optimization.
