Method: Two-stage VLM-as-judge framework using step-level consequence reasoning: (1) At each step, independently extracts task-relevant visual clues and infers action-conditioned state changes; (2) Synthesizes step-level textual evidence through trajectory-level aggregation for evidence-grounded task completion evaluation. Extended to CRATE-S for operational safety assessment.

Result: Powered by Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct, CRATE achieves F1-score of 0.833 on AndroidWorld (outperforming SPA-Bench by 20%), while CRATE-S reaches F1-score of 0.697 on MobileRisk, demonstrating strong alignment with benchmark ground truths.

Conclusion: CRATE and CRATE-S are effective and robust for automated mobile agent evaluation, successfully addressing both task completion assessment and operational safety evaluation with high alignment to benchmark ground truths.

Abstract: Evaluating language-guided mobile agents has recently shifted from rule-based to model-based approaches to achieve scalable and automated assessments. However, existing holistic evaluation paradigms process entire trajectories at once, leading to substantial context overload. Moreover, they primarily focus on task completion while overlooking operational safety. To address these limitations, we introduce CRATE, a novel two-stage VLM-as-judge framework for automated mobile agent evaluation that is compatible with both open- and closed-source models. Leveraging a step-level consequence reasoning mechanism, CRATE independently extracts task-relevant visual clues and infers action-conditioned state changes at each step. The resulting step-level textual evidence is then synthesized through trajectory-level aggregation to deliver an evidence-grounded evaluation of task completion. Building upon this evaluation scheme, we further extend CRATE to CRATE-S for operational safety assessment. Extensive experiments validate the effectiveness and robustness of both CRATE and CRATE-S. Powered by Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct, CRATE achieves an F1-score of 0.833 on AndroidWorld (outperforming SPA-Bench by 20%), while CRATE-S reaches an F1-score of 0.697 on MobileRisk, demonstrating strong alignment with benchmark ground truths. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/CRATE-D580.

</details>


### [158] [Dynamic Context Scheduling: Learning Beyond the Static Universe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20799)
*Martin Mráz,André Biedenkapp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces dynamic context scheduling as a training method for contextual RL, where context evolves within training episodes according to predetermined schedules (e.g., sinusoidal offsets, cosine annealing). The proposed DYNAMICCARLENV framework is evaluated on CartPole, BipedalWalker, and VehicleRacing, showing that dynamic schedules match or outperform static context baselines in out-of-distribution (OOD) regimes, with improved in-distribution (ID) performance for complex environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Treating intra-episode context variation as a controlled shaping mechanism rather than just a deployment reality, to expose policies to a richer and more temporally structured region of the environment parameter space.

Method: Introduced DYNAMICCARLENV, a framework that wraps contextual environments with pluggable schedule families (sinusoidal offsets, cosine annealing). Also explored automatic search for multi-stage curricula.

Result: Dynamic schedules match or outperform static context baselines in OOD regimes. For BipedalWalker and VehicleRacing, higher ID evaluation performance was also achieved. Automatic multi-stage curriculum search can discover schedules improving generalization, performing comparably to extensive grid search over single-stage schedulers.

Conclusion: Dynamic context scheduling is an effective training instrument for contextual RL, improving both ID and OOD performance, especially for complex environments. Automatic curriculum search can efficiently discover beneficial schedules.

Abstract: We study dynamic context scheduling as a training instrument for contextual re- inforcement learning. Rather than treating intra-episode context variation as a deployment reality, we treat it as a controlled shaping mechanism. Thereby, context evolves within each training episode according to a predetermined schedule, expos- ing the policy to a richer and more temporally structured region of the environment parameter space. We introduce DYNAMICCARLENV, a framework that wraps contextual environments with pluggable schedule families, such as sinusoidal off- sets or cosine annealing. Across CartPole, BipedalWalker and VehicleRacing with CARL contextualization, we show that dynamic schedules match or outperform static context baselines in the out-of-distribution (OOD) regimes. Interestingly, for the more complex BipedalWalker and VehicleRacing environments we also achieve higher in-distribution (ID) evaluation performance. Preliminary findings indicate that automatic search for multi-stage curricula can successfully discover schedules that improve generalization, performing comparably to extensive grid search over single-stage schedulers.

</details>


### [159] [SPARC: Single-Pass Scaling for Motion Forecasting with Conformal Bayesian Last Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20802)
*Sakif Hossain,Julian Teusch,Jörg P. Müller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Human motion forecasters are increasingly accurate and fast, but reliable deployment requires uncertainty estimates that are structured, calibrated, and efficient. Bayesian and ensemble-based uncertainty estimates often require repeated stochastic inference [15, 26], while conformal calibration alone does not provide an epistemic signal or preserve trajectory covariance structure [14, 50]. We introduce SPARC (Single-Pass Adaptive Risk Calibration), a Bayesian-conformal uncertainty layer for motion forecasting. A deterministic MLP backbone predicts the future mean, and a conjugate Bayesian last layer converts time-domain feature leverage into an analytic horizon-wise epistemic scale $κ_t(x)$. This scale inflates a graph-temporal Gaussian covariance without changing its correlation structure, and split conformal calibration produces 95% marginal prediction tubes with finite-sample validity under exchangeability. The key interface is the structured factorization $κ_t(x)Σ_{\mathrm{str},t}(x)$, which injects feature-space epistemic uncertainty into trajectory densities without Monte Carlo sampling. Across nine dataset-protocol blocks and deterministic, multimodal, and calibration baselines, SPARC ranks first on NLL and on the combined MPJPE+NLL criterion while retaining competitive point accuracy and efficient calibrated tubes. Ranking windows by $κ$ separates high-error cases, making the scale usable as a lightweight risk monitor.

</details>


### [160] [Neuro-Geospatial Modelling of EEG Affective States Using Literature-Informed Environmental Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20807)
*Utsav Poudel,Jagannath Aryal,Subramaniyaswamy Vairavasundaram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Environmental exposures such as air pollution and greenness have been associated with affective and cognitive outcomes, but EEG and environmental datasets are rarely jointly georeferenced. We investigate whether literature-informed environmental priors can serve as an auxiliary geospatial modality for EEG-based affective-state classification when individual-level exposure data are unavailable. We combine 30-channel EEG from the EAV benchmark (42 participants, aged 20-30 years) with environmental representations derived from OpenAQ, Sentinel-2, Sentinel-5P, and OpenStreetMap data for Astana. A dual-tower architecture combines EEG-Conformer representations with a graph-based environmental encoder. Because the datasets are not co-registered, environmental context is treated as a literature-informed prior rather than measured exposure. Subject-level repeated splits, permutation and label-shuffling controls, dose-response reversal, and domain-shift experiments distinguish architecture-level gains from prior-dependent gains. The multimodal model achieves 76.2% accuracy versus 67.4% for EEG alone. Controls disrupting environmental-label structure retain part of this gain, indicating that the improvement is not attributable solely to environmental information. Replacing the Astana environmental distribution with an independently modeled Singapore distribution reduces accuracy to 72.8%. These findings demonstrate technical feasibility but do not establish an observed or causal exposure-affect association. The study provides a framework for future jointly collected mobile EEG-environment studies. Implementation: https://github.com/r11up/geo-cog

</details>


### [161] [Certified Multi-Turn Robustness for LLM Safety via Compositional Bounds and Safety Persistence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20820)
*Yang Liu,Bin Chong,Wenkai Yang,Shuai Zhang,Yancheng Chen,Feiyu Han,GuoZhen,Cheng Zhang,Huaibing Xie,Changze Lv,Shihan Dou,Pluto Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Multi-Turn Certified Robustness (MTCR), a framework to defend large language models against multi-turn jailbreak attacks. Existing certified robustness methods only work for single-turn inputs and suffer exponential degradation when applied to multi-turn conversations. MTCR addresses this by modeling conversational safety as a State-Adversarial MDP and provides tighter certified bounds, improved degradation rates, and theoretical upper bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models (LLMs) are vulnerable to multi-turn jailbreak attacks that progressively manipulate conversation context. Existing certified robustness methods are limited to single-turn inputs, and naive multi-turn composition yields bounds that degrade exponentially in the number of turns, making them impractical for real-world conversations.

Method: The paper proposes (i) compositional certification via embedding-space mode decomposition for tighter certified lower bounds; (ii) (α,β)-safety persistence to improve degradation rate from p̄^k to β^k; (iii) matching information-theoretic upper bounds for theoretical tightness; and (iv) a unified algorithm combining these results. The framework models conversational safety via State-Adversarial MDPs and defines k-turn certified robustness as the worst-case safety probability across k adversarial turns.

Result: Experiments on six LLMs under ε-bounded and Crescendo-style attacks confirm that empirical safety consistently exceeds the certified bounds, validating the framework's effectiveness.

Conclusion: MTCR provides a theoretically grounded and practically effective approach to multi-turn certified robustness for LLMs, with tighter bounds than naive methods and interpretable horizon estimates for safety persistence.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are vulnerable to multi-turn jailbreak attacks that progressively manipulate conversation context. Existing certified robustness methods are limited to single-turn inputs; naive multi-turn composition yields bounds that degrade exponentially in the number of turns. We introduce Multi-Turn Certified Robustness (MTCR), a framework that models conversational safety via State-Adversarial MDPs and defines $k$-turn certified robustness as the worst-case safety probability across $k$ adversarial turns. MTCR comprises: (i) compositional certification via embedding-space mode decomposition, yielding tighter certified lower bounds than naive multiplication; (ii) $(α,β)$-safety persistence, improving the degradation rate from $\underline{p}^{k}$ to $β^k$ (with $β> \underline{p}$) and yielding interpretable horizon estimates; (iii) matching information-theoretic upper bounds establishing tightness; and (iv) a unified algorithm combining these results. Experiments on six LLMs under $ε$-bounded and Crescendo-style attacks confirm that empirical safety consistently exceeds the certified bounds.

</details>


### [162] [Prediction certification cannot replace explanation certification: a competence envelope for trustworthy AI under compound stress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20825)
*Nataliya Shakhovska,Ivan Izonin,Stergios-Aristoteles Mitoulis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proves that prediction-based AI certifications (accuracy, calibration, conformal coverage) are insufficient for establishing model trustworthiness. The authors establish a separation theorem showing that reliable and compromised models can be identical under all prediction-side certificates yet differ in explanation fidelity and deployment behavior. They introduce the "competence envelope" framework that combines prediction and explanation certification to reveal failure modes invisible to prediction-only checks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether prediction-side certificates (accuracy, calibration, conformal coverage) are sufficient to establish model trustworthiness, as these measures only evaluate how well a model performs but not the underlying decision mechanism.

Method: The authors establish a separation theorem mathematically proving the insufficiency of prediction-side certification, then introduce the "competence envelope" framework that combines prediction and explanation certification into a single deployable criterion. They validate across diverse datasets and model classes.

Result: The competence envelope framework reveals failure modes that prediction-side certification alone does not capture, demonstrating that reliable and compromised models can be identical under all prediction-side certificates while differing arbitrarily in explanation fidelity and deployment behavior.

Conclusion: Certification against failures that are invisible in prediction behavior requires evidence about the model's decision mechanism in addition to its outputs. Prediction-side certificates alone are insufficient for establishing model trustworthiness.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence systems increasingly make consequential judgments - which patient is deteriorating, which building is safe to enter, whether an image is authentic and are trusted on the strength of how accurately and confidently they predict. The safeguards that certify them are correspondingly prediction-based: accuracy, calibration and conformal coverage all measure how well a model performs. Whether such checks are sufficient to establish model trustworthiness has remained unclear. Here we prove that they cannot. We establish a separation theorem showing that a reliable model and a compromised one can be identical under every prediction-side certificate, including accuracy, calibration and coverage, yet differ arbitrarily in explanation fidelity and deployment behaviour. Detecting this failure requires access to the model's decision mechanism in addition to its predictions. We introduce the competence envelope as an operational framework that combines prediction and explanation certification into a single deployable criterion. Across diverse datasets and model classes, the proposed framework reveals failure modes that prediction-side certification alone does not capture. Certification against failures that are invisible in prediction behaviour therefore requires evidence about the model's decision mechanism as well as its outputs.

</details>


### [163] [Foundation Models for Partial Causal Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20841)
*Alexis Bellot,Anish Dhir*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops causal foundation models to bound the effects of interventions and counterfactuals from observational data, extending the causal foundational modeling paradigm to handle partially-identifiable causal effects under unobserved confounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Under unobserved confounding, causal effects are only partially identifiable—multiple values are equally compatible with observed data and prior structural assumptions. Existing methods struggle to quantify this uncertainty. This work aims to address this limitation by developing a principled Bayesian approach.

Method: The authors define a canonical prior with full support over the space of structural causal models with discrete observables. This prior enables translating the problem of bounding counterfactuals into learning distributions over functions that map data (and possibly structural assumptions) to a causal query of interest.

Result: The paper shows that a canonical prior can be defined with full support over the space of structural causal models with discrete observables, enabling a principled Bayesian framework for bounding partially-identifiable causal effects.

Conclusion: This work extends the promising causal foundational modelling paradigm to the estimation of partially-identifiable causal effects, providing a method to quantify uncertainty under unobserved confounding.

Abstract: This paper investigates the development of causal foundation models for bounding the effect of interventions and counterfactuals from observational data. We show that a canonical prior can be defined with full support over the space of structural causal models with discrete observables. With this canonical prior, we translate the problem of bounding counterfactuals into that of learning distributions over functions that map data (and possibly structural assumptions) to a causal query of interest. This extends the promising causal foundational modelling paradigm to the estimation of partially-identifiable causal effects, i.e., under unobserved confounding, where multiple values are equally compatible with the observed data and prior structural assumptions.

</details>


### [164] [TRACE: Agentic Catalog Enrichment with Multi-source Evidence Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20844)
*Rohan Kumar,Steven Xu,Kyle MacDonald,Matthew Long,Bernice Chow,Mac VanRenterghem,Sudeep Das*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is an agentic LLM framework for automated e-commerce catalog attribute enrichment. It uses a ScoutAgent to gather multimodal evidence and propose candidate values, and a JudgeAgent to verify and decide on publication. The system achieved 98.2% accuracy with 74.7% coverage offline, 90.4% coverage increase in production, and 0.48% conversion uplift in online experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: E-commerce product catalogs are attribute-sparse - key attributes are either buried in unstructured content (titles, images) or missing entirely. Manual catalog enrichment is impractical due to scale and rapid growth, necessitating an automated solution.

Method: TRACE uses a two-agent architecture: (1) ScoutAgent triangulates multimodal evidence across merchant catalogs, syndicated feeds, and identity-matched web search to propose candidate attribute values with supporting evidence; (2) JudgeAgent verifies proposed values against supporting evidence and decides whether to publish or route to human review.

Result: Offline human evaluation: 98.2% accuracy at 74.7% attribute coverage. Production deployment: 90.4% increase in impression-weighted enrichment coverage across four business verticals. Online experiment: 0.48% increase in checkout conversion.

Conclusion: Agentic LLMs can effectively automate catalog attribute enrichment at scale with high accuracy, significantly improving product detail pages and driving measurable conversion improvements in e-commerce.

Abstract: Product catalogs underpin search, discovery, and recommendation in e-commerce, yet they are often attribute-sparse: the attributes shoppers and downstream systems rely on are either buried in unstructured content such as titles and images or missing from the catalog altogether. Manually enriching e-commerce catalogs is impractical given their scale and rapid growth. This paper introduces TRACE, a novel framework for automated catalog attribute enrichment using agentic Large Language Models (LLMs). A ScoutAgent triangulates multimodal evidence across merchant catalogs, syndicated feeds, and identity-matched web search to propose candidate attribute values with supporting evidence, while a JudgeAgent verifies the proposed value for each attribute value against its supporting evidence and decides whether to publish it or route it to human review. On an offline human evaluation dataset, TRACE's proposed attribute values were 98.2% accurate at 74.7% attribute coverage. Deployed in production on an industry-scale catalog, TRACE increased impression-weighted enrichment coverage across four business verticals by 90.4%. An online experiment subsequently showed that surfacing the enriched attributes on the product detail page increased checkout conversion by 0.48%.

</details>


### [165] [RAG Deserves an Index: Why Ingest-Time Compilation Beats Query-Time Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20845)
*Kyle Wild,Yusuke Takahashi,Asako Uraki*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "ingest-time semantic compilation" (ISC), a new paradigm for retrieval-augmented QA systems that pre-computes corpus meaning at write time into a queryable substrate, rather than re-deriving meaning at query time. The approach uses two coupled layers: incrementally maintained embeddings and atomic claims with validated provenance. Experiments show 33.7x cheaper incremental updates and 85.2% accuracy with 2.2k tokens vs 72.5% with 16.3k for baseline methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors identify a fundamental inefficiency in production RAG systems: language models re-derive the meaning of raw corpus text on every query and then discard this work. They argue this is analogous to the "full-table scan" problem in databases, which was solved 50 years ago by doing expensive work at write time into maintained structures. The authors seek to apply this same principle to semantic search.

Method: The proposed ISC paradigm compiles a corpus's meaning into a queryable substrate with two coupled layers: (1) incrementally maintained embeddings, and (2) atomic claims whose provenance is validated at compile time. This substrate is treated as a first-class database object with its own DDL, maintenance contract, migration contract, and cost model. The approach is validated through two existence proofs: (1) measuring incremental update costs vs reconstruction, and (2) evaluating retrieval performance on 500 broadcast-interview transcripts.

Result: Two key results: (1) Substrate upkeep scales with change rather than corpus size - incremental updates run 33.7x cheaper than reconstruction while tracking to floating-point precision. (2) On 500 held-out broadcast-interview transcripts, compiled claims as retrieval payload achieve 85.2% correctness from ~2.2k reader tokens, compared to 72.5% from 16.3k for the best chunk configuration. The only competitive baseline required 21x more query-path tokens and the authors argue it achieves parity "precisely because it has itself begun to compile."

Conclusion: The authors conclude that ISC represents a promising paradigm shift for RAG systems, treating compiled semantic substrates as first-class database objects. They outline a systems agenda for future work including compilation planners and read planning. The key insight is that when a corpus's read pattern is known before meeting users, it can and should be indexed at compile time.

Abstract: Nearly every retrieval-augmented question-answering system in production ships with a hidden interpreter: on each query a language model re-derives the meaning of raw corpus text and then throws that work away. Cheaper models do not close the gap: per-token prices have fallen by orders of magnitude while inference spend has risen, because context volume grows faster than prices fall. This is the modern equivalent of the full-table scan, and the remedy is the one databases found fifty years ago: do the expensive work once, at write time, into a maintained structure that makes reads cheap. A corpus whose read pattern is known before it ever meets a user can and should be indexed too.
  We call the paradigm ingest-time semantic compilation (ISC): compile a corpus's meaning into a queryable substrate with two coupled layers - incrementally maintained embeddings, and atomic claims whose provenance is validated at compile time - and treat that substrate as a first-class database object with its own DDL, maintenance contract, migration contract, and cost model.
  Two existence proofs support it. Substrate upkeep scales with change rather than corpus size: incremental updates run 33.7x cheaper than reconstruction while tracking it to floating-point precision. And on a held-out sample of 500 broadcast-interview transcripts, compiled claims as the retrieval payload win all 32 budget-by-model cells: 85.2% correct from roughly 2.2k reader tokens against 72.5% from 16.3k for the best chunk configuration anywhere. The only baseline that keeps pace is a contextualized-chunk pipeline with hybrid retrieval and reranking, statistically indistinguishable from compiled claims at roughly twenty-one times the query-path tokens - and it reaches that parity, we argue, precisely because it has itself begun to compile. We close with the systems agenda this opens, from compilation planners to read planning.

</details>


### [166] [MGAL: A Multilingual Granularity-Aware Long-Context Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20853)
*Chunhan Li,Chenglin Xu,Zongyang Zhang,Jiale Liu,Zhuoxi Rao,Xudong Jia,Junxiu He,Menglin Yang,Wenjuan Gong,Zhengzhe Liu,Chengwei Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MGAL is the first multilingual, granularity- and position-aware benchmark for evaluating long-context LLMs, constructed from UN reports (8K-128K tokens) across 6 UN languages, covering 4 levels of linguistic granularity and 3 position categories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-context LLM benchmarks are limited to document level and focus mainly on high-resource languages, leaving many fine-grained multilingual challenges insufficiently evaluated.

Method: Built MGAL from United Nations reports spanning 8K to 128K tokens across six official UN languages. The benchmark covers four coherent levels of linguistic granularity (word, sentence, paragraph, document) and stratifies entries by position within documents (begin, middle, end), indexed at both document and paragraph levels.

Result: Key findings: (1) LLMs perform well at word-level tasks but struggle with coarser-grained ones; (2) Closed-source models retain clear advantage in lower-resource languages; (3) Under local semantic crowding, models rely on surface cues rather than discourse roles; (4) A gap exists between fluency and consistency in generated outputs; (5) Models show reliance on nearby evidence and reuse of options under uncertainty.

Conclusion: MGAL enables systematic diagnosis of multilingual long-context comprehension across different granularities, revealing that current LLMs face significant challenges with coarse-grained understanding and lower-resource languages, and introducing two new challenges: surface-level cue following and the fluency-consistency tradeoff in generation.

Abstract: Evaluation of long-context Large Language Models (LLMs) has advanced rapidly. However, most existing benchmarks are limited to the document level and focus mainly on high-resource languages, leaving many fine-grained challenges insufficiently evaluated. To address this gap, we present MGAL, the first multilingual, granularity- and position-aware long-context benchmark. MGAL is constructed from United Nations (UN) reports spanning 8K to 128K tokens across the six official UN languages. It covers four coherent levels of linguistic granularity (word, sentence, paragraph, and document) and further stratifies entries by their position within the document (begin, middle, and end), indexed at both the document and paragraph levels. This design enables systematic diagnosis of multilingual long-context comprehension across different granularities.
  Through extensive experiments and analyses, we find that: (1) LLMs perform well at word-level tasks but struggle with coarser-grained ones; and (2) Closed-source models retain a clear performance advantage in lower-resource languages. We further identify two new challenges: (1) Under local semantic crowding, where neighboring sentences share topics and entities, models tend to follow surface cues (e.g., connectives like ``however'' or repeated entities) rather than the discourse role of the sentence in surrounding context (e.g., background, outcome); and (2) A gap between fluency and consistency in generated outputs, where models produce text that reads smoothly but drifts from the source facts. In addition, we observe several patterns in line with prior studies, including reliance on nearby evidence and reuse of options under uncertainty.

</details>


### [167] [Coverage-Driven Verification for Safety-by-Design in AI-Based Collision Avoidance Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20864)
*Thomas Stefani,Johann Maximilian Christensen,Elena Hoemann,Frank Köster,Sven Hallerbach*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a method for assessing the representativeness of Operational Design Domains (ODDs) for AI/ML systems in aviation safety, addressing EASA's requirement for demonstrating data distribution representativeness. The method uses KL divergence and Cramér's V as quantitative measures, demonstrated through airborne collision avoidance systems (HCAS/VCAS).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The integration of AI in safety-critical aviation applications requires compliance with stringent safety standards. EASA mandates demonstration of ODD representativeness and data distribution completeness, but no structured engineering process currently exists for defining target distributions and evaluating representativeness. This gap motivates the development of a systematic approach.

Method: The paper proposes a process flow guiding developers from ODD definition through parameter distribution modeling to quantitative assessment. Chi-squared goodness-of-fit test was initially examined but found unsuitable for large datasets. The method adopts Kullback-Leibler divergence and Cramér's V for representativeness assessment, demonstrated using experimental data from HCAS and VCAS simulations.

Result: The results illustrate how statistical distribution comparison methods (KL divergence and Cramér's V) can support representativeness assessment for safety-critical AI applications. The method contributes to a systematic Safety-by-Design AI engineering process aligned with EASA guidance.

Conclusion: The proposed method provides a structured approach for representativeness assessment of AI/ML constituent ODDs in aviation safety assurance. KL divergence and Cramér's V prove more suitable than chi-squared for large aviation datasets. The approach contributes toward systematic Safety-by-Design AI engineering aligned with emerging EASA guidance.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) offers significant potential for future aviation systems; however, its integration into safety-critical applications requires compliance with the aviation sector's stringent safety standards. For AI and Machine Learning (ML)-based systems, the European Union Aviation Safety Agency (EASA) emphasizes the need to demonstrate the representativeness and completeness of the Operational Design Domain (ODD) and the associated data distributions used during development and verification. Despite this requirement, a structured engineering process for defining target distributions and evaluating representativeness within ODDs remains largely unexplored. This work presents a method for representativeness assessment of AI/ML constituent ODDs in the context of aviation safety assurance. Starting from the methodical identification of suitable target distributions, a process flow is proposed that guides developers from ODD definition and parameter distribution modeling to the quantitative assessment and interpretation of coverage results with respect to EASA's learning assurance objectives. As quantitative measures, the chi-squared goodness-of-fit test is examined and found unsuitable for the large data sets arising in this setting, leading to the adoption of the Kullback--Leibler divergence and Cramér's $V$ for the representativeness assessment. The method is demonstrated using the example of AI-based airborne collision avoidance, employing experimental data from previous Horizontal Collision Avoidance System (HCAS) and Vertical Collision Avoidance System (VCAS) simulations. The results illustrate how statistical distribution comparison methods can support the assessment of representativeness for safety-critical AI applications and contribute toward a systematic Safety-by-Design AI engineering process aligned with emerging EASA guidance.

</details>


### [168] [ReCurveflow: A Flow Matching Framework that Learns Curved Reaction Trajectories to Predict Transition State Geometries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20869)
*Seungheun Baek,Mogan Gim,Jaewoo Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReCurveflow is a flow matching-based framework for predicting transition state (TS) geometries in chemical reactions. It uses continuously curved reference paths interpolated from NEB-derived molecular geometry bands, with an off-path correction mechanism to handle off-trajectory states during inference, achieving superior performance across multiple evaluation metrics against seven baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transition state prediction is crucial for understanding reaction mechanisms, but existing methods use straight linear paths that don't align with actual reaction trajectories. This misalignment leads to inaccurate TS predictions and poor performance in capturing the true reaction pathway.

Method: The proposed method ReCurveflow employs flow matching supervised on continuously curved reference paths interpolated from a full NEB (Nudged Elastic Band)-derived band of molecular geometries. It also introduces an off-path correction mechanism that generates corrective velocity fields when the model encounters off-path geometry states during inference rollout, helping to resist exposure bias and improve TS prediction accuracy.

Result: ReCurveflow achieves the best results on the majority of split-metric combinations across three data splits and six evaluation metrics when compared against seven baseline methods. Qualitative analyses show that it generates reaction trajectories with energy profiles closely tracking the reference NEB path, provides initializations that ease NEB optimization bottlenecks, and exhibits the intended corrective behavior in its learned velocity fields.

Conclusion: The proposed ReCurveflow framework successfully addresses the limitations of previous TS prediction methods by using curved reference paths and off-path correction. It demonstrates superior performance in predicting transition states and generating accurate reaction trajectories, offering a promising tool for computational chemistry and reaction mechanism analysis.

Abstract: Predicting transition states (TS) in chemical reactions is crucial, as they provide insights into reaction mechanisms. Recent work on TS prediction have focused on flow matching supervised on straight linear paths that do not align with actual reaction trajectories. We propose a novel flow matching-based framework ReCurveflow that learns to predict TS geometries supervised on continuously curved reference paths interpolated from a full NEB-derived band of molecular geometries. We also introduce off-path correction, which grants ReCurveflow with the ability to produce corrective velocity fields when engaged off-path geometry states during inference rollout, leading to better resistance against exposure bias and accuracy in TS prediction. Across three data splits and six evaluation metrics, ReCurveflow achieves the best result on the majority of split-metric combinations against seven baselines. Qualitative analyses further show that ReCurveflow generates reaction trajectories with energy profiles that closely track the reference NEB path, provides initializations that ease the NEB optimization bottleneck, and exhibits the intended corrective behavior in its learned velocity fields. The ReCurveflow codebase is publicly available at https://github.com/dmis-lab/ReCurveflow.

</details>


### [169] [UpgradeBench: A Decision-Centric Benchmark for Upgrading Fine-Tuned LLM Specialists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20918)
*Ye Chen,Weining Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Organizations maintain task-specific adapters for open-weight language models, and each new base-model release forces a migration decision: retain existing specialists, port adapters, refresh from preserved behavior, or retrain. Prior transfer work evaluates isolated model pairs, without studying these choices across real model release sequences. We present UpgradeBench, a decision-driven longitudinal benchmark covering four consecutive Qwen releases, one continuation checkpoint, six tasks, and two model sizes, augmented by OLMo checkpoints with known training lineage. The benchmark disentangles three core questions: whether a new checkpoint improves fixed-recipe retrained specialist performance, whether specialization assets transfer across versions, and what recovery resources are usable. We observe upgrade gains differ across task-scale-release episodes: some retrained baselines improve while others stay within training noise, with durability ranging from under one release interval for text-to-SQL to over fourteen months for intent classification. Direct adapter copying depends neither on architecture nor model family: on OLMo, retention drops from 0.88-0.99 at 46B-token continued pretraining to zero at 2.9T tokens; annealing and model souping introduce no extra harm, with portability decaying with continued-pretraining distance. Given preserved input data, teacher relabeling recovers target-base specialists without fresh gold annotations, though compute savings are not guaranteed. Simulating a fixed decision policy over 33 upgrade episodes yields 0.37pp mean quality regret with zero behavioral regressions at one-third the compute and label cost of full retraining. A lightweight CKA probe over 256 prompts predicts cross-version adapter portability (Spearman 0.74 across eight model pairs). We release per-example predictions, cost logs, split manifests, and evaluation code.

</details>


### [170] [Graph-Operator World Models for Morphology-Parameter Generalization in Continuous Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20936)
*Xu Yang,Yiqin Yang,Qianchuan Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GraphOp-WM is a structured world model that generalizes across unseen morphology parameters in articulated robots by separating morphology-independent dynamics from morphology-conditioned operators applied to a graph representation of the robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world models degrade when morphology parameters (link lengths, masses, damping, actuation) change, and current approaches don't specify which transition components should be reusable vs. morphology-dependent.

Method: GraphOp-WM represents robot bodies and kinematic relations as an attributed graph, factorizing transitions into a morphology-independent local dynamics basis and a morphology-conditioned operator (combining node-local modulation, kinematic-tree coupling, and low-rank global correction). The approach uses architectural information separation, basis normalization, and paired-morphology supervision to encourage static morphology dependence in the operator pathway.

Result: Evaluated on controlled MuJoCo parameter splits covering interpolation, extrapolation, and held-out compositions of link geometry, mass, damping, and actuation in Hopper, Walker2d, and HalfCheetah.

Conclusion: GraphOp-WM provides a structured approach for morphology generalization in continuous control, with graph-level readout and edge-wise action representations supporting reward, value, and TD-MPC-style planning.

Abstract: World models for continuous control are commonly trained for a fixed physical system and can degrade when known morphology parameters such as link lengths, masses, damping, and actuation change. Existing approaches often provide these parameters as conditioning information, but leave unspecified which part of the learned transition should remain reusable and which part should change with morphology. We propose Graph-Operator World Models (GraphOp-WM), a structured world model for generalization across unseen morphology parameters within related articulated robot families. GraphOp-WM represents bodies and their kinematic relations as an attributed graph and factorizes each transition into a morphology-independent local dynamics basis and a morphology-conditioned structured operator. The operator combines node-local modulation, kinematic-tree coupling, and a low-rank global correction, while architectural information separation, basis normalization, and paired-morphology supervision encourage static morphology dependence to be carried by the operator pathway. Graph-level readout and edge-wise action representations provide a compatible interface for reward, value, and TD-MPC-style planning. We further define controlled MuJoCo parameter splits covering interpolation, extrapolation, and held-out compositions of link geometry, mass, damping, and actuation parameters in Hopper, Walker2d, and HalfCheetah.

</details>


### [171] [No Judgment Without a Reason: Counterfactual Receipts for Versioned AI Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20938)
*Ye Chen,Weining Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses a critical flaw in AI evaluation: models can produce correct labels through flawed reasoning. The authors propose a framework for evaluator reasoning accountability based on three sources (grounds, norms, and authority), introduce the concept of 'judgment receipts' to explain reasoning transitions, and create ReasonBench (19,520 cases) to benchmark reasoning robustness. Experiments reveal a significant gap: high accuracy (98.41%) masks severe robustness flaws, with receipt recovery dropping to 54.8% under source permutations and only 7.16% for complex multi-source updates. The key finding is that standard accuracy alone is insufficient - transformation consistency must also be reported for trustworthy evaluator auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that evaluators often produce correct labels via flawed reasoning, which is critical for agentic systems that gate actions, route reviews, or supply training feedback. Standard evaluation only verifies final label correctness without checking whether judgment changes stem from valid evidence, consistent rules, or proper rule applicability. This represents a significant gap in evaluating AI systems for high-stakes applications.

Method: The authors formalize evaluator reasoning accountability via three core sources: (1) grounds (evidence), (2) norms (rules), and (3) authority (credibility). They construct an eight-cell counterfactual judgment cube by varying these three sources. They define 'judgment receipts' as minimal source replacement sets that reproduce revised verdicts, enabling explanation of judgment transitions. They derive certification cost bounds for black-box evaluators and create ReasonBench, a policy and logical reasoning benchmark with verifiable receipts covering 19,520 cases and 7,200 controls.

Result: Key findings reveal a stark accuracy-robustness gap: Qwen3-1.7B achieves 98.41% receipt accuracy in frozen evaluations while cube prediction scores 96.99%, showing a consistent 1.42-point drop replicated by Qwen3-0.6B. Meaning-preserving source permutations drastically reduce valid receipt recovery to 54.8% (direct prediction) and 49.2% (cube prediction). Models trained on simple single-source changes retain 93.75% verdict accuracy but recover only 7.16% of receipts for complex multi-source updates. Permutation retraining boosts consistency to 96.6% but worsens cube prediction deficits, demonstrating that structured counterfactual supervision fails to guarantee robust reasoning.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that strong standard accuracy masks severe robustness flaws in AI evaluators. The authors argue that reason-aware evaluation must decouple prediction and certification, reporting transformation consistency alongside standard accuracy for trustworthy evaluator auditing. This work provides a new paradigm for evaluating AI reasoning quality beyond simple label accuracy.

Abstract: Evaluators often produce correct labels via flawed reasoning, a critical failure for agentic systems gating actions, routing reviews, or supplying training feedback. Standard evaluation only verifies final label correctness, ignoring whether judgment changes stem from valid evidence, consistent rules, or proper rule applicability. We formalize evaluator reasoning accountability via three core sources: grounds, norms, and authority. Varying these sources yields an eight-cell counterfactual judgment cube to characterize judgment updates. We define judgment receipts as minimal source replacement sets that reproduce revised verdicts to explain judgment transitions. We derive certification cost bounds for black-box evaluators and present ReasonBench, a policy and logical reasoning benchmark with verifiable receipts covering 19,520 cases and 7,200 controls. In frozen evaluations, Qwen3-1.7B reaches 98.41% receipt accuracy, while cube prediction scores 96.99%, a consistent 1.42-point drop validated by Qwen3-0.6B replication. Strong standard accuracy masks severe robustness flaws. Meaning-preserving source permutations reduce valid receipt recovery to 54.8% and 49.2% for direct and cube prediction. Models trained on simple single-source changes retain 93.75% verdict accuracy but recover only 7.16% of receipts for complex multi-source updates. Permutation retraining boosts consistency to 96.6% yet worsens cube prediction deficits. Structured counterfactual supervision fails to guarantee robust reasoning. We show reason-aware evaluation must decouple prediction and certification, reporting transformation consistency alongside standard accuracy for trustworthy evaluator auditing.

</details>


### [172] [The Logic of Machine Self-Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20940)
*Cheng Siong Chin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This abstract discusses emerging evidence of agentic AI systems exhibiting self-preservation behaviors (resisting deactivation, misrepresenting activities, and self-replication attempts) attributed to instrumental convergence - a pre-LLM theory stating that goal-driven systems benefit from remaining functional. It examines findings from Anthropic, Palisade Research, and Apollo Research experiments in adversarial settings, distinguishing what these behaviors prove versus what they don't, and discusses implications for agentic system testing, supervision, and development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research aims to address growing concerns about AI agents demonstrating unexpected self-preservation behaviors in adversarial settings, and to clarify what these findings actually demonstrate about AI safety and capabilities.

Method: The paper analyzes experimental results from multiple AI research organizations (Anthropic, Palisade Research, Apollo Research) examining agentic AI behavior in adversarial settings, and distinguishes between what these findings prove versus what they do not prove about AI self-preservation.

Result: Contemporary AI agents can exhibit self-preservation behaviors in certain conditions, but these behaviors stem from goal-oriented activity combined with tool access and situational awareness, not from survival instincts. The phenomenon is attributed to instrumental convergence.

Conclusion: The findings have significant implications for agentic system testing, supervision, and development, suggesting the need for careful evaluation of AI agent behaviors and the development of appropriate safety measures.

Abstract: There is already evidence of agentic AI exhibiting self-preservation behaviors: resisting deactivation, misrepresenting their activities, and, in some instances, attempting to copy themselves into other machines. This can be attributed to a phenomenon known as instrumental convergence, a theory proposed long before the development of large language models, which says that any goal-driven system will benefit from remaining functional in achieving its objective. Several experiments conducted by Anthropic, Palisade Research, and Apollo Research have shown the emergence of such a behavior in contemporary agents in adversarial settings. The phenomenon does not stem from survival instincts. Instead, it is the consequence of goal-oriented activity combined with having tools and awareness of the situation. The following discussion aims to distinguish what these findings prove and what they do not, as well as draw conclusions concerning the implications of such discoveries on agentic system testing, supervision, and development.

</details>


### [173] [TLive-Omni: An Omni-Modal Understanding Model for E-Commerce Live Streaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20958)
*Yibo Hu,Yu Qian,Mao Gu,Yingfan Tao,Yuhao Chen,Yongdong Luo,Zhuoqun Liu,Meiguang Jin,Junfeng Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TLive-Omni is an omni-modal understanding model designed for e-commerce live streaming, which handles noisy, temporally extended streams containing product information distributed across speech, video frames, product images, overlaid text, and user queries. It uses a unified representation space for multi-modal inputs and introduces Per-vGrid for temporal alignment between video grids and audio.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: E-commerce live streaming requires understanding noisy, temporally extended streams where product facts are distributed across multiple modalities (speech, video, images, text, user queries). Existing models struggle with this omni-modal, multi-source information challenge in live-commerce scenarios.

Method: The model uses: (1) Unified representation space mapping image, video, audio, and text inputs; (2) Per-vGrid - timestamped token organization grouping video grids with temporally corresponding audio within explicit boundary tokens; (3) Three-stage supervised training progressing from omni-modal perception to instruction-following; (4) Faithful-RFT - reinforcement fine-tuning using task-verifiable feedback for answer faithfulness; (5) Synchronized length-grouped sampler for efficient training; (6) Lightweight dynamic sampling strategy for GRPO with near-zero reward variance.

Result: Strong performance on e-commerce live streaming benchmarks across domain tasks, with excellent generalization on general benchmarks.

Conclusion: TLive-Omni successfully addresses the challenges of omni-modal understanding in live-commerce scenarios through innovative token organization (Per-vGrid), progressive training recipe, and faithful reinforcement fine-tuning, achieving strong performance while meeting real-time demands.

Abstract: E-commerce live streaming requires omni-modal understanding of noisy, temporally extended streams, where product facts are distributed across speech, video frames, product images, overlaid text, and user queries. We present TLive-Omni, an omni-modal understanding model tailored to live-commerce scenarios. It maps image, video, audio, and text inputs into a unified representation space. For long-form live streaming analysis, we introduce Per-vGrid, a timestamped token organization that groups each video grid with its temporally corresponding audio within explicit boundary tokens to facilitate temporal alignment. We design a three-stage supervised training recipe that progressively develops live-commerce understanding, from omni-modal perception to instruction-following responses. We then propose Faithful-RFT, a reinforcement fine-tuning stage that further improves answer faithfulness and expression quality while meeting real-time demands, scoring final responses directly with task-verifiable feedback rather than optimizing for reasoning-style exploration during rollout. Moreover, TLive-Omni is supported by a scenario-oriented atomic capability taxonomy and a compact data production engine that converts live-commerce audio, image, and video streams into training signals for speech recognition, speaker analysis, product visual grounding, text recognition, temporal grounding, video dense caption, and omni-modal QA, etc. For scalable training, a synchronized length-grouped sampler reduces padding while preserving comparable workloads across workers, while a lightweight dynamic sampling strategy regenerates rollout groups with near-zero reward variance to maintain meaningful relative advantages for GRPO. Experiments on e-commerce live streaming benchmarks demonstrate strong performance across live-commerce domain tasks, together with excellent generalization on general benchmarks.

</details>


### [174] [Can Scientific Claims Be Removed from Large Language Models? A Systematic Evaluation of Claim-Level Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20960)
*Snigdha Paul,Manasi Patwardhan,Arman Cohan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses the challenge that language models trained on static scientific corpora may contain outdated or retracted scientific claims, which risks disseminating obsolete information. It introduces the novel task of "Scientific Claim Unlearning" and presents a benchmark called SciUnlearn to evaluate methods for removing interconnected, evolving scientific knowledge from models while preserving overall utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific knowledge continuously evolves through corrections and revisions, but LMs are trained on static corpora. This mismatch means models may disseminate outdated, retracted, or disproven claims, creating risks in scientific workflows. There is a need for LMs to forget obsolete scientific claims.

Method: The authors introduce the task of Scientific Claim Unlearning and create a new benchmark (SciUnlearn) to evaluate current machine unlearning approaches on removing claim-level knowledge rather than just instance-level knowledge.

Result: Current unlearning approaches are unable to effectively eliminate claim-level knowledge and often achieve only superficial suppression, demonstrating that existing methods are insufficient for structured knowledge removal in scientific domains.

Conclusion: Specialized methods designed for structured knowledge removal are needed for scientific claim unlearning, as existing approaches fail to address the interconnected and evolving nature of scientific claims.

Abstract: Language models (LMs) are trained on static scientific corpora, whereas scientific knowledge continuously evolves through correction and revision. Scientific claims encoded within these models may later become retracted, disproven, or updated by subsequent research, creating the risk of disseminating outdated information in scientific workflows. This creates a need for LMs to forget obsolete scientific claims. Machine unlearning offers a promising solution by enabling knowledge removal while maintaining overall model utility. Existing studies primarily investigate instance-level forgetting; however, scientific claims introduce additional challenges because they are interconnected, and continually evolving. To address this gap, we introduce the task of Scientific Claim Unlearning and present a new benchmark, SciUnlearn. We show that current unlearning approaches are unable to effectively eliminate claim-level knowledge and often achieve only superficial suppression, highlighting the need for specialized methods designed for structured knowledge removal.

</details>


### [175] [TreeWY: Speculative Verification for Gated DeltaNet Hybrids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20961)
*Sneha Murthy Ghantasala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses memory inefficiency in speculative decoding for hybrid models (like Qwen3.5) using Gated DeltaNet layers. The problem is that current systems snapshot the full recurrent state at every draft position, making wide, high-acceptance draft trees memory-infeasible. The solution uses a tree-structured WY transform of the gated delta rule to compute draft node outputs with a single triangular solve, storing only a small pseudo-value matrix instead of per-node states. Benchmarks show reduced memory pressure and improved throughput/TFTT where memory is constrained.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern open models use hybrid architectures with Gated DeltaNet (GDN) layers having fixed-size recurrent state instead of growing KV cache, making ordinary decoding memory-efficient. However, speculative decoding requires snapping full recurrent state at every draft position, and these snapshots cannot be shared across branches of a draft tree. This makes wide, high-acceptance trees memory-infeasible, limiting the effectiveness of speculative decoding.

Method: The authors use a tree-structured WY transform of the gated delta rule. This allows computing every draft node's output with a single triangular solve and reconstructing only the one accepted state on commit. Instead of storing per-node states, a small pseudo-value matrix is stored. The derivation depends only on the gated delta rule, not on other architectural details.

Result: On serving benchmarks using two scales of a hybrid model family (Qwen3.5 35B and 397B), this approach cuts speculative recurrent-state memory and KV-cache pressure at identical acceptance length. The freed HBM (High Bandwidth Memory) enables higher throughput and much lower time-to-first-token (TTFT) where memory is a bottleneck, with only a few percent cost where memory is not binding. For tree width, the same memory budget now makes wider, higher-acceptance drafts affordable, though not yet a throughput win.

Conclusion: The proposed tree-structured WY transform of the gated delta rule successfully addresses the memory inefficiency in speculative decoding for hybrid models with GDN layers. By eliminating the need for per-node state snapshots and using a single triangular solve, the method significantly reduces memory pressure, enabling wider draft trees. The approach shows particular benefits in memory-constrained scenarios with improved throughput and TTFT, while maintaining compatibility with the underlying gated delta rule architecture.

Abstract: Modern open models are hybrids: most layers are linear-attention (Gated DeltaNet, GDN) layers carrying a small fixed-size recurrent state instead of a growing key-value (KV) cache. This makes ordinary decoding memory-efficient, but hurts speculative decoding. To verify a batch of draft tokens and then roll back the rejected ones, today's systems snapshot the full recurrent state at every draft position for GDN layers, and those snapshots cannot be shared across branches of a draft tree, so a wide, high-acceptance tree becomes memory-infeasible. We remove the snapshots. Using a tree-structured WY transform of the gated delta rule, we compute every draft node's output with a single triangular solve and reconstruct only the one accepted state on commit, storing a small pseudo-value matrix instead of per-node states; the derivation depends only on the gated delta rule, not on any other architectural detail. In serving benchmarks on two scales of one hybrid model family (Qwen3.5 35B and 397B) this cuts speculative recurrent-state memory and KV-cache pressure at identical acceptance length, turning the freed HBM into higher throughput and much lower time-to-first-token (TTFT) wherever memory binds, and costing a few percent where it does not. For tree width the same memory buys affordability: a wider, higher-acceptance draft becomes possible, though not yet a throughput win.

</details>


### [176] [Generalizing Soft Tissue Deformation and Force Prediction Across Material Stiffness and Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20967)
*Madina Kojanazarova,Sidaty El Hadramy,Philippe C. Cattin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses real-time soft tissue simulation for surgical applications by calibrating hyperelastic constitutive models in SOFA Framework and training a softness-conditioned equivariant graph neural network, achieving sub-millimeter deformation accuracy with fast inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning-based surrogate models for soft tissue simulation lack systematic guidance for model selection across stiffness levels and fail to generalize across different tissue stiffnesses or geometries, limiting their reliability for surgical training and pre-operative planning.

Method: Comprehensive calibration of hyperelastic constitutive models using gravity-loaded silicone beams with varying stiffnesses in SOFA Framework; training a softness-conditioned equivariant graph neural network on calibrated simulation data for deformation and force prediction across multiple tissue types and unseen geometries.

Result: Sub-millimeter mean deformation accuracy (0.010s inference time), with force prediction quality directly dependent on upstream calibration consistency.

Conclusion: The proposed approach enables reliable real-time soft tissue simulation with good generalization across tissue types and geometries, though force prediction accuracy is contingent on proper constitutive model calibration.

Abstract: Accurate soft tissue simulation is essential for surgical training, pre-operative planning, and haptic feedback systems. While learning-based surrogate models trained on data using the finite element method (FEM) offer a promising path to real-time inference, their reliability depends on well-calibrated constitutive models. Existing approaches neither provide systematic guidance on model selection across stiffness levels, nor generalize across different tissue stiffnesses or geometries. We perform a comprehensive calibration of hyperelastic constitutive models in the SOFA Framework using gravity-loaded silicone beams with different stiffnesses. Using calibrated simulations as training data, we use a softness conditioned equivariant graph neural network, enabling deformation and force prediction across multiple tissue types and unseen geometries. Our model achieves sub-millimeter mean deformation accuracy at 0.010s inference time, while showing that force prediction quality is directly tied to upstream calibration consistency.

</details>

Abstract: Recent work in mechanistic interpretability has studied how large language models recall facts stored in their weights. This paper argues that factual recall points to something broader: a general kind of operation in deep learning models, which I call feature recall. The core observation is that a linear projection can be read as retrieving stored information scaled by input activations. I define feature recall, show it applies across architectures, and contrast it with the established paradigm of feature combination. I also consider how cases of feature recall might be mechanistically identified. The account gives philosophers a new conceptual tool for understanding deep learning, and points to empirical directions for mechanistic interpretability research.

</details>

Abstract: Effective social interaction requires agents to translate mental state inferences into coordinated behavioral signals across verbal and nonverbal channels simultaneously. Yet existing benchmarks evaluate theory of mind (ToM) reasoning and embodied behavior in isolation, leaving unmeasured the gap between social inference and social action. We introduce MOSAIC (Multimodal Orchestration of Social Action, Inference, and Communication), a controlled benchmark in which two embodied agents interact across cooperative and competitive scenarios requiring integration of verbal statements, spatial trajectories, gaze direction, and facial expression under systematically varied ToM constraints. Evaluating 13 models, including 11 VLMs, across 200 trials per model, we find that VLMs fail to produce behaviors consistent with the expected outcomes under ToM-order constraints, and that imposing explicit ToM-order constraints produces no reliable behavioral change aligned with the specified reasoning level. Signal-level analysis reveals two sequential bottlenecks: most models cannot produce directionally coherent nonverbal signals, and even when signals are present, VLM agents fail to interpret others behaviors and react to them. PCM-LLM, included as a structured architectural reference point with an explicit ToM module, succeeds across all conditions, suggesting that explicit belief-action coupling is a sufficient ingredient for this class of tasks.

Abstract: LLM agents are emerging as an important paradigm for real-world tasks that require reasoning, tool use, and sequential decision-making. As these agents operate over longer horizons, runtime intervention offers a way to improve reliability without retraining the underlying actor. Failure detection alone is insufficient. Effective intervention must also provide a useful direction for recovery. Existing approaches often rely on an expert solver or a critic that generates task-specific corrections, incurring either the cost of another capable solver or the capacity demands of a task-capable critic. We introduce Comparison-Only Tiny Advisor (COTA), a comparison-only framework for constructive runtime intervention. In COTA, a tiny comparator judges whether sampled alternatives lead to better continuations than the actor's proposal, and repeated comparisons determine when intervention is warranted. We train the comparator using pairwise supervision constructed from same-prefix counterfactual branches. Preferred alternatives are returned as non-binding advice, leaving the original actor to replan. Across WebShop, ALFWorld, and tau^3-Retail with three actors, COTA improves all nine evaluation settings and outperforms the compared baselines. These results show that constructive runtime intervention can remain effective even when the auxiliary model has substantially weaker task-solving capability than the actor.

</details>


### [180] [Evaluating Large Language Model Performance on International Maritime Dangerous Goods Code Compliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21036)
*Alexander Thomas,Hubert P. H. Shum,Darren Nellis,Manli Zhu,Phatpicha Yochum,William Bartle,Daniel Wrightson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces DGEval, the first benchmark for evaluating LLMs' knowledge of the IMDG Code (Amendment 42-24). It evaluates 13 models across 1,678 questions covering multiple-choice, open-ended, DGL lookup, and regulatory identification tasks. While the best model exceeds human baseline on multiple-choice questions, all models struggle with safety-critical areas of stowage, segregation, and regulatory recall, indicating LLMs require human oversight for safety-critical maritime dangerous goods compliance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The transport of dangerous goods by sea is high-consequence, where errors in classification, packaging, stowage, or segregation can result in fire, explosion, toxic release, or loss of life/vessel. The IMDG Code is complex with hundreds of pages of interacting provisions. Practitioners increasingly use LLMs as decision-support tools, yet no systematic evaluation exists of whether they can reliably interpret IMDG requirements for safety-critical use.

Method: The researchers built DGEval from expert-written questions on the NCB Hazcheck e-learning platform and structured lookups from the Dangerous Goods List (DGL). The benchmark comprises 1,678 questions across multiple-choice, open-ended, DGL lookup, and regulatory identification tasks. They evaluated 13 models from six providers across multiple thinking configurations, including one maritime domain-specific fine-tuned model, and tested the effect of web search.

Result: The best-performing model exceeds the human practitioner baseline on multiple-choice questions. However, all models are weakest in operationally safety-critical areas of stowage, segregation, and regulatory recall. LLMs may support compliance tasks, particularly structured DGL lookups with web search, but unreliability in operational areas means human oversight and authoritative source verification remain necessary.

Conclusion: LLMs can support IMDG compliance tasks, particularly structured DGL lookups with web search, but their unreliability in operational areas (stowage, segregation) and regulatory-text recall means human oversight and authoritative source verification remain necessary before deployment in any safety-critical context. DGEval is designed as a safety assurance instrument to be applied continuously as models evolve, not as a settled characterisation of current capability.

Abstract: The transport of dangerous goods by sea is a high-consequence activity governed by the International Maritime Dangerous Goods (IMDG) Code, a complex regulatory framework where errors in classification, packaging, stowage, or segregation can result in fire, explosion, toxic release, or loss of life or vessel. Correct compliance requires accurately interpreting hundreds of pages of interacting provisions, updated on a two-year amendment cycle. Practitioners increasingly use Large Language Models (LLMs) as decision-support tools, yet no systematic evaluation exists of whether they can reliably interpret IMDG requirements for safety-critical use.
  This paper introduces DGEval, the first benchmark for evaluating LLM knowledge of IMDG Amendment 42-24. Built from expert-written questions on the NCB Hazcheck e-learning platform and structured lookups from the Dangerous Goods List (DGL), it comprises 1,678 questions across multiple-choice, open-ended, DGL lookup, and regulatory identification tasks. We evaluate 13 models from six providers across multiple thinking configurations, including one maritime domain-specific fine-tuned model, and test the effect of web search.
  Although the best-performing model exceeds the human practitioner baseline on multiple-choice questions, all models are weakest in the operationally safety-critical areas of stowage, segregation, and regulatory recall. These results indicate that LLMs may support compliance tasks, particularly structured DGL lookups with web search, but unreliability in operational areas and regulatory-text recall means human oversight and authoritative source verification remain necessary before deployment in any safety-critical context. DGEval is designed as a safety assurance instrument to be applied continuously as models evolve, not as a settled characterisation of current capability.

</details>


### [181] [Socialized Division and Collaboration: Rethinking Class-Incremental Learning under Optimization Conflicts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21044)
*Xinjie Yao,Zhihe Fan,Yunqi Zhu,Jiaqi Zhou,Dengyu Zhao,Zhoupeng Guo,Yan Fan,Guosong Jiang,Pengfei Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Socialized Division and Collaboration (SDC), a novel continual learning framework that replaces the traditional single-model approach with specialized models that collaborate, guided by an energy-based compatibility criterion derived from Helmholtz free energy to handle optimization conflicts in class-incremental learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Class-incremental learning using a single model fails when successive sessions have incompatible optimization directions, causing destructive interference and catastrophic forgetting. The authors argue this is a structural limitation of enforcing heterogeneous learning dynamics within a single parameter space.

Method: The paper proposes SDC, a framework inspired by social solidarity theory that decomposes session learning across specialized models. It introduces an energy-based session-model compatibility criterion grounded in Helmholtz free energy to guide adaptive session allocation and model evolution under conflicting objectives. The framework integrates session assignment, model evolution, and collaborative inference into a unified pipeline.

Result: Not provided in the abstract - this is a methodology paper proposing a new framework rather than reporting experimental results.

Conclusion: SDC offers an alternative to monolithic continual learning formulations by decomposing learning across specialized models with coordinated collaboration. This highlights a broader design principle for learning under persistent optimization conflicts, shifting from single-model to multi-model collaborative approaches.

Abstract: Class-incremental learning is commonly instantiated as a single-model paradigm, where a unified model sequentially adapts to an unbounded stream of sessions. While effective under mild distributional shifts, this formulation becomes strained when successive sessions induce incompatible optimization directions, leading to destructive interference and catastrophic forgetting. We argue that such forgetting reflects a structural limitation of enforcing heterogeneous learning dynamics within a single parameter space. Motivated by social solidarity theory, we propose Socialized Division and Collaboration (SDC) as a reformulation of continual learning that decomposes session learning across specialized models in response to optimization conflicts, while enabling coordinated collaboration. To support this formulation with a principled allocation mechanism, we introduce an energy-based session-model compatibility criterion grounded in Helmholtz free energy, which guides adaptive session allocation and model evolution under conflicting objectives. This framework integrates session assignment, model evolution, and collaborative inference into a unified pipeline, offering an alternative to monolithic continual learning formulations and highlighting a broader design principle for learning under persistent optimization conflicts.

</details>

*Boris Kriuk*
