Abstract: Shape-constrained and physics-informed learning reports an accuracy cost of enforcing a prior and treats it as a property of the prior. We show it is mostly a property of the free features and the validation split. Let P be the excess risk of restricting a hypothesis class to functions with a shape constraint on features S, and D the excess risk of the ablated model that ignores S. Because a function constant in x_j is both non-decreasing and non-increasing in x_j, the ablated class is contained in the constrained class, so 0 <= P <= D for every risk functional, with no convexity, smoothness, or realizability assumption. Empirically the bound is a sign test: a constrained model must never be beaten by its own ablation. We instantiate it on an ordinal wildfire-severity task (N = 26,681, K = 3) with hard monotone constraints on four meteorological drivers, coordinates left free, and a validation ladder from i.i.d. resampling to 2-degree spatial blocking. Coordinates act as a shield: alone they recover 92.9% of the full model's macro-F1 under spatial blocking, collapsing D from 0.1288 to 0.0427; the same prior costs 0.0473 shielded and 0.3470 unshielded, a ratio of 7.3 with identical physics. Because D is protocol-dependent it does not transfer: coarsening blocks from 1 to 10 degrees drives D from 0.0942 to 0.0050, leaving two configurations unidentifiable a priori. Inversions of the certified nesting bound the pipeline's additive resolution: over 318 comparisons they give a self-calibrating floor of 0.0220 macro-F1, below which no reported price is interpretable, including four cells in our own headline grid. Cost and compliance are independent: the unconstrained model violates the prior at rate 0.48-0.49 while enforcing it costs 0.0473. We give a two-fit screen that rejects unidentifiable experiments before a constrained model is trained.

</details>


### [183] [CellPath-Bench: A Multidimensional Benchmark for Whole-Slide Cellular Representations in Pathology Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21060)
*Bokai Zhao,Yiyang Zhang,Hanqing Chao,Yawei Ma,Long Bai,Tai Ma,Minfeng Xu,Ming Song,Tianzi Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CellPath-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating pathology foundation models (PFMs) on their cellular representation capabilities, including cell-type decodability and cross-domain transferability. It uses 25 spatially aligned H&E-Xenium tissue sections (11 organs, 7M+ cells) and evaluates 30 models through 304,920 runs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks cannot systematically diagnose whole-slide cellular representation capabilities of PFMs, including decodability of cell-type information and transferability across tissue sections, datasets, and anatomical organs.

Method: Constructed a panel of 25 spatially aligned H&E-Xenium tissue sections (11 organs, 7,079,283 cells) with fine- and coarse-grained taxonomies. Sampled frozen WSI feature maps at registered nuclear coordinates and evaluated using standardized multiclass linear probes. Two key metrics: CRA (Cell Representation Advantage) for within-section advantage, and CRT (Cell Representation Transferability) for cross-domain generalization.

Result: Benchmarked 30 pathology-specific and general-purpose foundation models. Results reveal substantial model-dependent differences in cell-type decodability and its cross-domain generalization, yielding distinct multidimensional capability profiles.

Conclusion: CellPath-Bench provides a standardized framework for auditing cellular information in frozen PFM representations, enabling systematic evaluation of cell-type decodability and transferability across domains.

Abstract: Pathology foundation models (PFMs) are increasingly used as general-purpose backbones, yet existing benchmarks cannot systematically diagnose their whole-slide cellular representation capabilities, including the decodability of cell-type information and the transferability of such information across tissue sections, datasets, and anatomical organs. We introduce CellPath-Bench, a cellular-resolution benchmark that evaluates frozen PFMs themselves. Following quality control of 52 candidate Xenium datasets, we construct a panel of 25 spatially aligned H\&E--Xenium tissue sections spanning 11 organs and 7,079,283 cells, harmonized into fine- and coarse-grained taxonomies. CellPath-Bench samples frozen WSI feature maps at registered nuclear coordinates and evaluates them using standardized multiclass linear probes. Cell Representation Advantage (CRA) measures the within-section advantage of nucleus-anchored representations over patch-level mean pooling, while Cell Representation Transferability (CRT) characterizes the generalization of cell-type decodability across tissue sections, datasets, and organs. We benchmark 30 pathology-specific and general-purpose foundation models through 304,920 runs across spatial readouts, magnifications, taxonomic granularities, and evaluation protocols. The results reveal substantial model-dependent differences in cell-type decodability and its cross-domain generalization, yielding distinct multidimensional capability profiles. CellPath-Bench provides a standardized framework for auditing cellular information in frozen PFM representations.

</details>


### [184] [Can Legal AI Know When It Is Wrong? And Do Students Know When It Is?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21089)
*Angel Mary John,Vipin Kumar Singh,Jerrin Thomas Panachakel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates risks of integrating LLMs into the Indian judiciary, identifying an "inertia of confidence" phenomenon where LLMs provide incorrect legal verdicts with high certainty. Phase I tested three LLMs on 60 Indian Contract Act cases, finding high error rates (Meta AI: 31.7%, Perplexity: 15.0%, ChatGPT: 6.7%). Phase II surveyed 380 law students, revealing reactive verification habits and insufficient AI ethics training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The integration of LLMs into legal practice promises improved access to justice but poses severe risks due to AI hallucinations. The researchers identified a critical gap: LLMs often produce incorrect legal verdicts with high confidence, potentially misleading legal professionals. This "inertia of confidence" phenomenon, combined with "precedent overfitting" bias, creates dangerous risks in judicial contexts.

Method: Phase I: Benchmarking three LLMs (ChatGPT GPT-5.2, Meta AI, Perplexity AI) on a 60-case battery regarding the Indian Contract Act, 1872 and statutory enforcement of specific performance. Introduced High-Confidence Error Rate (HCER) metric to quantify incorrect verdicts delivered with confidence >= 9/10. Phase II: Survey of 380 Indian law students to assess human vulnerability to AI overconfidence, including verification behaviors and knowledge of ethical AI use.

Result: Phase I: All models struggled with statutory updates. Meta AI was most vulnerable (31.7% HCER, 9.1/10 mean confidence), followed by Perplexity (15.0%) and ChatGPT (6.7%). Phase II: Students encountering fabricated citations showed reactive adaptation with higher verification scores (4.2/5 vs 2.8/5). While 81.6% knew about contempt-of-court risks, 71.1% received no formal training on ethical AI use.

Conclusion: The study reveals systemic vulnerabilities in LLM-generated legal content and inadequate preparation of law students for AI-assisted legal work. The researchers propose shifting toward adversarial legal research pedagogy and implementing source-grounded verification architectures to prevent professional negligence. The reactive nature of current verification practices highlights the need for proactive AI ethics education in legal curricula.

Abstract: Integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) into the Indian judiciary promises access to justice but introduces severe risks. We identify the 'inertia of confidence'--an overconfidence phenomenon analogous to the Dunning-Kruger effect where LLMs provide incorrect legal verdicts with near-maximum confidence, driven by a hypothesized 'precedent overfitting' bias. Phase I of our socio-technical audit tested ChatGPT (GPT-5.2), Meta AI, and Perplexity AI on a 60-case battery regarding the Indian Contract Act, 1872, and the shift toward statutory enforcement of specific performance. We introduce the High-Confidence Error Rate (HCER) to quantify incorrect verdicts delivered with dangerous certainty (>= 9 on a 1-10 scale). All models struggled with statutory updates. Meta AI proved most vulnerable (31.7% HCER), frequently misapplying pre-amendment rules with a 9.1/10 mean confidence, followed by Perplexity (15.0%) and ChatGPT (6.7%).
  Phase II investigated human vulnerability to this overconfidence via a survey of Indian law students (N=380). Verification often functions as a reactive adaptation to machine hallucinations: students encountering fabricated citations reported higher verification scores (4.2/5) than those with no such encounters (2.8/5). Furthermore, while 81.6% knew submitting hallucinated cases can lead to contempt-of-court, 71.1% received no formal training on ethical AI use. We propose shifting toward adversarial legal research pedagogy and implementing source-grounded verification architectures to prevent systemic professional negligence.

</details>


### [185] [Personalized Privacy Control in LLMs via Attention Head Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21209)
*Junseok Kim,Nakyeong Yang,Kyomin Jung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces personalized privacy to address user-specific disclosure preferences in LLM-based AI systems, and proposes Repair, an inference-time attention head intervention method to improve policy adherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rise of agentic AI enables LLMs to access diverse user data, raising critical privacy concerns. While prior work on contextual privacy studies whether LLMs regulate information disclosure according to context-dependent norms, acceptable disclosure boundaries may vary across users even within the same context. Existing approaches fail to incorporate user-specific preferences.

Method: The paper introduces P3Bench (Personalized Privacy Preservation Benchmark), which extends contextual privacy policies with personalized disclosure policies. It then proposes Repair, a robust inference-time attention head intervention method that adjusts disclosure behavior toward policy-consistent responses by intervening on attention heads.

Result: Experiments show that prompt-based policies fail to reliably enforce personalized privacy policies. Qwen2.5-7B and Gemma3-4B show average policy ignorance ratios of 51.25% and 74.28% respectively, indicating significant failures to follow personalized privacy policies. The proposed Repair method significantly improves adherence to user-specific privacy preferences by reducing cases where the model fails to follow the given policy.

Conclusion: Personalized privacy is a promising direction for privacy control in AI systems. The Repair method provides an effective inference-time intervention approach that significantly improves LLM compliance with user-specific privacy preferences, addressing the limitations of prompt-based policy enforcement.

Abstract: The rise of agentic AI enables LLMs to access diverse user data, raising critical privacy concerns. Prior work on contextual privacy studies whether LLMs regulate information disclosure according to context-dependent norms. However, acceptable disclosure boundaries may vary across users even within the same context. To address this limitation, we introduce \textit{personalized privacy}, which incorporates user-specific disclosure preferences into privacy control. We further present P3Bench~(\textbf{P}ersonalized \textbf{P}rivacy \textbf{P}reservation \textbf{Bench}mark), a novel benchmark extending contextual privacy policies with personalized disclosure policies. Experiments show that prompt-based policies fail to reliably enforce personalized privacy policies, with Qwen2.5-7B and Gemma3-4B showing average policy ignorance ratios of 51.25\% and 74.28\%, respectively. Finally, to address this problem, we propose \textsc{Repair}, a robust inference-time attention head intervention method that adjusts disclosure behavior toward policy-consistent responses. Our method significantly improves adherence to user-specific privacy preferences by reducing cases where the model fails to follow the given policy.

</details>

Abstract: LLM-as-Judge systems can produce multi-dimensional evaluations, such as trustworthiness, reliability, and factuality, and these outputs are often interpreted as independent evidence. We test this assumption for a common pair of judgments: trust scoring and binary truth classification. On correctness-controlled QA, LLM judges align trust scores with truth verdicts more tightly than human behavioral reference, suggesting weaker separations between trust and truth judgment. We then apply stress tests by changing only source cues of identical QA between Human and AI. Source attribution shifts not only trust scores but also truth verdicts and logit-derived correct-side probabilities. Results show that current LLM-as-Judge protocols should not treat trust scores as independent evidence for truth judgments.

</details>


### [187] [ReFrame: Evidence-Guided Test-Time Safety Alignment in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21100)
*Wenzheng Jiang,Xuankun Rong,Yuanzhao Zhai,Dawei Feng,Huaimin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReFrame is a training-free framework that enhances safety alignment in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by using two agents—a lightweight MLLM—to reframe multimodal inputs before passing them to the downstream model, addressing challenges like cross-modal jailbreaks, safety-awareness failures, and over-sensitive refusals without requiring retraining or internal model access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) extend capabilities beyond text but face increasing safety alignment challenges. Existing methods require retraining or internal-state inspection, making them inapplicable to deployed closed-source MLLMs. This limitation motivates the need for test-time safety alignment approaches.

Method: ReFrame employs two agents sharing a lightweight locally deployed MLLM: (1) Evidence-generation agent constructs complementary risk and utility evidence; (2) Rewrite-and-routing agent converts this evidence into a safe proxy prompt and image-routing decision before calling the downstream MLLM, without modifying it or accessing its internal information.

Result: Experiments across multiple MLLMs and benchmarks show that ReFrame improves jailbreak defense, safety awareness, and oversensitivity reduction while preserving multimodal utility.

Conclusion: ReFrame provides an effective training-free solution for multimodal safety alignment that works with closed-source MLLMs by reframing inputs at test time, successfully balancing safety and utility across various benchmarks.

Abstract: While multimodal large language models (MLLMs) extend model capabilities beyond text, they also make safety alignment increasingly challenging. Multimodal safety alignment methods must address cross-modal jailbreaks, safety-awareness failures, and over-sensitive refusals. However, existing methods often rely on retraining or internal-state inspection, limiting their applicability to deployed closed-source MLLMs and motivating test-time safety alignment. We analyze this setting and identify two key obstacles, utility dominance and reasoning inertia, which cause models to overlook latent risks or follow malicious reasoning trajectories. Guided by these insights, we propose ReFrame, a training-free multimodal input reframing framework where two agents share a lightweight locally deployed MLLM: the evidence-generation agent constructs complementary risk and utility evidence, and the rewrite-and-routing agent converts it into a safe proxy prompt and image-routing decision before calling the downstream MLLM, without modifying it or accessing its internal information. Experiments across multiple MLLMs and benchmarks show that ReFrame improves jailbreak defense, safety awareness, and oversensitivity reduction while preserving multimodal utility.

</details>


### [188] [Root cause analysis via difference graph discovery from linear time-series data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21117)
*Anouk Ruer,Timothée Loranchet,Daria Bystrova,Charles K. Assaad*

Abstract: Root cause analysis aims to identify the mechanisms responsible for anomalies in complex dynamical systems. In this paper, we study root cause analysis in linear time-series through the lens of difference graph discovery. We focus on effect-defying root causes, corresponding to variables whose causal coefficients change between a normal and an anomalous regime. We formalize this problem using linear discrete-time dynamic structural causal models and adapt several methods originally introduced for discovering difference graphs between two populations to the time-series setting, where the two populations are replaced by a normal and an anomalous regime. We first evaluate the proposed approaches on simulated data, and then demonstrate their practical relevance on real-world datasets from IT monitoring and intensive care monitoring. Our results show how difference graph discovery can help localize causal mechanisms responsible for anomalous behavior.

</details>


### [189] [From Attention Masks to Inert Zero-Vector Tokens: OAttention and O-Closure for Token Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21174)
*Heyang Gong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces OAttention, a novel attention mechanism that uses active-presence coefficients (derived from token hidden states) to control token participation in attention operations. It provides exact NULL state handling and an OTransformer architecture with closure properties, achieving marginal improvements (+0.088% to +0.177% RMSE) in a TabPFN retrofit experiment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard attention masks only provide relation-level controls without representation-carried token states for non-participating tokens at attention boundaries. The paper aims to create an attention mechanism where tokens can have explicit presence/absence states that gate information flow and participate in shared computations.

Method: The method assigns each token hidden carrier h_i an active-presence coefficient p_i = ||h_i||²/(τ+||h_i||²). This coefficient serves dual roles: gating information emitted by token i and determining mass for shared token computations. OAttention implements this by gating receiver output by p_i and weighting source j by p_j in both attention numerator and partition, while preserving standard score, visibility relation, exponential competition, and value aggregation.

Result: The canonical operator was validated through contract tests and GPU evaluation. In zero-fine-tuning retrofit experiments on a cloned TabPFN v3 regressor, OAttention variants showed small RMSE increases: +0.088% for OAttention and +0.177% for Full-O across 18 dataset-seed cases. A two-block ablation demonstrated that OAttention alone cannot preserve NULL state through ordinary host components, while the OTransformer path can.

Conclusion: OAttention provides exact NULL state handling with properties including null-receiver, null-source insertion, self-attention insertion, and empty-support. The OTransformer achieves compositional closure through local O-components and the O-Closure law M(H⊕0)=M(H)⊕0. However, these are scoped tests validating exactness, active-path compatibility, and compositional necessity—not universal claims about no-loss, arbitrary-host closure, or general semantics for missing values.

Abstract: Attention masks are relation-level controls: they specify which query--source pairs may interact. They do not provide a representation-carried token state that is non-participating at the attention boundary. We assign each token hidden carrier \(h_i\) an active-presence coefficient \(p_i=\lVert h_i\rVert^2/(τ+\lVert h_i\rVert^2)\). The same coefficient has two roles: it gates information emitted by token \(i\), and it determines the mass with which token \(i\) enters computations shared with other tokens.
  OAttention is the support-coupled attention realization of this rule. It gates the receiver output by \(p_i\) and weights source \(j\) by \(p_j\) in both the attention numerator and partition, while retaining the standard score, visibility relation, exponential competition, and value aggregation. This makes the zero-vector token a zero element and yields exact null-receiver, null-source insertion, self-attention insertion, and empty-support properties. The same token-level presence gives local O-components (OFFN, ONorm, and OInject), presence-weighted OStandardize, the O-Closure law \(M(H\oplus0)=M(H)\oplus0\), and an OTransformer by residual and compositional closure.
  The canonical operator is checked by contract tests and a GPU evaluation. In a zero-fine-tuning retrofit of a cloned pretrained TabPFN v3 regressor, calibrated hidden-carrier OAttention and Full-O variants change mean RMSE by $+0.088\%$ and $+0.177\%$, respectively, over 18 matched dataset--seed cases. A two-block ablation shows that OAttention alone does not preserve a NULL state through ordinary host components, whereas the OTransformer path does. These are scoped tests of exactness, active-path compatibility, and compositional necessity; they do not establish universal no-loss, arbitrary-host closure, learned attraction to the origin, or a general semantics for missing values.

</details>


### [190] [SENTRY: Deterministic, Intelligent Risk Assessment for IT Change Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21203)
*Daniel Arulpragasam,Christer Henrysson,Ella Ly,Deepika Anbalagan,Leo Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SENTRY is a machine learning-based risk assessment platform for technology change management in large financial institutions. It replaces subjective questionnaires with a deterministic pipeline combining XGBoost and hybrid RAG, using structured metadata, application dependency graphs, and historical incident records to detect high-risk changes with 0.87 ROC AUC and 85% accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional self-reported questionnaires for change risk assessment in financial institutions are subjective, easily gamed, and poor at distinguishing routine changes from those that trigger major incidents. There was a need for a more accurate, consistent, and auditable risk assessment method.

Method: The system uses gradient-boosted decision trees (XGBoost) combined with hybrid retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). It integrates structured operational metadata, application dependency graphs, and historical incident records with hybrid semantic and lexical search over historical change requests. The retrieval step compresses unstructured change request text into a single scalar feature before model inference, maintaining determinism and explainability via SHAP values.

Result: SENTRY achieves a ROC AUC of 0.87 and 85% overall accuracy. It detects high-risk changes at approximately 3.25 times the rate of the existing questionnaire-based process.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates a viable alternative to questionnaire-based risk assessment in regulated financial environments. The architectural design balances performance with determinism and explainability, providing insights into the use of machine learning in regulated change management contexts.

Abstract: Technology change management in large financial institutions depends on risk assessments that are accurate, consistent, and auditable. In practice, many institutions still rely on self-reported questionnaires. Those questionnaires are subjective, easy to game, and poor at separating routine changes from the ones that later trigger major incidents. This paper presents SENTRY, a risk assessment platform that replaces questionnaire-based scoring with a deterministic machine learning pipeline built from gradient-boosted decision trees (XGBoost) and hybrid retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). The system combines structured operational metadata, application dependency graphs, and historical incident records with a hybrid semantic and lexical search over historical change requests. The retrieval step captures the risk signal in unstructured change request text, then compresses that signal into a single scalar feature before model inference. That design keeps the model deterministic and preserves per-prediction explainability via SHAP values. Evaluated on enterprise-scale change data, SENTRY achieves a ROC AUC of 0.87 and 85% overall accuracy, and it detects high-risk changes at roughly 3.25 times the rate of the existing process. We close by examining the architectural trade-offs behind this design and what they imply for the use of machine learning in regulated change management.

</details>


### [191] [Enhancing LLMs in Predictive Political QA with Semi-Structured Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21218)
*Yinan Liu,Zihan Zhou,Zichun Jin,Xinyu Wang,Bin Wang,Xiaochun Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PSL is a dual-view framework for predictive political question answering that extracts stance signals from actor records (semantic view) and learns structure-aware actor representations from interaction graphs (vector view), outperforming baselines on three datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM augmentation methods treat external political resources as knowledge-based evidence but under-model prediction-relevant signals. The authors identify two complementary signals: actor stances (issue-specific preferences) and high-order structure signals (indirect dependencies among political actors).

Method: PSL converts semi-structured political records into inference-oriented evidence via two views: (1) Semantic view extracts stance signals from question-relevant actor records; (2) Vector view learns structure-aware actor representations from an actor interaction graph.

Result: PSL consistently outperforms baselines across three real-world datasets and multiple LLMs. Ablations confirm the complementary gains of both stance and structure signals.

Conclusion: The dual-view approach effectively captures both issue-specific preferences and indirect dependencies among political actors, demonstrating the value of modeling prediction-relevant signals rather than treating external resources merely as knowledge evidence.

Abstract: Predictive political question answering (QA), such as predicting how a political actor will vote, goes beyond factual lookup. External political resources offer rich historical evidence, but rarely contain the answer itself. Existing LLM augmentation methods, including actor-profile-based simulation and knowledge graph evidence injection, improve political reasoning but largely treat external resources as knowledge-based evidence, leaving prediction-relevant signals under-modeled. We identify two complementary signals for predictive political QA: actor stances that capture issue-specific preferences, and high-order structure signals that capture indirect dependencies among political actors. We propose PSL, a dual-view framework that converts semi-structured political records into inference-oriented evidence for LLMs. PSL extracts stance signals from question-relevant actor records in a semantic view, and learns structure-aware actor representations from an actor interaction graph in a vector view. Across three real-world datasets and multiple LLMs, PSL consistently outperforms baselines, with ablations confirming the complementary gains of stance and structure signals.

</details>


### [192] [Ontology-supported AI Model and Dataset Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21224)
*Jan Novacek,Ali Ahari,Tobias Müller,Sebastian Reiter,Alexander Viehl,Oliver Bringmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed

Abstract: Recently, there has been a great deal of research into improving AI methods and their application. The main focus is on tracking progress, enabling transparent comparisons, and fostering a more profound understanding of AI. In that process, different organizations generate and use plenty of assets that need to be tracked, traced and managed. Moreover, it is important to discover assets relevant for the task at hand. This paper presents research aiming to contribute to answering the question of what is required to exchange and manage AI models and related assets effectively without semantic gaps in an industrial context. We introduce a platform for AI model exchange, which facilitates the usage, exchange, and analysis of AI models and datasets. The platform incorporates an ontology that can foster a more profound common understanding of what is required in these tasks and help tackle the issues mentioned above. Finally, we elucidate the utility of the platform through the illustration of a use case in the context of real-time critical systems.

</details>


### [193] [Fine-Grain GPU Parallelization of the Generalized Partition Crossover for Large-Scale Traveling Salesman Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21233)
*Swetha Varadarajan,Darrell Whitley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a fine-grain GPU implementation of the partition phase of the Generalized Partition Crossover (GPX) operator for solving the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP). The approach reformulates GPX partitioning as a graph-parallel problem using coalesced memory layouts, ghost-node transformations, and connected-component analysis, achieving 48x-625x speedups on instances ranging from 10,000 to 2 million cities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Traveling Salesman Problem is NP-hard and extensively studied. While Genetic Algorithm (GA)-based solvers like Edge Assembly Crossover (EAX) achieve state-of-the-art performance, their scalability on massively parallel architectures is limited due to irregular memory access patterns, graph traversals, and sequential dependencies. Existing GPU-based TSP solvers primarily exploit population-level parallelism and are limited to relatively small problem sizes.

Method: The proposed approach reformulates GPX partitioning as a graph-parallel problem using: (1) coalesced memory layouts, (2) ghost-node transformations, and (3) connected-component analysis. The implementation parallelizes five key operations using CUDA: (1) union of parent tours, (2) splitting of degree-four vertices, (3) deletion of common edges, and (4) identification of recombining components.

Result: Experimental results on instances ranging from 10,000 to 2 million cities demonstrate substantial acceleration over a naive sequential CPU implementation. The proposed GPU partitioning achieves speedups between 48x and 625x while significantly reducing memory overhead.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that operator-level parallelism can substantially improve the scalability of GA-based TSP solvers on modern many-core architectures. This work addresses the limitation of existing GPU-based solvers that are restricted to small problem sizes.

Abstract: The Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) is one of the most extensively studied NP-hard optimization problems. Genetic Algorithm (GA)-based solvers, such as the Edge Assembly Crossover (EAX), achieve state-of-the-art performance on many benchmark instances. However, the scalability of these approaches in massively parallel architectures remains limited because crossover operations involve irregular memory access patterns, graph traversals, and sequential dependencies. Existing GPU-based TSP solvers primarily exploit population-level parallelism and are limited to relatively small problem sizes. This work presents a fine-grain GPU implementation of the partition phase of the Generalized Partition Crossover (GPX) operator for large-scale TSP instances. The proposed approach reformulates GPX partitioning as a graph-parallel problem using coalesced memory layouts, ghost-node transformations, and connected-component analysis. The im- plementation parallelizes the union of parent tours, the splitting of degree- four vertices, the deletion of common edges, and the identification of recombining components using CUDA. Experimental results on instances ranging from 10,000 to 2 million cities demonstrate substantial acceleration over a naive sequential CPU imple- mentation. The proposed GPU partitioning achieves speedups between 48x and 625x while significantly reducing memory overhead. The re- sults demonstrate that operator-level parallelism can substantially im- prove the scalability of GA-based TSP solvers on modern many-core architectures.

</details>


### [194] [CLEAR: Continuous Latent Adapter Routing for Utility-Preserving LLM Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21278)
*Chengxiao Wang,Enyi Jiang,Xiaojing Liao,Sanmi Koyejo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CLEAR (Continuous Latent Adapter Routing) is a conditional safety adaptation framework that uses a lightweight hidden-state gate to control the activation strength of a safety low-rank adapter, aiming to improve LLM safety without sacrificing utility on benign prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The trade-off between safety and utility in LLMs: globally applied safety tuning (like SFT or LoRA) reduces harmful outputs but also degrades performance on benign inputs, highlighting the need for a conditional approach that adapts safety mechanisms only when necessary.

Method: CLEAR introduces a lightweight hidden-state gate that continuously controls the activation strength of a safety low-rank adapter, enabling the model to dynamically adjust safety interventions based on input context while keeping the backbone model frozen.

Result: On Llama-3-8B-Instruct, CLEAR reduced HarmBench ASR from 32.3% to 0.5% while retaining most of the base model's utility and achieving up to 7.1 percentage points higher GSM8K accuracy than globally applied SFT or LoRA.

Conclusion: CLEAR demonstrates a promising mechanism for improving the safety-utility trade-off in LLM alignment by selectively applying safety interventions through a conditional gating mechanism, effectively reducing harmful completions with minimal utility degradation.

Abstract: Improving the safety of large language models (LLMs) often comes at the expense of utility, as globally applied safety tuning may affect model responses to both harmful and benign inputs. We propose \textbf{C}ontinuous \textbf{L}at\textbf{E}nt \textbf{A}dapter \textbf{R}outing (CLEAR), a conditional safety adaptation framework that uses a lightweight hidden-state gate to continuously control the activation strength of a safety low-rank adapter. CLEAR aims to reduce harmful completions while avoiding unnecessary changes to the frozen backbone that could degrade performance on benign prompts. Experiments on widely used safety and utility benchmarks show that CLEAR improves robustness on HarmBench while reducing the utility degradation observed with globally applied safety tuning such as SFT or standard low-rank adaptation (LoRA). On Llama-3-8B-Instruct, CLEAR reduces HarmBench ASR from 32.3\% to 0.5\%, while retaining most of the base model's utility and achieving up to 7.1 percentage points higher GSM8K accuracy than globally applied SFT or LoRA. These results suggest that CLEAR is a promising mechanism for improving the safety--utility trade-off in LLM alignment.

</details>


### [195] [AUSO: Action-Level Unified Skill Optimization from Internalization to Utilization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21292)
*Huizu Lin,Chengkai Huang,Tianqi Gao,Tao Huang,Daijiao Liu,Tongxin Li,Xiaoyan Sun,Lina Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AUSO is a novel reinforcement learning framework that enables skills to progress through a lifecycle - from external guidance to internalized decision knowledge - by using action-level evaluation to determine when skills should be invoked. It unifies skill learning and skill use through progressive optimization, distinguishing between beneficial and harmful skill-sensitive actions at the action level rather than the trajectory level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods fail to model the lifecycle of skills as an agent's policy evolves. They either keep skills external, fully internalize them, or use noisy task-level success rates for selection. These approaches fragment training and treat all actions uniformly within a trajectory, ignoring that skill guidance may help some decisions while distracting others.

Method: AUSO uses a three-stage progressive approach: (1) Initially, jointly learns from teacher guidance and environmental outcomes to acquire foundational skills; (2) Emphasizes outcome-based policy optimization to consolidate autonomous problem-solving; (3) Evaluates each sampled action under both skill-conditioned and skill-free contexts, coupling the action-level information signal with trajectory outcome advantage to selectively strengthen beneficial skill-sensitive actions and suppress harmful ones.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA show AUSO consistently improves agent performance and out-of-distribution generalization over competitive baselines.

Conclusion: AUSO successfully enables skills to transition from external supervision to decision knowledge adapted to action-level benefits, with RL remaining the shared backbone across all stages. This action-level, progressive approach outperforms existing methods in both performance and generalization.

Abstract: Skills play different roles as an agent's policy evolves: they should first provide learnable knowledge, then support capability formation, and finally be invoked only when they improve individual decisions. Existing methods rarely model this lifecycle. They either keep skills outside the model, fully internalize them, or select among internalization and utilization objectives through noisy task-level success rates. Such designs fragment training and assign uniform importance to actions within the same trajectory, even though skill guidance may help some decisions while distracting others. To solve these problems, we introduce AUSO (Action-level Unified Skill Optimization), which unifies skill learning and skill use through a progressive, action-aware optimization process. At the beginning of training, AUSO jointly learns from teacher guidance and environmental outcomes, enabling the policy to acquire foundational skills without losing task-oriented feedback. It subsequently emphasizes outcome-based policy optimization to consolidate autonomous problem-solving ability. As the policy matures, AUSO evaluates each sampled action under both skill-conditioned and skill-free contexts. The resulting action-level information signal is coupled with the trajectory outcome advantage, allowing beneficial skill-sensitive actions to receive stronger updates and harmful ones to be suppressed. Therefore, skills gradually transition from an external source of supervision into decision knowledge whose utilization is adapted to its action-level benefit, while reinforcement learning remains the shared backbone across all stages. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA show that AUSO consistently improves agent performance and out-of-distribution generalization over competitive baselines.

</details>


### [196] [From Regulation to Implementation: A Critical Evaluation of LLM-Assisted Regulatory Compliance in Industry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21317)
*Adriana Watson,Marco Bücheler,Grant Richards*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: The European Union (EU) has emerged as a leading regulatory body in the development of sustainability and privacy regulations. While new regulation requirements vary, many include a documentation artifact to ensure compliance. Notably, the Ecodesign for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR) introduces Digital Product Passports (DPPs) for life cycle transparency, while the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) mandates Data Protection Impact Assessments (DPIAs) to mitigate privacy risks. Creating these compliance artifacts, however, is challenging. Industrial data, which often exists in heterogeneous formats and is scattered across company and supplier systems, is required for DPPs and can be difficult to extract into compliant DPP formatting. Furthermore, DPIA documents require interdisciplinary expertise and follow no standardized format, making development difficult for novel systems. To address the particular complexity of compliance artifact creation for both regulations, researchers have proposed the use of LLMs in the generation process; however, the impact of the aforementioned problems on the output of these systems is largely unaddressed. This work investigates the existing research gap by exploring how data extraction instructions and regulatory vagueness impact the quality and consistency of LLM-produced compliance artifacts. The resulting artifacts are evaluated by benchmarking different models against manually created ground-truth schemas. The results reveal that less strict guidelines, such as DPIA formatting, require higher context prompts to maintain consistency and completeness. Stricter guidelines, such as formatting for Digital Battery Passports (DBP), result in consistent results regardless of prompt context, but may lead to more hallucinations in the output

</details>


### [197] [Unified Branch-and-Bound Search for the Steiner Traveling Salesman Problem on Graphs of Convex Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21319)
*Jingtao Tang,Hang Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formalizes and solves the Steiner Traveling Salesman Problem on Graphs of Convex Sets (GCS), which seeks minimum-cost closed trajectories through required convex sets with optional transit vertices and revisits. The method combines branch-and-bound search with two lower-bounding techniques and demonstrates applicability on a mobile-manipulator inspection task with LTL_f constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Steiner-TSP on GCS extends classical TSP to continuous domains with convex set constraints, requiring simultaneous optimization of visitation order and continuous trajectories. This arises in robotic inspection, coverage planning, and motion planning where tasks involve visiting regions (convex sets) rather than discrete points.

Method: The authors propose: (1) A unified branch-and-bound search over rooted walk prefixes exploring the infinite solution space; (2) Additive lower-bound-graph costs for bounding committed prefixes; (3) A cut-separated connected-flow relaxation to lower-bound residual costs of visiting remaining targets and returning to root. For ε≥1, a global lower bound certifies approximation quality. Both best-first and depth-first traversal strategies are analyzed.

Result: Both traversal strategies find feasible solutions on all benchmark instances within 30 seconds. Mean certified optimality gaps are 28.1% (best-first) and 29.7% (depth-first). Two recent baselines succeed on only about half of the instances. The method also handles joint sensing-mode, visitation-order, and continuous-trajectory selection with LTL_f action precedences.

Conclusion: The branch-and-bound approach with dual lower-bounding techniques provides a practical solution to the Steiner-TSP on GCS, outperforming recent baselines in terms of success rate. The finite-termination guarantees under positive-cost assumptions and ε-approximation certification provide theoretical rigor. Future work could focus on reducing the optimality gap and extending to more complex constraint types.

Abstract: We formalize the Steiner Traveling Salesman Problem (Steiner-TSP) on Graphs of Convex Sets (GCS), which seeks a minimum-cost closed trajectory through required convex sets while allowing optional transit vertices and revisits. To explore the resulting infinite solution space, we propose a unified branch-and-bound search over rooted walk prefixes. Additive lower-bound-graph costs bound committed prefixes, while a cut-separated connected-flow relaxation lower-bounds the residual cost of visiting every remaining target and returning to the root. Under a uniform positive-cost assumption, best-first traversal terminates after finitely many expansions on every feasible instance without an initial incumbent, whereas depth-first traversal does so once a finite incumbent is available. For a user-specified factor $ε\geq1$, a global lower bound certifies that either strategy's incumbent cost is at most $ε$ times the global optimum. We further demonstrate joint sensing-mode, visitation-order, and continuous-trajectory selection for a mobile-manipulator inspection task, including action precedences expressed in linear temporal logic over finite traces (LTL$_f$). Both traversal strategies find feasible solutions on all benchmark instances within 30s with mean certified optimality gaps of 28.1% and 29.7%, respectively, whereas two recent baselines succeed on only about half of the instances

</details>


### [198] [Anatomy-Informed Neural Networks: Encoding Anatomic Priors in Loss and Architecture, with an SE(3) Formulation of Guidewire-Induced Aortoiliac Deformation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21332)
*David P. Stonko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Anatomy-Informed Neural Networks (AINN), which incorporate anatomical priors into deep learning for medical imaging. Soft priors (e.g., branching penalties) are added as loss penalties, while hard priors (e.g., vessel continuity) are built into the architecture. The method is demonstrated for predicting aortoiliac tree deformation during endovascular wire insertion, using SE(3) Lie group representations, Cosserat-rod mechanics, and Wasserstein-2 optimal transport loss for 2D-to-3D supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models for anatomical prediction can produce numerically plausible but anatomically impossible results, and they generalize poorly when training data is scarce. This limits their clinical applicability, particularly in specialized domains like endovascular surgery where data is inherently limited.

Method: The approach uses: (1) Lifting vessel centerline and wire path from R^3 to SE(3) Lie group frames; (2) Coupling a Cosserat-rod wire model to a tortuosity-modulated vessel through unilateral lumen-contact inequality; (3) Constrained minimization of coupled elastic energy with contact forces as Lagrange multipliers; (4) Wasserstein-2 optimal transport loss between predicted C-arm projections and observed angiograms for 2D-to-3D training.

Result: The kinematics, loss function, and projection model were verified against known ground truth. The mechanics solver was verified against its own optimality conditions. Mesh convergence for predicted displacement was not yet achieved. Currently, no neural network has been trained.

Conclusion: AINN provides a promising framework for anatomically constrained deep learning in medical imaging. Future work will transfer this in silico model to real CT scans and evaluate whether it improves predictive accuracy while reducing required training data. The approach has potential applications in autonomous endovascular navigation.

Abstract: Deep-learning models of anatomy can be numerically plausible yet anatomically impossible, and they generalize poorly when data are scarce. We introduce Anatomy-Informed Neural Networks (AINN), in which soft anatomic priors enter as penalty terms in the loss (e.g., a branching penalty that treats a renal transplant artery off the iliac instead of the aorta as unexpected rather than impossible), in direct analogy to a physics-informed neural network, and hard anatomic priors (e.g., continuity of the vessel) are built into the architecture and state representation, making such invalid predictions impossible by construction wherever the prior admits architectural enforcement.
  We develop it on a clinical test case with limited data: how the aortoiliac tree deforms when a stiff wire is introduced endoluminally. This is important to contemporary aortic surgery and will matter to autonomous endovascular navigation. We lift the vessel centerline and the wire path from R^3 to curves of frames in the Lie group SE(3), and couple a Cosserat-rod wire to a tortuosity-modulated, anatomically anchored vessel through a unilateral lumen-contact inequality. The prediction is a constrained minimizer of the coupled elastic energy, with contact forces as its Lagrange multipliers. Supervision is a Wasserstein-2 optimal-transport loss between the predicted projection through the C-arm geometry and the observed angiogram, so a 2D angiogram can train a 3D prediction. The kinematics, loss and projection are verified against known ground truth; the mechanics solver only against its own optimality conditions, and predicted displacement is not yet mesh-converged. Here, no network is trained. Future work will transfer this in silico model to real CT scans and test whether it improves predictive accuracy and reduces the training data required.

</details>


### [199] [VIALS: A Benchmark for Visual Interpretation of Artifacts in the Life Sciences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21357)
*Elaine Lau,Thanuka Udumulla,Lee Izhaki-Tavor,Francisco Guzmán,Nicholas Magazine,Jonas Mueller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VIALS is a visual question-answering benchmark with 161 tasks focused on interpreting scientific visual artifacts (gel blots, microscopy images, plasmid maps, etc.) that scientists encounter in professional biotech workflows. The study finds that current frontier vision-language models fail at these tasks despite performing well on natural images, highlighting a significant gap in AI's domain-specific visual reasoning capabilities for life sciences applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientists in professional life sciences workflows routinely interpret visual artifacts to make research decisions. However, current AI vision-language models lack the domain knowledge and visual reasoning capabilities to handle these specialized scientific images, limiting their utility in real-world biotech settings where such artifacts are central to scientific reasoning and decision-making.

Method: The researchers created VIALS, a benchmark consisting of 161 visual interpretation tasks spanning various artifact types encountered throughout experimental workflows in the biotech industry. They tested frontier vision-language models on these tasks and compared their performance against scientists with relevant domain expertise.

Result: Frontier vision-language models were unable to accurately interpret scientific visual artifacts, performing significantly worse than domain experts. While these models can describe natural images fluently, they lack the domain-specific knowledge and visual reasoning capabilities required for scientific image interpretation.

Conclusion: Current AI systems have limited utility in professional life sciences workflows because they cannot interpret the visual artifacts that are central to how scientists reason, communicate, and make decisions. Developing AI that can accurately interpret scientific images requires advances in both domain knowledge and domain-specific visual reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: In professional life sciences workflows, scientists routinely interpret visual artifacts (gel blots, microscopy images, plasmid maps, flow cytometry plots, molecular structures, ...) to inform research decisions. We introduce VIALS, a visual question-answering benchmark with 161 such interpretation tasks, spanning the types of artifacts examined throughout experimental workflows in the biotech industry (rather than polished figures from publications and textbooks). While frontier vision-language models can now fluently describe natural images, we find that they are unable to accurately interpret these scientific images, reflecting limitations in domain knowledge and domain-specific visual reasoning capabilities. In contrast, scientists with relevant domain expertise find these visual interpretation tasks straightforward. AI that cannot similarly interpret such images will have limited utility in professional life sciences workflows, where such artifacts are central to how scientists reason, communicate, and make decisions.

</details>

### [200] [Bankruptcy Prediction via Hybrid Resampling and Stacking Ensemble Techniques with Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI)-Driven Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20343)
*Obu-Amoah Ampomah,Edmund Fosu Agyemang,Kofi Acheampong,Louis Agyekum,Enock Adu Bonsu,Eric Nyarko*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: This study develops and evaluates a bankruptcy prediction framework that integrates consensus-based feature selection, hybrid resampling, stacking ensembles, and explainable artificial intelligence to improve minority-class detection in severely imbalanced financial data. Using the Taiwanese Bankruptcy Prediction dataset from the UCI Machine Learning Repository, five feature-selection algorithms were first applied, and a consensus retention rule reduced the input space to 23 robust variables. The balanced training data were then generated using SVM-SMOTE, SMOTE-Tomek, and SMOTE-ENN. Five ensemble machine learning classifiers, namely gradient boosting, extreme gradient boosting, histogram-based gradient boosting, LightGBM, and AdaBoost, were compared with five deep learning models, including RNN, LSTM, GRU, DNN, and MLP. In addition, hybrid stacking ensembles combined the five machine learning classifiers as base learners with each deep learning model as a meta-learner. Model performance was assessed using accuracy, recall, specificity, G-mean, and ROC-AUC, while SHAP was used to explain feature contributions. The results show that resampling strategy materially shaped model behavior. SVM-SMOTE and SMOTE-Tomek favored accuracy and specificity, whereas SMOTE-ENN delivered stronger minority-class detection. Among standalone models, the GRU with SMOTE-ENN achieved the best overall predictive balance, with recall of 0.8627, G-mean of 0.8517, and ROC-AUC of 0.9431. Among stacking ensembles, SMOTE-ENN with (GB+XGB+HGB+LGBM+AB)+LSTM provided the strongest compromise between sensitivity and specificity. SHAP analysis identified leverage, profitability, solvency, and operational efficiency indicators as the most influential predictors of bankruptcy risk. These findings support more reliable and interpretable early warning systems for financially distressed firms.

</details>


### [201] [Machine Learning and ARIMA Model Averaging for Adaptive Public Health Forecasting: Comparative Evaluation and an Ontario COVID-19 Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20406)
*Yushu Zou,Ye Li,Johra Moosa,Martin Grunnill,Samir N. Patel,Venkata R. Duvvuri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares forecasting models (ARIMA, Random Forest, XGBoost) and a novel ensemble method (MLAMA) for predicting Ontario COVID-19 case counts. Using 190 weekly observations, the study evaluates model performance across different forecast horizons (1-6 weeks), responsiveness after turning points, and training data length. MLAMA achieved the lowest normalized error across most conditions, supporting the need for context-dependent model selection rather than a universal approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public health forecasts must balance responsiveness to abrupt changes while avoiding over-extrapolation of noise, artifacts, or temporary trends. There is a need to identify which forecasting methods perform best under different operating conditions (short vs. long horizons, turning points vs. stable periods).

Method: The study used 190 weekly observations of Ontario COVID-19 case counts (January 2020–October 2023). Three models were evaluated: ARIMA, Random Forest, and XGBoost. Rolling-origin time-series cross-validation preserved temporal order. Performance was assessed across three dimensions: responsiveness after turning points, forecast horizons of 1–6 weeks, and amount of historical training data. A novel ensemble called MLAMA (Machine Learning and ARIMA Model Averaging) was developed—a non-negative performance-weighted ensemble with weights varying by forecast horizon and responsiveness setting.

Result: ARIMA adapted rapidly after turning points but showed increased normalized error at longer horizons. Random Forest and XGBoost were less responsive initially but maintained more stable error over longer horizons. For two-week forecasts at the end of the study period, using the most recent training data outperformed longer historical periods, especially for XGBoost. MLAMA achieved the lowest normalized mean absolute percentage error across most forecast horizons and ranked among the best across responsiveness settings.

Conclusion: The findings support selecting forecasting models according to operating conditions rather than relying on a single universally preferred approach. MLAMA provides a practical framework for combining complementary statistical and machine-learning forecasts. The accompanying Python package is under validation.

Abstract: Public health forecasts must respond to abrupt changes in surveillance data without over-extrapolating noise, reporting artifacts, or temporary trends. We evaluated autoregressive integrated moving average (ARIMA), random forest, and extreme gradient boosting (XGBoost) models using 190 weekly observations of publicly available Ontario COVID-19 case counts from January 2020 to October 2023. Rolling-origin time-series cross-validation preserved temporal order during model tuning and evaluation. Performance was assessed across three operating dimensions: responsiveness following selected turning points, forecast horizons of one to six weeks, and the amount of historical training data. We also developed Machine Learning and ARIMA Model Averaging (MLAMA), a non-negative performance-weighted ensemble with weights that vary by forecast horizon and responsiveness setting. Retrospective comparisons showed that ARIMA adapted rapidly after turning points but its normalized error increased at longer horizons. Random forest and XGBoost were less responsive initially but maintained more stable normalized error over longer horizons. For two-week forecasts at the end of the study period, training on the most recent data outperformed using longer historical periods, particularly for XGBoost. MLAMA achieved the lowest normalized mean absolute percentage error across most forecast horizons and ranked among the best-performing methods across responsiveness settings. These findings support selecting forecasting models according to operating conditions rather than relying on a single universally preferred approach. MLAMA provides a practical framework for combining complementary statistical and machine-learning forecasts. The accompanying Python package is currently maintained in a private repository while software validation and reproducibility testing are completed.

</details>


### [202] [From Thermal Preference Prediction to Adaptive Thermal Intervention: A Reinforcement Learning Approach Using Physiological and Environmental Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20423)
*Isibor Kennedy Ihianle,Emmanuel Manu,Ehsan Asnaashari,Mojgan Jadidi,Pedro Machado,Amrit Sagoo,Ahmad Lotfi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a two-stage personalized thermal comfort approach that combines multimodal physiological and environmental sensing with reinforcement learning-based decision-making to address limitations of conventional HVAC systems that rely on static setpoints and population-level comfort models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional HVAC systems use static setpoints and population-level comfort models that fail to capture individual physiological variability, leading to reduced occupant comfort and wellbeing. There is a need for more responsive building-control strategies that can adapt to individual occupants.

Method: The proposed approach integrates two key components: (1) multimodal physiological and environmental sensing to capture individual-level data, and (2) reinforcement learning-based decision-making to optimize thermal comfort control based on the sensed data.

Result: The abstract presents a methodology framework rather than specific results, demonstrating a novel two-stage approach for personalized thermal comfort optimization.

Conclusion: The integration of multimodal sensing with reinforcement learning offers a promising direction for developing more responsive and personalized building HVAC control strategies that can better support occupant wellbeing.

Abstract: Personalised thermal comfort is essential for occupant wellbeing and for the development of more responsive building-control strategies, yet conventional Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning (HVAC) systems rely on static setpoints and population-level comfort models that fail to capture individual physiological variability. This paper presents a two-stage personalised thermal comfort approach integrating multimodal physiological and environmental sensing with reinforcement learning-based decision-making.

</details>


### [203] [BF1: A Causal Dyadic Sparse-Attention Retrofit for Efficient Long-Context Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20427)
*Hina Dixit*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BF1 is a deterministic block-aligned sparse attention mechanism that achieves 10.91x speedup at 32K tokens while maintaining language modeling quality, enabling selective retrofitting of pretrained models with real long-context systems value.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense causal attention remains expensive at long context even with optimized exact kernels, necessitating efficient sparse attention alternatives that can be retrofitted into pretrained models without full retraining.

Method: BF1 combines a small exact local neighborhood, a global first block, and logarithmically spaced historical blocks. The approach includes: (1) correctness-gated pretrained-model retrofit methodology, (2) matched topology-control study, and (3) systems characterization connecting per-layer sparsity to whole-model latency. Implementation uses optimized BF16 kernels on NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU. Evaluation includes numerical correctness, selected-interaction scaling, kernel performance, partial-model inference, and next-token language modeling under a matched 1,000-step, 16.384M-token adaptation protocol.

Result: BF1 achieves 2-4x dense crossover on 2K-4K tokens and 10.91x per-layer prefill speedup at 32K tokens. Retrofitting 8 of 28 Qwen3-0.6B attention layers reduces warm whole-model time-to-first-token by 7.7%, 11.3%, and 15.3% at 8K, 16K, and 32K tokens respectively. In training evaluation, BF1 achieves mean perplexity of 1.68639, outperforming static-random graph (1.69154), dense continued training (1.69258), and local sliding (1.81505). At seed 1234, Dense-CT is 0.3169-0.4055% above BF1 and static-random is 0.2441-0.3642% above BF1.

Conclusion: BF1 establishes itself as a reproducible sparse operator and selective retrofit primitive with real long-context systems value, demonstrating that block-aligned dyadic sparse attention can significantly accelerate inference while maintaining or improving language modeling quality through matched adaptation protocols.

Abstract: Dense causal attention remains expensive at long context even when implemented with highly optimized exact kernels. We study BF1, a deterministic block-aligned dyadic sparse-attention route that combines a small exact local neighborhood, a global first block, and logarithmically spaced historical blocks. The route is related to prior log-sparse and dilated attention patterns; our contribution is a correctness-gated pretrained-model retrofit, a matched topology-control study, and a systems characterization that connects per-layer sparsity to whole-model latency. For fixed block width, every converted layer uses O(n log n) selected token interactions and has O(log n) graph communication depth. On an NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU, an optimized BF16 implementation crosses dense attention between 2K and 4K tokens and reaches a 10.91x per-layer prefill speedup at 32K. Retrofitting eight of 28 Qwen3-0.6B attention layers lowers warm whole-model time to first token by 7.7%, 11.3%, and 15.3% at 8K, 16K, and 32K, respectively, while the remaining dense layers keep the complete model asymptotically quadratic. Under a matched 1,000-step, 16.384M-token adaptation protocol, BF1 ranks first across three training seeds: mean report perplexity is 1.68639 versus 1.69154 for a matched static-random nonlocal graph, 1.69258 for dense continued training, and 1.81505 for equal-budget local sliding. At seed 1234, the packed-report paired interval places Dense-CT 0.3169-0.4055% above BF1 and static-random graph 17 0.2441-0.3642% above BF1. These results establish BF1 as a reproducible sparse operator and selective retrofit primitive with real long-context systems value. This paper evaluates numerical correctness, selected-interaction scaling, kernel performance, partial-model inference, and matched next-token language modeling.

</details>


### [204] [Approximate Homomorphisms and Convergent Representations in Transducers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20428)
*Santiago Cifuentes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies the stability of minimal representations of controlled stochastic processes (transducers) under perturbations. It introduces approximate homomorphisms and interface metrics to compare structural similarity between transducer implementations. The key results show that while standard transducers can fail to have approximate homomorphisms under certain interfaces, finite-rank interfaces exhibit stability with error linear in perturbation size. Analogous results hold for predictive transducers under residual metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research is motivated by recent experiments finding predictive-state structure in latent representations of neural networks. The authors aim to provide theoretical support for the hypothesis that latent representations in modern AI models exhibit structural convergence, by examining whether canonical transducer representations are robust to perturbations.

Method: The paper introduces: (1) Approximate homomorphisms capturing local structural similarity between transducers, (2) Metrics comparing induced dynamics (interfaces), (3) Proof of composability of approximate homomorphisms. Methods involve analysis of standard, linear, and predictive transducers under various perturbation scenarios.

Result: Key findings: (1) For standard transducers, there exist simple interfaces with no approximate homomorphism between different implementations, (2) For finite-rank interfaces, all minimal linear transducers implementing interfaces close to I have approximate homomorphism to the minimal implementation of I, with error linear in perturbation size, (3) Analogous stability result for predictive transducers under residual metric with mild hypotheses on belief state indistinguishability.

Conclusion: The results identify conditions under which canonical transducer representations are robust to perturbations. Specifically, convergence holds for finite-rank interfaces and predictive transducers with appropriate structural restrictions, but fails without additional structural restrictions. This provides theoretical support for the hypothesis that latent representations in AI models exhibit structural convergence.

Abstract: We study the stability of minimal representations of controlled stochastic processes (in particular, transducers) under perturbations. This question is motivated by recent experiments finding predictive-state structure in the latent representations of neural networks. We consider standard, linear and predictive transducers. We introduce notions of approximate homomorphism capturing local structural similarity between them, together with metrics comparing their induced dynamics (which we refer to as interfaces), and prove properties such as composability of the approximate homomorphisms. For standard transducers, we show that there exist simple interfaces for which there is no approximate homomorphism between the different implementations of the dynamics. In contrast, for every finite-rank interface $\mathcal I$, we prove that all minimal linear transducers implementing interfaces sufficiently close to $\mathcal I$ have an approximate homomorphism to the minimal implementation of $\mathcal I$, with error linear in the perturbation size. We prove an analogous stability result for predictive transducers under a residual metric using some mild hypothesis regarding the indistinguishability of the belief states. These results identify conditions under which canonical transducer representations are robust to perturbations, while showing that such convergence fails without additional structural restrictions. Under the assumption that these type of abstractions are embedded into the hidden layers of modern AI models, this gives some theoretical support to the hypothesis that their latent representations exhibit structural convergence.

</details>


### [205] [Wrong-Physics Backdoors in Neural PDE Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20439)
*Hanbing Liang,Fujun Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces 'cross-parameter relinking', a data-poisoning attack that embeds a backdoor in neural PDE operators trained on multi-parameter solver archives. The attack causes the model to produce physically plausible but incorrect solutions for a target physical parameter by relinking inputs to alternate-parameter solutions. Evaluated across 476 attack campaigns on Burgers, advection-diffusion, 2D Navier-Stokes, and Poisson equations using FNO, DeepONet, Transformer, GRU, and LSTM models, achieving up to 100% backdoor success rate while maintaining low clean error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural PDE operators are increasingly trained on reusable solver archives, but current validation methods only check clean prediction error and parameter-agnostic plausibility (e.g., smoothness). This leaves a structural validation gap where physically plausible but wrong outputs can pass validation. The authors seek to expose this vulnerability by introducing a novel data-poisoning primitive.

Method: The authors propose 'cross-parameter relinking' (wrong-physics backdoor): a data-poisoning primitive that exploits tensor-to-parameter provenance failures in multi-parameter archives. The attack stamps a surrogate input and relinks its supervision to a cached alternate-parameter solution for the same latent sample. They conduct 476 attack campaigns across multiple PDE families (Burgers, advection-diffusion, 2D Navier-Stokes, elliptic Poisson) using various architectures (FNO, DeepONet, Transformer, GRU, LSTM) and evaluate with clean-label, label-only, and shuffled controls.

Result: FNO achieves 100% backdoor success rate on both advection-diffusion and 2D Navier-Stokes while retaining low clean relative L2 error. The attack moves predictions toward the intended alternate-physics target while preserving bounded clean error. Control experiments show that high attack success alone is insufficient—successful attacks must shift predictions toward the alternate-physics target while maintaining low clean error.

Conclusion: The results expose a structural validation gap in neural PDE operators: smoothness or generic solver-like behavior is insufficient for validation unless the provenance of the intended physical parameter is also verified. The authors demonstrate that current validation practices fail to detect wrong-physics backdoors, highlighting the need for provenance verification in multi-parameter solver archives.

Abstract: Neural PDE operators are increasingly trained on reusable solver archives, yet validation often relies on clean prediction error and parameter-agnostic plausibility checks. We introduce cross-parameter relinking, a data-poisoning primitive that makes a triggered input select a valid solution from the same PDE family under an incorrect physical parameter. We term this a wrong-physics backdoor: the output remains physically plausible but is wrong for the intended parameter. The attack exploits tensor-to-parameter provenance failures in multi-parameter archives by stamping the surrogate input and relinking its supervision to a cached alternate-parameter solution for the same latent sample. Across 476 attack campaigns, we evaluate Burgers, advection-diffusion, two-dimensional Navier-Stokes, and an elliptic Poisson case. Fourier Neural Operators and DeepONet provide the primary evidence, with Transformer, GRU, and LSTM models as support. FNO reaches a backdoor success rate of 1.0000 on both advection-diffusion and two-dimensional Navier-Stokes while retaining low clean relative L2 error. Clean-label, label-only, and shuffled controls show that high attack success alone is insufficient: successful attacks must move predictions toward the intended alternate-physics target while preserving bounded clean error. These results expose a structural validation gap: smoothness or generic solver-like behavior is insufficient unless the provenance of the intended physical parameter is also verified.

</details>


### [206] [Decision Tree and K-Means Analysis of Raman Spectra for Edible Oils: A Physics-Informed AI Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20440)
*Amrita Shaw,Chandrasekar S. N.,Sai Muthukumar V.,Jhinuk Gupta,Deepak L. N. Kallepalli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study develops an integrated Raman spectroscopy and machine-learning framework with Physics-Informed AI (PI-AI) for authenticating edible oils in processed foods. Five edible oils were analyzed in pure form and within fried-potato-chip matrix using clustering, Decision Trees, and NNLS-based spectral decomposition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Authentication of edible oils in processed foods is critical for food quality assurance, fraud prevention, and regulatory compliance. Current methods lack efficiency and interpretability for practical deployment.

Method: Five edible oils were investigated in pure form and within a fried-potato-chip matrix using t-SNE, K-means clustering, Decision Trees, and Non-Negative Least Squares (NNLS)-based spectral decomposition. Physics-Informed AI (PI-AI) was applied to separate oil signatures from matrix contributions.

Result: Decision Trees achieved 100% classification accuracy for pure oils using only 4 Raman variables (0.21% of 1866 features). For matrix-containing samples, NNLS-based decomposition achieved 86.4% accuracy (paper-subtracted) and 85.4% (paper-plus-potato-subtracted) with only 5 and 4 variables, respectively, representing a 99.44% data reduction.

Conclusion: Accurate Raman-based oil identification can be achieved through physically meaningful, highly compact, and interpretable spectral representations, providing a promising foundation for Frugal AI, Edge AI, portable sensing, and embedded food-quality monitoring.

Abstract: Authentication of edible oils in processed foods is important for food quality, fraud prevention, and regulatory compliance. This study establishes an integrated Raman spectroscopy and machine-learning framework that links intrinsic spectral organization, interpretable classification, and Physics-Informed Artificial Intelligence (PI-AI). Five edible oils were investigated in pure form and within a fried-potato-chip matrix using t-SNE, K-means clustering, Decision Trees, and Non-Negative Least Squares (NNLS)-based spectral decomposition. Unsupervised analyses revealed substantially stronger class organization and separability in pure oils, whereas food-matrix effects introduced pronounced spectral overlap. Decision Trees achieved 100% classification accuracy for pure oils using only four Raman variables from the original 1866-feature spectral space. These four variables, consistently identified by both pre-pruned and post-pruned models, represented only approximately 0.21% of the available spectral information while retaining perfect test-set performance. For matrix-containing samples, NNLS-based PI-AI spectral decomposition substantially improved classification by separating oil-related signatures from paper and potato contributions. Optimized post-pruned models achieved accuracies of 86.4% and 85.4% for paper-subtracted and paper-plus-potato-subtracted datasets, respectively, while reducing the number of important Raman variables to only five and four. The compact four-feature representation further reduced the data footprint by 99.44% without loss of classification accuracy. Collectively, these findings demonstrate that accurate Raman-based oil identification can be achieved through physically meaningful, highly compact, and interpretable spectral representations, providing a promising foundation for Frugal AI, Edge AI, portable sensing, and embedded food-quality monitoring.

</details>


### [207] [Shared Physics Responses Recover Hidden Rankings in Neural Operator Libraries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20441)
*Hanbing Liang,Fujun Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of selecting optimal neural-operator predictions during deployment when ground-truth solutions are unavailable. The authors propose using a shared physical diagnostic based on a single anchor-based linearized response of the governing equation, which accurately recovers model preferences and often outperforms individual candidates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Selecting optimal neural-operator predictions during deployment is challenging when high-fidelity reference solutions are unavailable. The core problem is to develop a method to rank and select the best model from a finite library without requiring ground-truth data.

Method: The method uses a squared Hilbert-space loss where ranking depends on the low-dimensional span of candidate differences. A single anchor-based linearized response of the governing equation provides a shared physical diagnostic for scoring all models simultaneously. The approach exploits local dynamical response rather than raw defect magnitude.

Result: The shared physical diagnostic recovered over 99.6% of pairwise preferences and 99.0% of optimal checkpoints across diverse Fourier and convolutional operator libraries for fluid, reaction-diffusion, and wave dynamics. The corrected physical proxy frequently outperformed the best individual candidates. Computable sufficient conditions were established to rigorously certify exact decisions for strongly monotone discretizations.
