Conclusion: This framework enables reliable and highly efficient deployment of scientific surrogates without requiring ground-truth data by exploiting local dynamical response rather than raw defect magnitude.

Abstract: Selecting the optimal neural-operator prediction during deployment is challenging when high-fidelity reference solutions are unavailable. We demonstrate that under a squared Hilbert-space loss, ranking a finite model library depends strictly on the low-dimensional span of candidate differences, allowing us to score all models simultaneously using a single anchor-based linearized response of the governing equation. This shared physical diagnostic accurately recovered over 99.6\% of pairwise preferences and 99.0\% of optimal checkpoints across diverse Fourier and convolutional operator libraries for fluid, reaction-diffusion, and wave dynamics. Furthermore, the corrected physical proxy frequently outperformed the best individual candidates, and we establish computable sufficient conditions that rigorously certify exact decisions for strongly monotone discretizations. By exploiting the local dynamical response rather than raw defect magnitude, this framework enables the reliable and highly efficient deployment of scientific surrogates without requiring ground-truth data.

</details>


### [208] [Stored in Optimizer State, Valued by Later Training: A Causal Account of Subliminal Trait Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20442)
*Qinyang Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper discovers a two-stage mechanism for "subliminal trait transfer" in neural networks: (1) optimizer state (first moment) carries source perturbations without affecting immediate parameters/hidden states, and (2) later training determines whether this transferred signal becomes positive, negative, or neutral in terms of behavioral impact. The key insight is that optimizer moments, not just parameters, serve as causal carriers of traits that survive source removal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work explained how subliminal signals enter gradients but failed to explain: (1) how these signals survive source removal, and (2) how they acquire different signs under later training. This work aims to understand the causal mechanism behind trait transfer beyond gradient propagation.

Method: The authors treat parameters and optimizer moments as a single "trainer state" and derive an exact transport-valuation identity. They perform "state surgery" experiments - transplanting optimizer first moments alone vs. with parameters - to identify the causal carrier. They test across multiple models (Qwen, SmolLM2, Llama-3.2-1B) and architectures (including CNNs on MNIST with AdamW and momentum SGD).

Result: Key findings: (1) First moment alone is the causal carrier - transplanting it leaves immediate parameters/hidden states unchanged but causes growing differences under source-free updates; (2) Different "futures" (continuation training scenarios) produce negative (-0.658), near-zero (+0.008), and positive (+0.658) Qwen effects; (3) This ordering recurs in all 12 Llama-3.2-1B seeds after 8 updates; (4) Full-horizon costate predicts all 42 Qwen route-mean signs and 21 Llama ordinary-route signs; (5) Results replicate across Qwen, SmolLM2, and Llama models, and extend to non-LoRA MNIST systems including CNNs with AdamW and momentum SGD.

Conclusion: Subliminal trait transfer operates through a two-stage mechanism: (1) optimizer state transports the source perturbation observer-independently, and (2) later training determines its behavioral value. This identifies optimizer moments as critical causal agents in trait transfer, explaining how traits survive source removal and acquire different signs under different training continuations.

Abstract: Subliminal trait transfer allows a student model to acquire behavioral dispositions from teacher-generated data in which the trait is not semantically expressed. Recent work explains how such signals enter gradients, but not how they survive source removal or acquire different signs under later training. We treat parameters and optimizer moments as a single trainer state and derive an exact transport-valuation identity separating observer-independent propagation of the source perturbation from the value assigned by a future continuation and behavioral readout. State surgery identifies the first moment as a causal carrier. Transplanting it alone leaves parameters, hidden states, and outputs unchanged at the cut, yet source-free updates generate growing parameter and hidden-state differences; transplanting parameters with the first moment recovers the terminal behavioral response. Sending the same source-induced difference through matched futures produces negative, near-zero, and positive Qwen effects (-0.658, +0.008, and +0.658 seed means). This ordering recurs in all 12 Llama-3.2-1B seeds after eight updates, while state-difference norms remain nearly equal across routes. Both contrasts grow in every paired seed when the continuation extends to sixteen updates. A full-horizon costate predicts all 42 Qwen route-mean signs and all 21 resolved Llama ordinary-route signs. Observer-independent transport also replicates across Qwen, SmolLM2, and Llama, while the complete-state recurrence predicts physical, hidden, and fixed-head responses in non-LoRA MNIST systems, including CNNs trained with AdamW and momentum SGD. Together, these results identify a two-stage mechanism for subliminal trait transfer: optimizer state transports the source perturbation, and later training determines its behavioral value.

</details>


### [209] [Amortized Bandwidth Learning for Kernel Density Estimation under Logarithmic Score](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20445)
*Junyi Liang,Hailiang Du*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes an amortized neural network framework for bandwidth selection in kernel density estimation (KDE). Instead of computing bandwidths individually for each dataset using traditional methods like Silverman's rule or cross-validation, the method learns a mapping from samples to optimal bandwidths across diverse training tasks using finite Gaussian mixtures. The approach uses truncated-and-renormalized bounded-support formulation and affine standardization for transferability across different data ranges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bandwidth selection is critical for KDE performance but existing methods either rely on asymptotic approximations (often inaccurate for finite samples), require expensive per-sample optimization (cross-validation), or fail to leverage patterns across similar density estimation tasks. The authors propose learning the sample-to-bandwidth mapping amortized across many tasks.

Method: 1) Train an amortized selector using finite Gaussian mixtures as training densities (supported by L1 approximation property)
2) Use truncated-and-renormalized bounded-support formulation for stable learning
3) Apply affine standardization to enable transfer across different bounded intervals
4) Optimize using logarithmic score (proper scoring rule)
5) The trained selector can then directly predict bandwidths for new samples without optimization

Result: The amortized selector consistently and substantially outperforms classical methods (Silverman's rule, Sheather-Jones selector, least-squares cross-validation) across Gaussian sampling, multi-family benchmark, and randomized Gaussian-mixture training scenarios. Particularly large improvements observed for small samples and heterogeneous data.

Conclusion: The proposed amortized framework achieves broad generalization across different density structures without requiring distributional assumptions. This makes it attractive for applications where finite samples must be converted to continuous probability densities, offering both strong empirical performance and wide applicability.

Abstract: Kernel density estimation converts finite samples into probability densities, but its performance depends critically on bandwidth selection. Classical selectors prescribe the sample-to-bandwidth rule analytically or asymptotically, or solve a new optimization for each sample. An amortized framework is proposed that instead learns this mapping across a distribution of density-estimation tasks by optimizing the logarithmic score. A truncated-and-renormalized bounded-support formulation enables stable learning across heterogeneous tasks, while affine standardization allows a selector trained on a single reference interval to transfer across bounded intervals. Experiments under Gaussian sampling, a multi-family benchmark, and randomized Gaussian-mixture training show that the amortized selector consistently and substantially outperforms Silverman's rule, the Sheather--Jones selector, and least-squares cross-validation, with especially large gains in small and heterogeneous samples. Finite Gaussian mixtures provide a generic training mechanism supported by their $L^1$ approximation property. Selectors trained in this way generalize strongly across different density structures, allowing the same trained selector to be applied directly to finite samples from unknown densities without specifying or fitting a distributional family. This combination of broad applicability and strong empirical performance makes the framework attractive for a wide range of applications in which finite samples or ensembles must be converted into continuous probability densities.

</details>


### [210] [Mutual information and sensitivity analysis for feature selection in customer targeting: a comparative study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20447)
*Nestor Barraza,Sergio Moro,Marcelo Ferreyra,Adolfo de la Peña*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares two feature selection techniques—mutual information and data-based sensitivity analysis—in the context of a bank telemarketing case study. Both methods were applied to identify the most influential features for predicting telemarketing success, followed by logistic regression modeling. Mutual information selected 13 features while data-based sensitivity analysis selected 9. The study concludes that mutual information remains a valid method, while data-based sensitivity analysis achieves good results with fewer features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Feature selection is critical in data-driven knowledge discovery projects. The motivation is to analyze and compare the advantages and disadvantages of two feature selection techniques: mutual information (a traditional method) and data-based sensitivity analysis (a more recent approach), using a real-world bank telemarketing case to determine which method better identifies features that influence the success of telemarketing contacts.

Method: The study applied both mutual information and data-based sensitivity analysis to a bank telemarketing dataset to identify the most influencing features for predicting telemarketing success. Subsequently, logistic regression models were built using the features selected by each technique (13 features for mutual information, 9 for data-based sensitivity analysis). The models were evaluated and compared based on their performance across different false positive ratios.

Result: The data-based sensitivity analysis performed better at lower false positive values, while mutual information showed slightly better performance at higher false positive ratios. Mutual information identified 13 features as relevant, whereas data-based sensitivity analysis selected 9 features. The results indicate that mutual information, despite being a non-recent method, remains a valid and effective feature selection technique, while data-based sensitivity analysis achieves competitive prediction accuracy with fewer features.

Conclusion: Mutual information remains a valid and effective feature selection method, even when compared to more recent techniques like data-based sensitivity analysis. The choice between methods depends on the specific use case: data-based sensitivity analysis is advantageous when fewer features are desired and lower false positive rates are acceptable, while mutual information is preferable when bank managers want to reduce contact costs without risking significant loss of successful outcomes. The study demonstrates that both techniques have merit and can be valuable depending on the operational requirements and cost considerations.

Abstract: Feature selection is a highly relevant task in a data-driven knowledge discovery project. Several techniques have been developed aiming at finding the features that influence most an outcome to predict, including mutual information and, in recent years, the data-based sensitivity analysis. The present research focus on analyzing the advantages and disadvantages of each of these two techniques, by applying both to a bank telemarketing case. Thereafter, a logistic regression model is built on the tuned set of features identified by each of the two techniques as the most influencing set of features on the success of a telemarketing contact, in a total of 13 features for mutual information and 9 features for the data-based sensitivity analysis. The latter performs better for lower values of false positives while the former is slightly better for a higher false positive ratio. Thus, mutual information becomes a better choice if bank managers intend to reduce slightly the cost of contacts without risking losing a high number of successes. Such results show that mutual information, although not recent, is still a valid method for feature selection. On the other side, the data-based sensitivity analysis selection achieved good prediction results with less features.

</details>


### [211] [When Clean Data Hurts: Learning with Monotone Corruptions Beyond Binary Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20480)
*Julian Asilis,Shaddin Dughmi,Chirag Pabbaraju*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates learning with monotone adversarial corruptions, where i.i.d. training data is corrupted with examples from an adversarial source. The authors show that while binary classification remains learnable (albeit with a logarithmic penalty), certain multiclass problems (DS dimension 2) and partial binary concept classes become completely unlearnable under monotone adversaries who can insert corrupted datapoints. However, learnability is preserved when the number of adaptive insertions is o(n), and standard error rates remain achievable under various constrained adversary models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optimal learners are designed for the standard PAC model assuming i.i.d. data. This work investigates what happens when this i.i.d. assumption is violated through monotone adversarial corruptions - a recently introduced model. The motivation is to understand the power of monotone adversaries in more general learning settings beyond binary classification, which was previously studied by Mehrotra (2026).

Method: The authors construct specific learning problems to demonstrate the power of monotone adversaries: (1) A multiclass problem with DS dimension 2 that becomes unlearnable when an adaptive adversary inserts b = n corrupted datapoints, and (2) an analogous construction for partial binary concept classes. They also prove positive results showing learnability is preserved under various constraints: o(n) insertions, constant budget b = O(1), semi-adaptive adversaries viewing only a p-fraction of the training data, and oblivious adversaries.

Result: The main negative result shows that monotone adversaries can make learnable multiclass problems (DS dimension 2) completely unlearnable by inserting a linear number (b = n) of corrupted points. This contrasts with binary classification where learnability is preserved (though with increased error). For partial binary concept classes, similar impossibility results hold. On the positive side, the paper shows that every class remains learnable when adaptive insertions are o(n), and the classic error rate O(d_DS/n) is achievable against adversaries with constant budget, limited viewing access, or no access to the training data.

Conclusion: Monotone adversaries are significantly more powerful in multiclass and partial binary settings than in binary classification. The threshold for preserving learnability is o(n) corrupted insertions. The classic PAC error rate can be maintained when the adversary is constrained (constant budget, limited information, or oblivious). This work closes the gap left by Mehrotra (2026) regarding the consequences of corruption for more general learning settings.

Abstract: Optimal learners are tailored to exploit the i.i.d.\ data assumption underlying the classic PAC model. What if an i.i.d.\ training sample were corrupted with correctly labeled examples drawn from an otherwise unrelated, even adversarial source? This model of learning with monotone adversarial corruptions was recently introduced by Larsen et al. (2026), who demonstrated that all known optimal binary learners suffer increased error rates in this setting, from $O(d / n)$ in the PAC model to $Ω(d \log(n / d) / n)$ under monotone corruption. Mehrotra (2026) proved this logarithmic factor to be necessary for binary classification, but left open the consequences of corruption for more general learning settings, such as multiclass classification and partial binary concept classes.
  As our primary result, we demonstrate that monotone adversaries are frighteningly more powerful in each of these settings. We exhibit a learnable multiclass problem, of DS dimension only 2, that becomes altogether unlearnable under a monotone adversary, and show an analogous result for partial binary concept classes. These results are achieved by an adaptive adversary permitted to view the original i.i.d.\ training set $S$ and to insert $b < \infty$ corrupted datapoints into $S$. In the multiclass example, the adversary need only insert a linear number $b = |S| = n$ of datapoints.
  We complement these impossibility results by proving that every class remains learnable when the number of adaptive additions is $o(n)$, which our previous multiclass lower bound proves to be tight. We further observe that the classic multiclass error rate of $O(d_{\mathrm{DS}} / n)$ remains achievable against adaptive adversaries restricted to a known constant budget $b = O(1)$, against semi-adaptive adversaries viewing only a $p$-fraction of $S$ for $p \in (0, 1)$, and against oblivious adversaries that cannot view $S$.

</details>


### [212] [Metag: A dataset to build agentic meta-reviewing capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20488)
*Anirudh Sundar,Min Chen,Divya Tadimeti,Gemma Zhang,Alice Li,Nigel Boachie Kumankumah,Pavan Uttej Ravva,Sadid Hasan,Somya Chatterjee,Pruthvi Prakash Navada,Xiao Wang,Yue Kang,Sulaiman Vesal,Larry Heck*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Metag is a dataset of 349 action items designed to accelerate development of meta-reviewing agents that identify changes made to scientific articles during the review-rebuttal process, by aligning manuscript diffs with reviewer concerns and author rebuttals from OpenReview.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing number of conference submissions has increased the burden on meta-reviewers who must synthesize reviewer feedback, author rebuttals, and manuscript revisions. There is a need for tools to help meta-reviewers quickly identify whether authors have addressed reviewer statements and where changes were made.

Method: Dataset collected by obtaining manuscript versions from before review deadlines and after acceptance, computing document differences, and having human annotators align these differences with action items from OpenReview discussions.

Result: A dataset of 349 high-quality action items tied to paper differences, publicly available on GitHub.

Conclusion: Metag dataset will enable building methods to empower meta-reviewers, adding transparency and traceability to the peer review process.

Abstract: AI tools increasingly support tasks across the scientific research cycle, from experiment design and manuscript preparation to peer review. At the same time, the continuing growth in conference submissions has increased the burden on meta-reviewers, who must synthesize reviewer feedback, author rebuttals, and manuscript revisions. To address this concern, this paper introduces Metag, a dataset to accelerate the development of meta-reviewing agents, specifically to identify changes made to scientific articles during the review-rebuttal process. Each instance contains a reviewer concern, the author's proposed resolution, and the manuscript diffs implementing the stated change. Metag is collected by obtaining manuscript versions from before the review deadline and after acceptance, computing differences between the two documents, and asking human annotators to align these differences with action items from OpenReview discussions. The resulting dataset consists of 349 high-quality action items tied to paper differences and will enable building methods to empower meta reviewers to quickly identify whether authors have addressed reviewer statements and where in the paper those changes have been made, resulting in additional transparency and traceability throughout peer review. The dataset is publicly available at https://github.com/microsoft/Metag-dataset.

</details>


### [213] [Bern2Edge: A Neurosymbolic Compiler for Edge Deployment via Bernstein Polynomial Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20497)
*Malak Gamal El-Din,Yifan Zhang,Yasser Shoukry,Sitao Huang,Salma Elmalaki*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Deploying high-accuracy neural networks on resource-constrained edge devices remains challenging, as existing approaches treat training, compression, and hardware synthesis as separate stages, leaving a gap between software-trained models and efficient end-to-end deployment with limited support for interpretability. We propose Bern2Edge, an end-to-end framework that uses knowledge distillation to convert a pretrained teacher feed-forward network into hardware-efficient representations via Bernstein polynomial activations. This representation enables two deployment paths: (i) a high-fidelity LUT-based realization that preserves model fidelity under compression, and (ii) a symbolic rule-based representation derived from Bernstein activation geometry, enabling interpretable inference with explicit input-space constraints. The resulting BNNs achieve up to 2.12 percentage-point (pp) accuracy improvement over ReLU under identical compression constraints. At the system level, Bern2Edge achieves up to 99.8% latency reduction and 95.2% BRAM reduction relative to a W8A8 quantized teacher on an AMD Xilinx KV260 FPGA, while maintaining accuracy within 0.5 pp, and further deploys on a low-power Spartan-7 XC7S15 FPGA. The rule-based path reduces DSP usage by up to 89.0% at a cost of 1.5 pp in total accuracy.

</details>


### [214] [When Graph-JEPA Learns the Wrong Thing: Diagnosing and Repairing Category-Conditional Collapse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20516)
*Gollam Rabby,Sören Auer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper reveals a critical evaluation failure in Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPA): linear probing and effective rank can appear healthy while the representation carries zero usable instance information. Using a scientific-reasoning graph dataset, the authors demonstrate this failure mode stems from variance allocation issues in the objective's optimum, and show that even successful repairs can saturate on reducible targets that carry no actual reasoning capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors investigate why Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPA) are almost universally selected by linear probing and effective rank metrics, seeking to understand if these evaluation methods can mislead researchers about representation quality.

Method: The authors use a scientific-reasoning graph containing 57,903 articles (each a subgraph), where a Graph-JEPA predicts one masked aspect from remaining aspects. They compare retrieval performance against three upper bounds, analyze variance allocation between frozen inputs vs trained latents, and trace the degenerate solution to the objective's global minimum present at initialization.

Result: Initial Graph-JEPA achieved linear-probe accuracy 0.871 and effective rank 18-47, yet retrieval recovered 0.00 of 14.4 bits (MRR 1.9e-4 vs chance 1.99e-4, p=0.98). Root cause: frozen inputs allocated 86.05% variance to subgraph identity vs 0.40% to aspect identity, while trained latents showed 0.39% and 99.61% respectively. After repair, performance reached 14.377 of 14.379 bits (above 13.865-bit oracle), but the target was found to be reducible (intra-subgraph edges are deterministic function of node census), meaning no actual reasoning capability was learned.

Conclusion: Linear probing, effective rank, and retrieval metrics can all saturate on unsupportive evaluations where representations carry no usable instance information or genuine reasoning capability. The authors provide a harness with reducibility audit and target gate to prevent such evaluation failures.

Abstract: Joint-embedding predictive architectures are selected almost universally by linear probing and effective rank. We report a case where both read healthily while the representation carries zero usable instance information. We repair it, and a second failure appears: the repaired metric saturates on a target carrying no structural information. Our corpus is a scientific-reasoning graph over 57,903 articles, each a subgraph. A Graph-JEPA predicts one masked aspect from a subgraph's remaining aspects, attaining linear-probe accuracy 0.871 and effective rank 18-47, yet retrieval recovers 0.00 of 14.4 bits (MRR 1.9e-4 vs chance 1.99e-4, p=0.98). Three upper bounds on the same pool and code recover nearly everything (+14.28, +14.34, +14.22 bits), ruling out corpus, masking, pool, and metric as causes. We trace this to variance allocation - frozen inputs place 86.05% of variance on subgraph identity and 0.40% on aspect identity, while trained latents place 0.39% and 99.61%. This is a property of the objective's optimum: the degenerate solution is a global minimum of the coupled predictor/EMA-target objective, present already at init. A repaired configuration reaches 14.377 of 14.379 bits, above the 13.865-bit oracle; reverting the loss to regression drops it to 0.307 bits, confirming it. Yet the repair licenses nothing about reasoning: the target is reducible, since intra-subgraph edges are a deterministic function of node census. The oracle reaches 96.4% of the ceiling, and our largest effect is the learning-rate schedule, not architecture. Bits and a reasoning probe show no relation across ten cells. A data-derived target fails a quality gate - 25.96% of nodes are duplicate placeholders, and the rest is more generic than supporting evidence. Rank, probes, and metrics can all saturate on an unsupportive evaluation. We release a harness with a reducibility audit and target gate.

</details>


### [215] [Learning Exact NVIDIA SASS Encoders with $\mathbb{F}_2$ Linear Algebra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20532)
*Jiading Gai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: F2Asm is the first open-source NVIDIA SASS assembler for modern data-center GPUs (Hopper SM90, Blackwell SM100, Rubin SM107), learned from 3,225 CUBINs using Gaussian elimination over F2 to reconstruct exact 128-bit instruction encoders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NVIDIA provides SASS disassemblers but no public SASS assemblers for recent data-center GPUs, limiting controlled machine-code rewriting capabilities.

Method: F2Asm learns SASS instruction encoders as vector-valued affine maps over F2 using Gaussian elimination to incrementally build a compact basis, detect inconsistencies, and reject inputs outside the learned span. It separates target-specific control bits, relocation rules, and CUBIN metadata from the learning algorithm.

Result: F2Asm successfully trains encoders for Hopper SM90/SM90a, Blackwell SM100, and Rubin SM107 using 3,225 CUBINs from production libraries and CUDA packages. In round-trip tests, all reassembled executable text sections match originals exactly.

Conclusion: F2Asm enables exact reconstruction of SASS instructions from disassembled code, providing the first open-source assembler support for the latest NVIDIA Rubin SM107 GPU architecture.

Abstract: NVIDIA provides a SASS disassembler but no public SASS assembler for recent data-center GPUs, limiting controlled machine-code rewriting. We present F2Asm, which learns exact 128-bit SASS encoders from paired disassembly and original CUBIN instruction words. To our knowledge, F2Asm is the first system to learn SASS instruction encoders as vector-valued affine maps over F2 and the first open-source NVIDIA SASS assembler to support Rubin SM107. F2Asm uses Gaussian elimination over F2 to incrementally build a compact basis, detect inconsistencies, and reject inputs outside the learned span. F2Asm separates target-specific control bits, relocation rules, and CUBIN metadata from its learning algorithm. We train encoders for Hopper SM90/SM90a, Blackwell SM100, and Rubin SM107 using 3,225 CUBINs from pinned NVIDIA and third-party production libraries, CUDA 13.3 packages, and CUDA 13.4 Developer Preview archives. In round-trip tests, F2Asm reassembles the disassembled SASS for each CUBIN, and all compared executable text sections match the originals exactly.

</details>


### [216] [AgentDecarbonizer: Carbon-Aware Execution for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20566)
*Leyi Yan,Shuangning Li,Sihang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents AgentDecarbonizer, a carbon-aware optimization system for AI agent workflows that reduces carbon emissions by up to 57.9% by intelligently scheduling task execution based on grid carbon intensity and deadline constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents extend LLMs to long-running workflows with repeated model invocations that incur substantial carbon emissions. The authors identify deadline flexibility as an opportunity for carbon-aware execution, allowing tasks to wait for lower-carbon periods or shift to lower-carbon grids.

Method: The authors characterized carbon emissions of OpenClaw agent workloads using WildClawBench, analyzing dependencies on token consumption, context cache reuse, and grid carbon intensity. They developed AgentDecarbonizer that conservatively estimates task duration and selects deadline-feasible execution schedules while accounting for cache recomputation overhead during spatial shifting.

Result: Evaluated on WildClawBench with 60 agent tasks across four grids, AgentDecarbonizer reduces carbon emissions by up to 57.9% compared with a carbon-agnostic baseline and by up to 37.5% compared with a baseline that selects the carbon-optimal grid at task start time.

Conclusion: Carbon-aware scheduling for AI agents is feasible and effective. By leveraging deadline flexibility and accounting for cache recomputation overhead, significant carbon emission reductions can be achieved without compromising task completion deadlines.

Abstract: AI agents extend large language models from single prompt-response interactions to long-running, goaldirected workflows that issue many model calls, invoke tools, and interact with external environments. These workflows enable tasks such as software repair, data analysis, and experiment management, but their repeated model invocations can incur substantial carbon emissions. This paper characterizes the carbon emissions of OpenClaw agent workloads using WildClawBench, and shows that emissions depend on token consumption, context cache reuse, and the carbon intensity of the grid. Our characterization identifies deadline flexibility as an opportunity for carbon-aware execution: agent tasks can wait for lower-carbon-intensity periods or shift to lower-carbon grids. However, doing so requires handling uncertain execution time for temporal shifting and cached context recomputation during spatial shifting. We present AgentDecarbonizer, a carbon optimizer for AI agents that runs alongside OpenClaw. Given a task prompt and user-specified deadline, AgentDecarbonizer conservatively estimates task duration and selects deadline-feasible execution schedules, while accounting for cache recomputation overhead during spatial shifting. Evaluated on WildClawBench workloads with 60 agent tasks across four grids, AgentDecarbonizer reduces carbon emissions by up to 57.9 % compared with a carbon-agnostic baseline and by up to 37.5 % compared with a baseline that selects the carbon-optimal grid at task start time.

</details>


### [217] [Faults That Fortify: CNN Adversarial Robustness via GPU Undervolting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20572)
*Behnam Omidi,Ahmad Tahmasivand,Husam Alsyouri,Saba Al-Sayouri,Chongzhou Fang,Ihsen Alouani,Khaled N. Khasawneh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) face a dual challenge: vulnerability to adversarial attacks and prohibitive training cost. Adversarial training is effective but expensive, a burden that grows as learning shifts to the energy-constrained edge. This paper addresses both through GPU undervolting during training. Reducing supply voltage introduces stochastic perturbations that act as implicit regularization, improving robustness while lowering power. We characterize undervolting-induced faults at the bit level, then train LeNet, VGG-6, and MobileNetV3 on MNIST and CIFAR-10 under two training regimes, standard and adversarial, each at nominal and undervolted voltage, and evaluate all models against adversarial attacks. In both regimes, the undervolted model consistently achieves higher adversarial accuracy than its nominal-voltage counterpart, showing that hardware-induced faults strengthen even adversarial training. Because dynamic power scales quadratically with supply voltage, these robustness gains arrive with substantial energy savings. GPU undervolting is therefore a readily deployable hardware-level defense requiring no algorithmic change, and opens a promising direction in which robustness and energy efficiency move together.

</details>


### [218] [Provable Edge-of-Stability for Adam on a One-Dimensional Quadratic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20638)
*Yiman Fong,Heng Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the edge-of-stability (EoS) phenomenon in the Adam optimizer using a one-dimensional quadratic function as a clean setting. The authors characterize Adam's dynamics across the parameter space, proving that Adam exhibits a restoring tendency toward a frozen stability threshold in broad regimes, while also identifying conditions where this edge-seeking mechanism breaks down.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The edge-of-stability phenomenon of Adam has been widely observed, but its underlying dynamical mechanism is not yet fully understood. The authors seek to provide a concrete dynamical explanation for Adam's EoS in a setting free of evolving loss geometry.

Method: The study analyzes uncorrected Adam on a one-dimensional quadratic function, which provides a clean setting where constant curvature isolates the optimizer-induced dynamics behind the EoS. The authors characterize the resulting dynamics across the parameter space.

Result: In broad regimes, Adam exhibits a restoring tendency toward its frozen stability threshold $2(1+β_1)/[η(1-β_1)]$. However, the edge-seeking mechanism breaks down in certain settings, including strictly subcritical periodic orbits and specially tuned trajectories that converge to the optimum while remaining uniformly supercritical.

Conclusion: These results give a concrete dynamical explanation for Adam's EoS in a setting free of evolving loss geometry, while also exposing its limitations. The study provides insight into when and why Adam's edge-seeking behavior occurs or fails.

Abstract: The edge-of-stability (EoS) phenomenon of Adam has been widely observed, while its underlying dynamical mechanism is not yet fully understood. We study uncorrected Adam on a one-dimensional quadratic, a clean setting where constant curvature isolates the optimizer-induced dynamics behind the EoS. We characterize the resulting dynamics across the parameter space. In broad regimes, we prove that Adam exhibits a restoring tendency toward its frozen stability threshold $2(1+β_1)/[η(1-β_1)]$. We also identify settings in which this edge-seeking mechanism breaks down, including strictly subcritical periodic orbits and specially tuned trajectories that converge to the optimum while remaining uniformly supercritical. These results give a concrete dynamical explanation for Adam's EoS in a setting free of evolving loss geometry, while also exposing its limitations.

</details>


### [219] [Meta-clustering of milk mid-infrared spectra identifies dairy cow groups associated with negative energy balance in early lactation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20653)
*T. Touil,E. R. Paquet*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study used clustering methods on milk mid-infrared (MIR) spectra to identify distinct groups of early-lactation dairy cows. Using 407,632 milk records from 3,408 farms, eight clustering approaches (combining spectral filtering, dimensionality reduction (PCA/autoencoder), and clustering algorithms (k-means/spectral clustering)) were compared. Five meta-clusters were identified, strongly associated with days in milk (DIM) and reflecting a gradient of negative energy balance (NEB) severity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Applying clustering directly to MIR spectral data could reveal latent groups of cows associated with milk traits or health disorders, enabling prevention and monitoring of at-risk animals. The study aimed to identify groups of individual dairy cows in early lactation directly from milk MIR spectra and analyze their associations with milk traits.

Method: Dataset: 407,632 individual milk MIR records from 3,408 commercial farms. Eight clustering approaches were tested: (i) spectral filtering for informative wavenumbers, (ii) two dimensionality-reduction methods (PCA and autoencoder), and (iii) two clustering algorithms (k-means and spectral clustering). Clusters were regrouped into meta-clusters encompassing similar groups identified by the eight approaches.

Result: Five distinct meta-clusters of early-lactation dairy cows were identified, significantly associated with milk traits. The eight approaches converged on the same five meta-clusters. The classic PCA-based k-means using the full spectrum performed comparably to more sophisticated approaches. The five meta-clusters were strongly associated with DIM and appeared to reflect a gradient of NEB severity: severe, moderate, mild, and two representing recovery stages (rapid restoration and early recovery).

Conclusion: Clustering of milk MIR spectra can effectively identify distinct groups of early-lactation dairy cows with meaningful biological associations. The simple PCA-based k-means approach using the full spectrum is sufficient and computationally efficient. The five meta-clusters reflect different stages of negative energy balance, providing a potential tool for monitoring cow health and metabolic status in early lactation.

Abstract: Clustering methods have been used to identify distinct groups of milk samples, cows, or herds. Fourier-transform infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy, particularly mid-infrared (MIR) spectroscopy, has been applied to individual cow milk samples to predict various milk traits. Applying clustering directly to MIR spectral data may reveal latent groups of cows associated with milk traits or health disorders and can help prevent these conditions or monitor at-risk animals. This study aimed to identify groups of individual dairy cows in early lactation directly from milk MIR spectra and to analyze their associations with milk traits. Using a dataset of 407,632 individual milk MIR records from 3,408 commercial farms, we combined (i) spectral filtering that selects informative wavenumbers, (ii) two dimensionality-reduction methods: principal component analysis (PCA) and an autoencoder, and (iii) two clustering algorithms: k-means and spectral clustering to yield eight different clustering approaches. We regrouped the assigned clusters into meta-clusters that encompassed the most similar ones identified by the eight approaches. Our results revealed five distinct meta-clusters of early-lactation individual dairy cows significantly associated with milk traits. Despite substantial differences, the eight approaches converged on the same five meta-clusters, and the classic, computationally efficient PCA-based k-means approach using the full spectrum recaptured clusters identified by more sophisticated, computationally intensive approaches. The five meta-clusters were strongly associated with DIM and appeared to reflect a gradient of negative energy balance (NEB) severity: severe, moderate, and possibly mild, while the remaining two likely represented cows recovering from NEB, one with rapid restoration of energy balance and one in early recovery.

</details>


### [220] [RiskTraf: Risk-Extrapolated Residual Learning for Multi-Variate Traffic Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20656)
*Guangyu Wang,Zhidan Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces PEMSB-3V, a public benchmark preserving raw flow, speed, and occupancy from PeMS detectors for traffic flow prediction, and RiskTraf, a model-agnostic plug-in that mitigates regime-specific shortcut correlations by learning risk-extrapolated residuals from historical speed and occupancy data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard traffic flow forecasting benchmarks and models rarely exploit all three raw measurements (flow, speed, occupancy) reliably. Existing releases often omit these variables, replace them with proxies, or contain logically inconsistent records. Additionally, direct empirical risk minimization over three-variable inputs may exploit regime-dependent shortcuts because relationships among flow, speed, and occupancy vary substantially between free-flow and congested states.

Method: The authors propose two contributions: (1) PEMSB-3V benchmark suite preserving raw flow, speed, and occupancy from PeMS detectors; (2) RiskTraf, a model-agnostic risk-extrapolated residual plug-in. For each trained spatio-temporal backbone, RiskTraf freezes the selected checkpoint and learns a lightweight zero-start residual head from historical speed and occupancy. The residual head constructs ordered traffic-risk environments and optimizes horizon-wise flow corrections with a risk extrapolation objective.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate that RiskTraf consistently improves diverse forecasting backbones and outperforms debiasing and distribution-shift adaptation methods.

Conclusion: The proposed approach effectively mitigates regime-specific shortcut correlations without modifying the backbone architecture, providing a general-purpose improvement for traffic flow forecasting models.

Abstract: Traffic sensors commonly record flow, speed, and occupancy, but standard traffic flow forecasting benchmarks and models rarely exploit all three raw measurements reliably. Although speed and occupancy provide sensor-native traffic-state information beyond flow alone, existing releases often omit these variables, replace them with proxies, or contain logically inconsistent records. Moreover, direct empirical risk minimization over three-variable inputs may exploit regime-dependent shortcuts, as the relationships among flow, speed, and occupancy vary substantially between free-flow and congested states. We introduce \textbf{PEMSB-3V}, a public benchmark suite that preserves raw flow, speed, and occupancy measurements from PeMS detectors for flow prediction. We also propose \textbf{RiskTraf}, a model-agnostic risk-extrapolated residual plug-in. For each trained spatio-temporal backbone, RiskTraf freezes the selected checkpoint and learns a lightweight zero-start residual head from historical speed and occupancy. The residual head constructs ordered traffic-risk environments and optimizes horizon-wise flow corrections with a risk extrapolation objective, thereby mitigating regime-specific shortcut correlations without modifying the backbone. Extensive experiments demonstrate that RiskTraf consistently improves diverse forecasting backbones and outperforms debiasing and distribution-shift adaptation methods. Our code and benchmark are available at https://github.com/Guangyu4/RiskTraf.

</details>


### [221] [C-Score: Beyond Accuracy for Robustness Assessment in Semi-Supervised Learning under Open-World Unlabeled Contamination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20667)
*Tsao-Lun Chen,Chi-Cheng Fu,Han-Yi E. Chou,Shun-Feng Su*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses hidden collapse in pseudo-label-based SSL under open-world unlabeled contamination. The authors propose C-Score, a diagnostic framework evaluating training behavior across prediction, feature representation, and optimization spaces, using metrics like PLE, CCI, Sem-Drift, and Grad-Align. Experiments show that clean accuracy alone fails to detect significant degradation caused by OOD contamination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pseudo-label-based SSL is typically developed under closed-world assumptions (unlabeled data from same distribution as labeled data). In practice, unlabeled data may contain OOD samples, which can receive high-confidence predictions and be incorrectly incorporated into training. This creates a critical evaluation problem: clean test accuracy may appear stable even when internal learning dynamics have already deteriorated.

Method: C-Score framework evaluating training behavior in three complementary spaces: (1) Prediction space - PLE (Pseudo-label Entropy) and CCI (Confident Confusion Index) for unlabeled prediction behavior; (2) Feature representation space - Sem-Drift for deviation from labeled semantic anchors; (3) Optimization space - Grad-Align for compatibility between labeled and unlabeled optimization. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with multiple OOD sources, varying contamination ratios, and four SSL algorithms.

Result: C-Score metrics reveal hidden degradation that clean accuracy fails to detect: under SVHN contamination, CCI rises over 280% while best-accuracy remains within 3% of uncontaminated baseline; near-OOD sources (CIFAR-100, STL-10) cause up to 14.9% accuracy collapse (FlexMatch, r=0.5).

Conclusion: Clean accuracy alone is insufficient for evaluating SSL robustness in open-world environments. Internal diagnostic signals are necessary for more reliable robustness assessment under unlabeled contamination.

Abstract: Pseudo-label-based semi-supervised learning has achieved strong performance due to its simplicity and scalability. However, it is typically developed under a closed-world assumption that unlabeled data are drawn from the same distribution as labeled data. In practical deployment, unlabeled data are often collected from open environments and may contain OOD samples. Under such contamination, OOD samples may still receive high-confidence predictions and be incorporated into training as if they were valid target examples. This creates an important evaluation problem: clean in-distribution test accuracy may appear stable even when the internal learning dynamics of SSL have already deteriorated. To address this issue, we study hidden collapse in pseudo-label-based SSL under open-world unlabeled contamination from a diagnostic evaluation perspective. We present C-Score, a compact framework that evaluates training behavior in three complementary spaces: prediction, feature representation, and optimization. C-Score includes PLE and CCI for unlabeled prediction behavior, Sem-Drift for deviation from labeled semantic anchors, and Grad-Align for the compatibility between labeled and unlabeled optimization. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with multiple OOD sources, varying contamination ratios, and four pseudo-label-based SSL algorithms show that C-Score metrics reveal hidden degradation that clean accuracy alone fails to detect: under SVHN contamination, CCI rises over 280% while best-accuracy remains within 3% of the uncontaminated baseline; near-OOD sources (CIFAR-100, STL-10) cause up to 14.9% accuracy collapse (FlexMatch, r=0.5). The results suggest that clean accuracy alone is insufficient for evaluating SSL robustness in open-world environments, and that internal diagnostic signals are necessary for more reliable robustness assessment under unlabeled contamination.

</details>


### [222] [Lightweight Adaptive ReduNet via Hyperspherical Manifold Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20668)
*Zhenglin Huang,Qifa Yan,Bin Dai,Xiaohu Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LA-ReduNet is a lightweight adaptive architecture that improves the layer-wise construction of ReduNet by using hyperspherical manifold learning and adaptive step sizes, reducing the number of layers required for MCR² objective stabilization by approximately 29 times while maintaining comparable classification accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original ReduNet, a white-box neural network using maximal coding rate reduction (MCR²) principle, requires a large number of layers for the MCR² objective to reach a stable value, leading to excessive parameter storage in the unfolded module.

Method: LA-ReduNet refines the layer-wise update rule by incorporating hyperspherical manifold learning and adaptive step sizes, enabling discriminative feature representations with substantially fewer unfolded layers.

Result: LA-ReduNet achieves comparable classification accuracy while requiring significantly fewer layers for the MCR² objective to stabilize. Under the experimental settings, it requires only approximately 1/29 of the parameter storage of the original unfolded ReduNet module.

Conclusion: LA-ReduNet successfully addresses the parameter efficiency issue of ReduNet by reducing layer count by an order of magnitude through hyperspherical manifold learning and adaptive step sizes, making it a more practical white-box neural network architecture.

Abstract: In recent years, a white-box neural network called ReduNet has been proposed, which employs the maximal coding rate reduction (MCR$^2$) principle to transform raw data into low-dimensional discriminative features via a forward layer-wise construction process. Unlike traditional deep networks that rely on backpropagation, ReduNet explicitly derives the parameters of each layer from the features of its preceding layer, offering a mathematically interpretable paradigm. However, this layer-wise construction often requires a large number of layers for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value, which increases the parameter storage of the unfolded module. To address this issue, we propose LA-ReduNet, a lightweight adaptive architecture that refines the layer-wise update rule and enables discriminative feature representations to be obtained with substantially fewer unfolded layers. Specifically, LA-ReduNet employs hyperspherical manifold learning and adaptive step sizes, thereby reducing by an order of magnitude the number of layers required for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value. Simulation results demonstrate that, while maintaining comparable classification accuracy, LA-ReduNet requires significantly fewer layers for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value. Remarkably, under the considered experimental settings, LA-ReduNet requires only approximately $1/29$ of the parameter storage of the unfolded ReduNet module for the MCR$^2$ objective to reach a stable value.

</details>


### [223] [Reinforcement Learning for Continuous-Time Jump Markov Decision Processes with Applications to Network Dynamic Pricing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20680)
*Huiling Meng,Ningyuan Chen,Xuefeng Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops theoretical foundations and algorithms for q-learning in Continuous-Time Jump Markov Decision Processes (CTJMDPs) with general discrete state spaces, addressing a gap in existing continuous-time RL methods that require Euclidean space structures. The approach uses entropy-regularized control with stochastic policies and demonstrates effective learning in network dynamic pricing applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing continuous-time RL techniques (like q-learning for controlled diffusions) are limited to continuous state spaces τd and rely on semimartingale theory in Euclidean spaces. These methods cannot be directly applied to CTJMDPs with general discrete state spaces, which may lack the algebraic addition and subtraction structures inherent to Euclidean spaces. The motivation is to bridge this gap and enable q-learning for general discrete state spaces in CTJMDPs.

Method: The paper (1) formulates an entropy-regularized continuous-time control problem with stochastic policies to model the exploration-exploitation tradeoff; (2) establishes theoretical foundations of q-learning for CTJMDPs with general discrete state spaces; (3) develops model-free q-learning algorithms that avoid naive time discretization and discrete-time MDP approximation; (4) validates through numerical experiments in network dynamic pricing.

Result: The proposed RL algorithm reliably learns near-optimal policies and consistently outperforms standard benchmark methods in network dynamic pricing problems. It demonstrates superior solution quality and effective scalability to large-scale network instances.

Conclusion: The developed q-learning framework successfully extends continuous-time RL to CTJMDPs with general discrete state spaces, providing a theoretically grounded and empirically effective approach for applications like multi-product dynamic pricing with capacitated resources.

Abstract: We study reinforcement learning (RL) in Continuous-Time Jump Markov Decision Processes (CTJMDPs) featuring general discrete state spaces (which need not possess a vector space structure) and continuous/discrete action spaces. The setup covers many well-known applications in operations such as multi-product dynamic pricing with capacitated resources (Gallego and van Ryzin 1997). To model the exploration-exploitation tradeoff, we formulate an entropy-regularized continuous-time control problem with stochastic policies. Recent continuous-time RL techniques such as $q$-learning for controlled diffusions in (Jia and Zhou 2023) focus on continuous state spaces $\mathbb{R}^d$ and rely heavily on semimartingale theory in $\mathbb{R}^d$ for their theoretical analysis. Consequently, their methods cannot be directly applied to CTJMDPs with general discrete state spaces, which may lack the algebraic addition and subtraction structures inherent to Euclidean spaces. To bridge this gap, we establish the theoretical foundations of $q$-learning for CTJMDPs and develop model-free $q$-learning algorithms. Compared to naïve time discretization and approximating CTJMDPs using discrete-time MDPs, our approach has several conceptual and empirical benefits. Numerical experiments in network dynamic pricing (Gallego and van Ryzin 1997) show that our proposed RL algorithm reliably learns near-optimal policies and consistently outperforms standard benchmark methods, demonstrating superior solution quality and effective scalability to large-scale network instances.

</details>


### [224] [Geometric Regularization for Long-Tailed Semi-Supervised Learning via Gaussian Feature Bridges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20710)
*Hongyang He,Xinyuan Song,Yan Zhong,Daizong Liu,Yanbin Li,Yang-fan He,Wenqiao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GBC addresses long-tailed semi-supervised learning by using semantic interpolation paths between unlabeled samples and class prototypes, with a dynamic Prototype Atlas, Gaussian Feature Bridge for smooth trajectory traversal, bridge consistency loss, and BridgeMix for feature mixing. Experiments on CIFAR10-LT and ImageNet-LT validate its effectiveness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world SSL faces challenges with long-tailed label distributions (imbalanced classes) and noisy pseudo-labels, which hinder generalization and amplify confirmation bias. Existing SSL methods struggle under these realistic conditions.

Method: 1) Dynamic Prototype Atlas storing labeled and pseudo-labeled exemplars per class. 2) Class-conditional Gaussian Feature Bridge in latent space forming smooth trajectories from uncertain predictions to reliable class prototypes. 3) Bridge consistency loss enforcing alignment with geometrically interpolated target distribution. 4) BridgeMix: confidence-aware feature mixing strategy interpolating sample-anchor pairs.

Result: Extensive experiments on CIFAR10-LT and ImageNet-LT (USB benchmarks) show GBC consistently improves long tail-class performance without sacrificing scalability.

Conclusion: GBC provides a robust and effective solution for realistic long-tailed SSL settings, successfully addressing the challenges of class imbalance and noisy pseudo-labels while maintaining scalability.

Abstract: Real-world semi-supervised learning (SSL) often encounters significant challenges with long-tailed label distributions and noisy pseudo-labels, which hinder generalization and amplify confirmation bias. In this work, we introduce a novel framework, Gaussian Bridge Consistency (GBC), to address these challenges by constructing semantic interpolation paths between unlabeled samples and high-quality class anchors. Our method maintains a dynamic Prototype Atlas that stores a diverse and evolving set of labeled and pseudo-labeled exemplars per class. For each unlabeled instance, GBC forms a class-conditional Gaussian Feature Bridge in the latent space, enabling the student model to traverse a smooth trajectory from uncertain predictions to reliable class prototypes. A bridge consistency loss is applied along this path to enforce alignment with a geometrically interpolated target distribution. Furthermore, we propose BridgeMix, a confidence-aware feature mixing strategy that interpolates both sample and anchor pairs to amplify cross-sample generalization. Extensive experiments on CIFAR10-LT and ImageNet-LT (USB benchmarks) validate the robustness and effectiveness of GBC under realistic long-tailed SSL settings, consistently improving long tail-class performance without sacrificing scalability.

</details>


### [225] [Hidden Axis of Uncertainty: Latent-Posterior Alignment in Graph Neural Networks with Bayesian Output Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20758)
*Suk Hoon Choi,Damdae Park,Junhyuk Choi,Hyein Jung,Changsoo Kim,Ung Lee,Kyeongsu Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates uncertainty quantification in Graph Neural Networks with Bayesian output layers and discovers a new mechanism called Latent-Posterior Alignment (LPA), where predictive uncertainty decreases as latent representations shift toward lower-variance posterior directions, challenging the conventional belief that uncertainty reduction comes solely from posterior contraction. Based on this insight, the authors propose Alignment-Guided Learning (AGL) to explicitly promote alignment during training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional theory attributes uncertainty reduction in Bayesian Neural Networks to posterior contraction, but this assumption may not hold for deep models. The authors investigate this mechanism in GNNs with Bayesian output layers, where they observe unexpected uncertainty dynamics that challenge existing theory.

Method: The authors conducted interventional experiments on Graph Neural Networks with Bayesian output layers to examine how predictive uncertainty is shaped. They identified Latent-Posterior Alignment (LPA) as a mechanism where uncertainty decreases through shifts in latent representations toward lower-variance posterior directions, independent of posterior variance contraction. Based on this discovery, they proposed Alignment-Guided Learning (AGL), which explicitly promotes alignment during training.

Result: AGL effectively reduces predictive uncertainty while preserving model accuracy and improves structural calibration, ensuring model confidence faithfully reflects underlying data density. The findings demonstrate that uncertainty dynamics in deep Bayesian models depend on the geometric interplay between latent and parameter spaces rather than just posterior magnitude.

Conclusion: This work provides a new perspective on uncertainty dynamics in GNNs with mean-field Bayesian output layers, shifting the focus from the magnitude of the posterior to the geometric relationship between latent and parameter spaces. The proposed LPA mechanism and AGL method offer a principled approach for improving uncertainty quantification in graph-based deep learning models.

Abstract: Bayesian Neural Networks (BNNs) with Bayesian output layers provide a principled and tractable framework for quantifying predictive uncertainty, yet the mechanisms shaping that uncertainty remain unclear. While conventional theory attributes uncertainty reduction to posterior contraction, the corresponding assumptions need not hold for deep models. In the Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) with Bayesian output layers studied here, we observe that predictive uncertainty decreases as latent representations shift toward lower-variance posterior directions, even though the posterior variance does not contract. We term this behavior Latent-Posterior Alignment (LPA) and conduct interventional experiments that support its functional role in shaping predictive uncertainty. Building on this insight, we propose Alignment-Guided Learning (AGL), which explicitly promotes this alignment during training. AGL effectively reduces predictive uncertainty while preserving accuracy and improves structural calibration, ensuring that the model confidence faithfully mirrors underlying data density. These findings provide a new perspective on uncertainty dynamics in GNNs with mean-field Bayesian output layers, shifting the focus from the magnitude of the posterior to the geometric interplay between latent and parameter spaces.

</details>


### [226] [Fuzzy-MoE: Interpretable Regime-Conditioned Expert Routing for Non-Stationary Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20761)
*Lan Guo,Jie Xiao,Zhao Su,Jun Shen,Haoran Li,Weixia Ma,Qingguo Zhou,Binbin Yong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fuzzy-MoE is a fuzzy logic-based Mixture-of-Experts model that reformulates time series forecasting as latent temporal state identification and interpretable expert routing. It addresses limitations of existing deep forecasting models that compress heterogeneous dynamics into unified mappings, lacking interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In non-stationary multivariate time series, different variables and samples exhibit heterogeneous latent dynamic states. Existing deep forecasting models typically compress them into a unified end-to-end mapping, leading to suboptimal modeling of time-varying dynamics and limited interpretability regarding which forecasting mechanism is activated under different latent states.

Method: Fuzzy-MoE consists of multiple parallel expert mapping networks and a dual-view fuzzy router. The router jointly exploits local convolutional dynamics and global segmented statistics to infer latent temporal states. It computes expert activation strengths through learnable Gaussian membership functions, enabling explicit IF-THEN rule-based expert selection. This allows different variables within the same sequence to activate different experts.

Result: Experimental results on multiple public time series benchmark datasets show that Fuzzy-MoE significantly outperforms mainstream forecasting methods in forecasting accuracy. Fuzzy memberships and rule activations provide interpretable routing diagnostics, demonstrating effectiveness in both forecasting performance and mechanism transparency.

Conclusion: Unlike traditional MoE models that use black-box routing, Fuzzy-MoE's routing is based on clear, interpretable fuzzy rules. This makes the expert selection transparent and traceable, effectively capturing heterogeneous temporal dynamics while improving model interpretability.

Abstract: In non-stationary multivariate time series, different variables and samples often exhibit heterogeneous latent dynamic states, while existing deep forecasting models usually compress them into a unified end-to-end mapping, leading to suboptimal modeling of time-varying dynamics and limited interpretability regarding which forecasting mechanism is activated under different latent states. To overcome these limitations, we reformulate time series forecasting as a unified framework of latent temporal state identification and interpretable expert routing, and propose Fuzzy-MoE, a fuzzy logic-based dynamic Mixture-of-Experts model. Fuzzy-MoE consists of multiple parallel expert mapping networks and a dual-view fuzzy router. By jointly exploiting local convolutional dynamics and global segmented statistics, the router infers latent temporal states and computes expert activation strengths through learnable Gaussian membership functions, enabling explicit IF-THEN rule-based expert selection. This fine-grained routing strategy allows different variables within the same sequence to activate different experts, effectively capturing heterogeneous temporal dynamics while improving model interpretability. Experimental results on multiple public time series benchmark datasets show that Fuzzy-MoE significantly outperforms mainstream forecasting methods in forecasting accuracy. Moreover, fuzzy memberships and rule activations provide interpretable routing diagnostics, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed framework in both forecasting performance and mechanism transparency. Unlike traditional MoE models that use black-box routing, Fuzzy-MoE`s routing is based on clear, interpretable fuzzy rules. This makes the expert selection transparent and traceable.

</details>


### [227] [Resolution-Consistent Greedy Neural Approximation on Infinite-Dimensional Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20812)
*Pablo M. Berná,Antonio Falcó,Diego Mondéjar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops theoretical guarantees for shallow neural networks handling infinite-dimensional inputs observed through finite coordinates. It introduces a parameter-normalized neural dictionary framework to analyze approximation error, proposing a fully-corrective greedy procedure with uniform statistical complexity across input resolutions. The framework extends to Hilbert-valued responses without output dimension dependence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish constructive approximation and learning guarantees for neural models dealing with infinite-dimensional inputs that are only observed through finitely many coordinates, addressing the theoretical gap in understanding how infinite-dimensional signal processing via finite observations interacts with neural network approximation.

Method: Uses a parameter-normalized neural dictionary and its associated weighted variation class. Approximation error is decomposed into distribution-dependent coordinate-truncation and greedy finite-width terms. Implements a fully-corrective greedy procedure for empirical regression, extending the framework to Hilbert-valued responses.

Result: The approximation error separates into two components: a distribution-dependent coordinate-truncation term and a greedy finite-width term. The fully-corrective greedy procedure yields population guarantees with statistical complexity uniform in retained input resolution. Dimension-free statements are achieved for Hilbert-valued responses without explicit dependence on output dimension.

Conclusion: The framework provides theoretical foundations for infinite-dimensional neural approximation with finite observations, though computational challenges remain (nonconvex parameter-search problems). The quasi-Polish construction serves as a motivating example, with synthetic experiments validating the predicted resolution, width, and sample-size regimes.

Abstract: We develop constructive approximation and learning guarantees for shallow neural models with infinite-dimensional inputs observed through finitely many coordinates. The analysis is based on a parameter-normalized neural dictionary and its associated weighted variation class. Within this class, the approximation error separates into a distribution-dependent coordinate-truncation term and a greedy finite-width term. For empirical regression, a fully-corrective greedy procedure yields population guarantees whose statistical complexity is uniform in the retained input resolution. The same framework extends to Hilbert-valued responses without an explicit dependence on the output dimension. The dimension-free statements are statistical, not computational: selecting a new neuron still requires solving a nonconvex parameter-search problem. The quasi-Polish construction underlying recent infinite-dimensional universal approximation results provides a motivating example, and synthetic experiments illustrate the predicted resolution, width, and sample-size regimes.

</details>


### [228] [Scaling Muon for Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20818)
*Chenghao Li,Xiao Han,Xinxin Huang,Wei Liu,Boyang Li,Bing Xiao,Heran Zhang,Juanma Perez Rua,Ke Xu,Kangning Liu,Linjun Kuang,Na Li,Tan Wang,Tian Xie,Wei Peng,Yang Pei,Yifan Xu,Yuanhao Zhai,Yuwei Lin,Zhe Wang,Zihao He,Daniel Li,Junbiao Tang,Ziyang Jiang,Dake Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Periodic Row-wise Muon, an optimized version of the matrix-aware optimizer Muon for training large Diffusion Transformers (DiTs). The method performs full spectral updates periodically while using low-cost row-wise updates for intermediate steps, preserving Muon's generative quality advantages (12.9-19.1% improvement over AdamW) while significantly reducing computational overhead (46.9-54.3% optimizer time reduction, 15.7-24.3% end-to-end step time reduction).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While Muon improves large model training by balancing updates across singular directions, its scaling behavior and end-to-end efficiency on large DiTs remained unclear. At scale, the 5-step Newton-Schulz iteration and full-momentum materialization introduce substantial computation and communication overhead that can offset Muon's step-efficiency advantage.

Method: The authors (1) established Muon's scaling behavior on DiTs from 1.3B to 15B parameters; (2) introduced Periodic Row-wise Muon, which performs full NS5 spectral update once every K steps and applies low-cost row-wise constrained update based on current momentum at remaining steps; (3) co-designed distributed implementation operating on sharded momentum during non-refresh steps with bucketed all-gather and communication-computation overlap.

Result: Muon improves best generative quality over AdamW by 12.9-19.1%. Periodic Row-wise Muon remains within 0.5% quality on 1.3B-4B models and improves by 4.5% at 9B. It reduces optimizer time by 46.9-54.3%, end-to-end step time by 15.7-24.3%, and logical communication volume by 66.7%, while reaching best quality with 33.7-64.8% less active training time.

Conclusion: Periodic Row-wise Muon preserves Muon's generative quality advantage while translating it into end-to-end training efficiency for large DiTs, demonstrating that the optimization method scales effectively from 1.3B to 15B parameters.

Abstract: The matrix-aware optimizer Muon improves large model training by balancing updates across singular directions, yet its scaling behavior and end-to-end efficiency on large Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) remain unclear. We first establish Muon's scaling behavior on DiTs from 1.3B to 15B parameters, showing that its optimization and generative quality advantages over AdamW persist across model scales. However, at scale, the 5-step Newton--Schulz iteration (NS5) performed at every optimization step, together with full-momentum materialization, introduces substantial computation and communication overhead that can offset Muon's step-efficiency advantage. We introduce \emph{Periodic Row-wise Muon}, which performs a full NS5 spectral update once every \(K\) steps and applies a low compute and communication cost row-wise constrained update based on the current momentum at the remaining steps. We further co-design a distributed implementation that operates directly on sharded momentum during non-refresh steps and accelerates spectral refreshes through bucketed all-gather and communication--computation overlap. Across all scales, Muon improves the best observed generative quality over AdamW by 12.9--19.1\%. Compared with vanilla Muon, Periodic Row-wise Muon remains within 0.5\% in best generative quality on the 1.3B--4B models and improves it by 4.5\% at 9B. It reduces optimizer time by 46.9--54.3\%, end-to-end step time by 15.7--24.3\%, and logical communication volume by 66.7\%, while reaching its respective best generative quality with 33.7--64.8\% less active training time. These results show that Periodic Row-wise Muon preserves Muon's generative quality advantage while translating it into end-to-end training efficiency for large DiTs.

</details>


### [229] [Nothing Changed but the Model: CellFill -- Bounded In-Cell Learning for Bit-Identical, Revocable Updates to Quantized LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20873)
*Zifeng Liu,Zhiyong Du,Yaxin Lu,Yiming Mao,Zhenhe Wang,Wenqi Shi,Zhengkun Jing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a method for updating quantized language models by learning knowledge inside the "dequantization gap" - writing new information into per-weight residuals within quantization decision cells while keeping the original 4-bit weights frozen. This approach preserves the original model bit-for-bit, making updates revocable and bounding drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for teaching new knowledge to deployed language models (full fine-tuning, adapter merging, model editing) require replacing the released checkpoint, which breaks all existing evaluations and caches that reference those exact weights. The authors seek a way to update models without modifying the original quantized weights.

Method: The method learns inside the dequantization gap: with integer codes and scales of a 4-bit release frozen, new knowledge is written into the per-weight residual within each quantization decision cell. Re-quantization returns the released artifact bit-for-bit. The authors propose CellFill, a bounded reparameterization that makes invariance structural rather than enforced.

Result: Exact invariance is nearly free: the constrained dense path achieves 58.9% fact recall vs 59.3% for unconstrained reference (paired difference -0.5 points, 95% CI [-5.0,+4.0]), with better cross-domain perplexity. Projection into quantization cells reduces cross-domain forgetting in every converged run. The method scales to 27B hybrid linear-attention model (2.4e10 constrained weights), where matched recall costs about half as much cross-domain perplexity as at 1.7B.

Conclusion: The approach provides a machine-checkable guarantee that updates are exactly revocable by dropping the residual, with bounded drift. Exact invariance comes at nearly no cost. Different methods trade off knowledge acquisition against cross-domain perplexity differently, and in-domain perplexity improvements past the anchor are artifacts of rehearsal sharing a corpus with the metric.

Abstract: Every way of teaching a deployed language model something new -- full fine-tuning, adapter merging, model editing -- replaces the released checkpoint, and with it every evaluation and cache that referred to those exact bits. We instead learn inside the dequantization gap: with the integer codes and scales of a 4-bit release frozen, new knowledge is written only into the per-weight residual that lives strictly inside each quantization decision cell. Re-quantization then returns the released artifact bit-for-bit, a machine-checkable guarantee; updates are exactly revocable by dropping the residual; and drift is bounded. We give six propositions and three training paths, including CellFill, a bounded reparameterization that makes invariance structural rather than enforced.
  Exact invariance turns out to be nearly free: across three paired seeds the constrained dense path matches an unconstrained reference whose weights provably escape the artifact (58.9 vs 59.3 percent fact recall; paired difference -0.5 points, 95% CI [-5.0,+4.0]), and is better on held-out cross-domain perplexity. Against the natural null hypothesis -- serving the same update as an unmerged adapter -- projecting into the cells reduces cross-domain forgetting in every run that converged, and a diverged control shows the boundary: projection is a trust region, not a repair. What no method escapes is the cost of knowledge itself, and the apparent free lunch of in-domain perplexity improving past the anchor is an artifact of rehearsal sharing a corpus with the metric. Methods differ threefold at matched rehearsal in knowledge bought per point of cross-domain perplexity, a ranking that is not the recall ranking. The method transfers to a 27B hybrid linear-attention model (2.4e10 constrained weights, verified bit-identical), where matched recall costs about half as much cross-domain perplexity as at 1.7B.

</details>


### [230] [Decoupling Policy Extraction for Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20909)
*Xuyao Lin,Yixiang Shan,Jinru Duan,Tao Yang,Xinyu Zhao,Runyu Lei,Yiming Zhao,Jiaxin Fan,Zongbao Feng,Peng Jia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a decoupled policy extraction paradigm for offline reinforcement learning, where the actor is trained solely to model behavior distribution and policy improvement is performed at inference time by reranking actor-generated proposals with a separately learned critic. This approach addresses the fundamental limitation of coupled actor-critic training in offline RL, where actor improvement cannot generate new data to validate the critic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The conventional offline RL paradigm jointly trains actor and critic, inheriting the coupled approach from online RL. However, this is problematic in offline settings because training data remains fixed - actor-side policy improvement cannot generate new data to validate or correct the critic.
