This leads to two challenges: (1) actor updates can drift toward high-valued but out-of-distribution (OOD) actions, amplifying critic overestimation, and (2) conservative value estimation or behavior-cloning regularization creates a trade-off between suppressing OOD actions and selecting high-value actions within the data-supported region.

Method: The authors propose decoupling policy improvement from actor training. Under the decoupled policy extraction paradigm: (1) The actor is trained solely to model the behavior distribution (similar to behavior cloning), and (2) Policy improvement is performed at inference time by reranking multiple actor-generated proposals with a separately learned critic. This ensures the actor provides behavior-supported action candidates while the critic performs value-based selection within this candidate set.

Result: Extensive experiments show that the decoupled policy extraction paradigm outperforms both behavior cloning and jointly learned offline RL methods, while remaining effective even with a naive Q-learning critic.

Conclusion: The decoupled policy extraction paradigm offers a promising alternative to conventional offline RL methods by separating behavior modeling from value-based selection. This approach effectively addresses the OOD action problem and the trade-off between suppression and selection by ensuring all evaluated actions remain within the behavior-supported region.

Abstract: Offline RL methods commonly jointly train the actor and critic, where the critic is used to guide the actor toward higher-value actions. This coupled learning process is well motivated in online RL, where an improved actor collects new data that can further update the actor and the critic. However, training data remains fixed in offline RL, making actor-side policy improvement unable to generate new data to validate or correct the critic. Moreover, retaining this coupled paradigm leads to two related challenges. Firstly, actor updates can drift toward high-valued but potentially out-of-distribution (OOD) actions and amplify critic overestimation. Secondly, conservative value estimation or behavior-cloning regularization creates a difficult trade-off between suppressing OOD actions and selecting high-value actions within the data-supported region. Motivated by this observation, we revisit the conventional offline RL paradigm and propose decoupling policy improvement from actor training. Specifically, we train the actor solely to model the behavior distribution and perform policy improvement at inference time by reranking multiple actor-generated proposals with a separately learned critic. We refer to this paradigm as the decoupled policy extraction paradigm. Under such paradigm, the actor provides behavior-supported action candidates, while the critic performs value-based selection within this candidate set. Extensive experiments show that the decoupled policy extraction paradigm outperforms both behavior cloning and jointly learned offline RL methods, while remaining effective even with a naive Q-learning critic.

</details>


### [231] [Training, learning and inference: unified dynamics of neural systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20965)
*Mian Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a Generation-Fact Graph (GFG) framework that records atomic generation facts preserving scientific generation histories, combined with a unified training-learning dynamics model using nanoGPT. The approach achieves 91.43% accuracy and 91.49% macro-averaged recall on held-out runs, with experiments confirming the framework extends beyond nanoGPT to ResNet and diffusion models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to create an 'AI-native, compilable scientific fact substrate' that preserves generation histories, enabling a recursive scientific process where analysis, intervention, replay, and validation form facts for later cycles. They seek to establish unified training-learning dynamics that can predict training outcomes before post-update outputs are available.

Method: The paper defines atomic generation facts f=(u,tau,omega,z;rho) capturing origin, realized transformation, concrete occurrence, generated result, and relation role. These are compiled into a Generation-Fact Graph (GFG). Using nanoGPT, they establish training-learning dynamics where training involves parameter-optimizer evolution with state and memory, while learning involves persistent reorganization of distributed functional support. Three primary coordinates (target-boundary state, target-specific update geometry, parameter-Adam receiving state) yield a second-order predictor operating before post-update outputs. Inference is treated as frozen projection of training-learning dynamics, with component gating and rollback showing causal recruitment.

Result: The second-order predictor achieved 91.43% accuracy and 91.49% macro-averaged recall across four transitions on held-out runs. Experiments on ResNet/CIFAR-100 and diffusion/CIFAR-10 confirmed receiving-state-conditioned responses, persistent support reorganization, and frozen inference projection beyond nanoGPT. Controlled feedback indicated possible double-edged reinforcement effects.

Conclusion: The GFG framework provides an AI-native approach to preserving scientific generation histories. The unified training-learning dynamics with three primary coordinates enables accurate prediction of training outcomes. The approach generalizes beyond nanoGPT to other architectures (ResNet) and paradigms (diffusion models), suggesting broad applicability of the receiving-state-conditioned response and frozen inference projection concepts.

Abstract: We define an atomic generation fact f=(u,tau,omega,z;rho), recording the origin, realized transformation, concrete occurrence, generated result and relation role. Compiled into a Generation-Fact Graph (GFG), these facts provide an AI-native, compilable scientific fact substrate preserving generation histories. We establish a GFG-based recursive scientific process in which analysis, intervention, replay and validation form facts for later cycles. Using nanoGPT, we establish unified training-learning dynamics. Training is the evolution of a parameter-optimizer system with state and memory: each actual training action enters the receiving state and produces a finite-amplitude nonlinear functional response conditioned by that state and target-specific update geometry. Learning is the persistent reorganization of distributed functional support by these responses; capability formation, maintenance, decline or recovery becomes observable when target-specific states are evaluated against their readout boundaries. Three primary coordinates - target-boundary state, target-specific update geometry and parameter-Adam receiving state - yield a second-order predictor operating before post-update outputs are read. On held-out runs, it achieved 91.43% accuracy and 91.49% macro-averaged recall across four transitions. We further establish inference as a frozen projection of training-learning dynamics. Component gating and rollback show causal recruitment and non-additive combination of query-conditioned support formed during training, deriving organizational conditions realized by Attention. Controlled feedback indicates possible double-edged reinforcement effects. ResNet/CIFAR-100 and diffusion/CIFAR-10 experiments confirm receiving-state-conditioned responses, persistent support reorganization and frozen inference projection beyond nanoGPT.

</details>


### [232] [A Critical Audit of Spatiotemporal Forecasting Benchmark Datasets and Baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20980)
*Kenneth Martin,Simon Heilig,Asja Fischer,Michel F. C. Haddad,Adam M. Sykulski,Moshe Eliasof*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates why simple spatially-unaware linear models perform competitively against GNNs on standard benchmark datasets (Chickenpox, PedalMe, WikiMaths, METR-LA, PEMS-BAY) for spatial-temporal time series forecasting. Through classical time series analysis, the authors uncover that these widely-used datasets have structural biases, particularly from first-order differencing, that limit their discriminative reliability for method comparison.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors observe that while many GNN methods have been developed for short-range forecasting on multivariate time series with spatial graph structure, baseline methods (historical averages, classical ML) show competitive performance against GNNs. This raises questions about whether the benchmark datasets are truly discriminative for evaluating method innovations.

Method: The authors apply classical time series statistical analysis methods to the benchmark datasets. They develop a toolset for identifying significant spatial and temporal correlations, and analyze the effect of first-order differencing (common preprocessing step) on the data structure.

Result: The analysis reveals that spatially-unaware linear models pose a stronger competitor than previously reported. A structural bias was identified, introduced by first-order differenced datasets. This bias undermines the discriminative reliability of the widely adopted benchmark datasets.

Conclusion: The authors recommend reducing over-reliance on these benchmark datasets for method comparison and advocate for more rigorous statistical evaluation. They demonstrate that their analytical methodology can lead to novel GNN model development by applying it to create a simple hybrid model, offering a new path forward for the field.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are routinely employed for short-range forecasting on multivariate time series with a spatial graph structure. Despite the availability of many alternative datasets, method innovations within this domain are predominantly assessed against a rather limited set of benchmark datasets, most notably Chickenpox, PedalMe, WikiMaths, METR-LA, and PEMS-BAY. The evaluation protocols contain baselines spanning from historical averages to classical machine learning approaches. These baselines often show competitive performance compared to GNNs. In the present work, we take a step back and analyse the benchmark datasets via classical time series methods to uncover why spatially-unaware linear models pose a stronger competitor than previously reported, casting further doubt on the discriminative reliability of the aforementioned widely adopted datasets. Our statistical analysis provides a toolset for identifying significant spatial and temporal correlations, while revealing a structural bias introduced by first-order differenced datasets. We therefore recommend reducing the over-reliance on such datasets for method comparison, and instead advocate for more rigorous statistical evaluation. By applying the results of our analysis to a simple hybrid model, we show how our methodology can lead to novel ways of developing GNN models

</details>


### [233] [Jacobian-guided Noise Injection for Quantization Robustness in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20988)
*Deepanshu Pandey,Arnav Chavan,Nahush Lele,Sankalp Dayal,Deepak Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses LLM quantization instability caused by the self-attention mechanism's sensitivity to discretization errors. The authors identify the softmax operator as the key bottleneck due to its outlier sensitivity and state-dependent Jacobian. They propose Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection, which adds Gaussian noise to pre-attention logits with variance derived from the Jacobian Frobenius norm, providing a principled approach to determine optimal noise levels based on local attention sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantization of LLMs is challenging because the self-attention mechanism is highly sensitive to discretization errors. Existing Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) methods struggle with this sensitivity. The core issue lies in the softmax operator, which exhibits outlier sensitivity and a state-dependent Jacobian that amplifies quantization-induced errors.

Method: The authors theoretically establish that suppressing the Jacobian Frobenius norm helps bound quantization-induced performance degradation. Based on this, they propose Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection (JAGNI) - a training strategy that injects zero-mean Gaussian noise into pre-attention logits. The noise variance is directly derived from the Jacobian Frobenius norm, providing a principled way to determine optimal noise levels based on local attention sensitivity, unlike prior heuristic-based approaches.

Result: The method demonstrates significant improvements across benchmarks: up to +37% relative gains on Top-1 accuracy on ImageNet-1K for SigLIP, and up to 40% improved relative perplexity on WikiText for language models in low-bit quantization settings. The approach shows improved robustness over state-of-the-art PTQ methods on various LLM architectures.

Conclusion: The paper provides a theoretically grounded solution to the quantization instability problem in LLMs. By deriving optimal noise variance from the Jacobian Frobenius norm, Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection offers a principled alternative to heuristic approaches. The substantial performance gains in both vision (SigLIP) and language (WikiText) tasks demonstrate the method's broad applicability and effectiveness in low-bit quantization scenarios.

Abstract: Quantization of Large Language Models (LLMs) is often hindered by the sensitivity of the self-attention mechanism to discretization errors. We identify the softmax operator as a bottleneck for quantization stability due to its sensitivity to outliers and state-dependent Jacobian. We theoretically establish that suppressing the norm of this Jacobian helps in bounding quantization-induced performance degradation. Based on this, we propose Jacobian-Guided Noise Injection, a training strategy that injects zero-mean Gaussian noise into pre-attention logits, with variance derived directly from the Jacobian Frobenius norm. Unlike prior approaches that rely on heuristic or penalise jacobian directly, our method provides a way to identify the optimal noise variance based on the local attention sensitivity. We evaluate the method on SOTA LLM architectures, where it demonstrates improved robustness over popular PTQ methods. Empirical analysis reveals that the proposed method gives up to +37% relative gains on Top-1 accuracy on ImageNet-1K for SigLIP and improves relative perplexity by upto 40% on WikiText for language models in low bit quantisation settings, proving the efficacy of the approach.

</details>


### [234] [Trojaning the Alignment: Stealthy Backdoor Attacks against Graph Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20991)
*Minhua Lin,Zhicheng Gao,Yilong Wang,Hanqing Lu,Xiang Zhang,Suhang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces STAG, a novel backdoor attack framework targeting Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) on Text-Attributed Graphs (TAGs). It coordinates graph-trigger generators with text-side soft prompts to achieve stealthy trojan attacks at the graph-language alignment interface.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The backdoor vulnerability of GFMs on TAGs remains insufficiently understood, particularly under graph-language alignment where graph and text representations constrain each other. Existing backdoor attacks only target one modality (graph or text) independently, making them ineffective against aligned systems. Additionally, TAGs pose unique stealth challenges as triggers are exposed in both node text and local graph structure.

Method: STAG coordinates two components: (1) a graph-trigger generator that creates trigger nodes, and (2) a text-side soft prompt. These work together so that trigger-attached graph representations and triggered text representations move toward the same target-class text region. For stealthiness, STAG uses candidate retrieval to generate readable text for trigger nodes and regularizes the trigger-attached subgraph to maintain local structure similarity to the original subgraph.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple TAG datasets and representative GFMs demonstrate the effectiveness and stealthiness of STAG.

Conclusion: STAG represents a novel stealthy trojan attack framework specifically designed for the graph-language alignment interface of GFMs on TAGs, successfully coordinating graph and text modalities while maintaining stealth through readable text triggers and structural regularization.

Abstract: Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) on text-attributed graphs (TAGs) align graph representations with language semantics to support transferable graph learning. Despite these advantages, the backdoor vulnerability of GFMs on TAGs remains insufficiently understood, especially under graph-language alignment, where graph and text representations are trained to constrain each other in a shared semantic space. Existing backdoor attacks mainly target either the graph side or the text side, treating the two modalities independently. This makes direct adaptation ineffective: graph-only triggers can be constrained by clean text semantics, while text-only triggers alter the language view but do not directly shift the graph representation being aligned and scored. TAGs also impose a stealth challenge because triggers are exposed as both node text and local graph structure, making incoherent trigger attributes or anomalous subgraphs easy to inspect or filter. In this paper, we propose STAG, a stealthy trojan attack framework designed for the graph-language alignment interface of GFMs on TAGs. STAG coordinates a graph-trigger generator with a text-side soft prompt so that trigger-attached graph representations and triggered text representations move toward the same target-class text region. To address TAG-specific stealthiness, STAG realizes trigger nodes as readable text through candidate retrieval and regularizes the trigger-attached subgraph so that its local structure remains close to the original subgraph. Extensive experiments on multiple TAG datasets and representative GFMs demonstrate the effectiveness and stealthiness of STAG. Our code is available at https://github.com/ventr1c/STAG.

</details>


### [235] [Free-Probability Kernels for Zero-Rollout Hyperparameter Selection in Reservoir Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20998)
*Sara Malacarne,Andrea Ceni,Claudio Gallicchio*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a deterministic, pilot-informed method for selecting hyperparameters in reservoir computing (RC) without requiring extensive simulations. Using free probability theory, the method derives cross-lag propagation coefficients that form a temporal kernel, enabling kernel ridge regression on a short pilot sequence to rank candidate operating regimes. This approach avoids expensive rollouts while achieving comparable performance to exhaustive search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reservoir computing efficiency is compromised during hyperparameter selection, as the recurrent gain, input scale, and leakage rate typically require many rollouts to tune. This bottleneck motivates a method to select hyperparameters without instantiating or rolling out the reservoir.

Method: The authors use free probability theory to derive cross-lag propagation coefficients that summarize how the reservoir mixes past inputs. In the large-width limit, these coefficients define a deterministic temporal kernel approximating the finite-reservoir feature geometry. Kernel ridge regression on a short labelled pilot sequence ranks candidate operating regimes without reservoir instantiation or rollout.

Result: On ten synthetic temporal benchmarks, zero-rollout selection achieves a mean deployment score of 0.772 vs. 0.774 for exhaustive search, avoiding 156,600 rollouts. With small rollout budgets, the method provides strongest mean performance at every tested budget, reaching exhaustive reference at 4.8% of its rollout cost. On four ETT forecasting datasets, five retained candidates recover the exhaustive operating point on three datasets. On multivariate cellular-traffic forecasting, 15 rollouts per cell match the 462-rollout exhaustive reference and outperform random search and Bayesian optimization at low budgets.

Conclusion: Free-probability kernels serve as effective deterministic surrogates for selecting reservoir operating regimes when validation rollouts are scarce, significantly reducing computational cost while maintaining performance comparable to exhaustive simulation-based search.

Abstract: Reservoir computing (RC) couples a fixed recurrent dynamical system with a trained lightweight readout, but this efficiency is partly lost during hyperparameter selection: the recurrent gain, input scale, and leakage rate determine the reservoir's stability and temporal processing regime and are usually tuned through many rollouts. We introduce a deterministic, pilot-informed selector for leaky linear reservoirs followed by coordinate-wise nonlinear features. Free probability yields cross-lag propagation coefficients that summarize how the reservoir mixes past inputs. In the large-width limit, these coefficients define a deterministic temporal kernel that approximates the finite-reservoir feature geometry. Kernel ridge regression on a short labelled pilot sequence therefore ranks candidate operating regimes without instantiating or rolling out a reservoir, and the selected configuration transfers across widths. Across ten synthetic temporal benchmarks, zero-rollout selection obtains a mean deployment score of $0.772$, compared with $0.774$ for exhaustive simulation-based search, while avoiding $156\,600$ selection rollouts. With a small rollout budget, the proposed ranking provides the strongest mean performance at every tested budget and reaches the exhaustive reference using $4.8\%$ of its rollout cost. On four public electricity-transformer-temperature (ETT) forecasting datasets, five retained candidates recover the exhaustive operating point on three datasets. On multivariate cellular-traffic forecasting, 15 rollouts per cell reach the 462-rollout exhaustive reference and outperform random search and Bayesian optimization at low budgets. These results position free-probability kernels as deterministic surrogates for selecting reservoir operating regimes when validation rollouts are scarce.

</details>


### [236] [RODE: A Radial-Orthogonal Decoupled Engine for Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21024)
*Guoxiang Xu,Bince Qu,Qi Sun,Cheng Zhuo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RODE is a novel optimizer that decouples radial (norm) and directional components of matrix updates, using separate update rules and step sizes for each. It outperforms Muon variants across language modeling and image classification tasks, including at 1.5B and 9B scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern matrix-aware optimizers add conditioned matrix steps directly to weights, jointly changing norm and direction. This interaction is problematic because current norm determines angular motion, while directional learning can drive norm growth and alter later steps unpredictably.

Method: RODE separates radial and directional components with independent update rules: (1) a scalar radial rule explicitly updates the matrix Frobenius norm, and (2) a directional channel performs Newton-Schulz-conditioned updates in the tangent space.

Result: On GPT-2: gains from both direct norm control and directional update. On two language-modeling and two image-classification tasks: RODE outperforms both Muon variants in every comparison and ends with lower full-model norms. At 1.5B scale: loss reduced from 4.145 to 3.346, global norm from 11964 to 2183 (vs Muon RMS); fixed-radius RODE improves further. On Qwen3.5-9B fine-tuning: RODE outperforms both Muon variants on all four evaluation tasks and achieves highest mean on GSM8K and MATH-500.

Conclusion: Decoupling radial and directional dynamics provides a more effective and controllable approach to matrix optimization, enabling better norm control and superior performance across diverse tasks and scales.

Abstract: Modern neural network training increasingly uses matrix-aware optimizers, yet their conditioned matrix step is typically added directly to the weight, jointly changing its norm and direction. This interaction matters because the current norm determines angular motion, while directional learning can drive norm growth and thereby alter later steps. We introduce RODE, which gives the radial and directional components separate update rules and step sizes. RODE explicitly updates the matrix Frobenius norm through a scalar radial rule, while its directional channel performs Newton--Schulz-conditioned updates in the tangent space. Controlled GPT-2 interventions show gains from both direct norm control and RODE's directional update. Across two language-modeling and two image-classification tasks, RODE outperforms both Muon variants in every direct comparison and ends with lower full-model norms. At 1.5B scale, using the learning rate transferred directly from the Qwen2-style LM sweep, RODE lowers loss from 4.145 to 3.346 and final global norm from 11964 to 2183 relative to Muon RMS, with fixed-radius RODE improving further. For Qwen3.5-9B full-parameter fine-tuning, all six optimizers use the same tuning budget and the same formal-training and evaluation settings; RODE outperforms both Muon variants on all four evaluation tasks and attains the highest mean on GSM8K and MATH-500. Thus, decoupling radial and directional dynamics offers a more effective and controllable approach to matrix optimization.

</details>


### [237] [Designing a Robust LLM-Based Evaluation System for Agentic AI in Drug Discovery Through Human Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21057)
*Emma Granqvist,Rocío Mercado,Samuel Genheden*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Agentic large language model (LLM) systems are reshaping scientific workflows in chemistry and drug discovery, but evaluating their open-ended, tool-augmented outputs remains a fundamental bottleneck. Reference-based metrics such as BLEU and ROUGE fail to capture semantic correctness, while expert human evaluation does not scale to the iteration speed these systems demand. The LLM-as-a-Judge paradigm has emerged as a scalable alternative, but existing drug discovery benchmarks deploy LLM judges without validating their alignment with human experts. In this work, we present an LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation framework for ChatInvent, an agentic drug discovery assistant deployed at AstraZeneca, with four contributions. First, we define four output-quality evaluation dimensions---Completeness, Relevancy, Structural Clarity, and Scope Adherence---alongside deterministic Tool Call Correctness checks. Second, we validate the judge through a human alignment study with five expert annotators, comparing Gemini 3.1 Pro, Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5, and Llama 3.1 70B as candidate judges. Third, we optimize the best-performing judge using few-shot demonstrations of human-annotated examples, improving alignment with the human majority vote from 0.80 to 0.86. Fourth, applying the optimized judge to 70 held-out questions, we surface concrete limitations and find that informal phrasings do not systematically degrade output quality; if anything, it is helpful to have the LLM rewrite the original question before querying the agent. Our framework provides a reusable template for human-aligned evaluation of agentic systems in scientific domains.

</details>


### [238] [TracingFlow: A Simulation-Free Trajectory Inference Framework Based on Second-Order Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21070)
*Yuhao Sun,Zekun Wu,Zixun Huang,Peijie Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TracingFlow is a novel simulation-free Flow Matching framework that extends optimal transport to second-order dynamics (acceleration-based) for inferring continuous system evolution from sparse temporal snapshots. It addresses limitations of first-order methods in capturing regulatory momentum and time-delayed responses in complex processes like cell differentiation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Optimal Transport frameworks for system dynamics are largely restricted to first-order dynamics with memoryless velocity fields. This limits expressiveness because first-order systems fail to account for regulatory momentum and time-delayed responses inherent in biological processes like cell differentiation. The problem is particularly acute when inferring evolution from sparse temporal snapshots.

Method: TracingFlow uses neural networks to regress the acceleration field (second-order dynamics), providing an exact, efficient solution to the Dynamical Optimal Acceleration Transport (DOAT) problem. Unlike simulation-based approaches, it is simulation-free. By learning underlying force fields rather than velocity fields, it captures high-curvature transitions and nonlinear evolutions. The method also integrates lineage tracing priors for biological plausibility.

Result: Evaluated on complex synthetic and large-scale scRNA-seq datasets, TracingFlow achieves superior accuracy in distributional reconstruction and trajectory faithfulness compared to first-order methods that yield over-smoothed trajectories.

Conclusion: TracingFlow successfully generalizes flow matching to second-order dynamics, enabling more accurate modeling of complex system evolutions. It recovers dynamical structures that are both mathematically optimal (through OT principles) and biologically plausible (through lineage tracing integration), making it particularly valuable for single-cell omics applications.

Abstract: Inferring continuous system evolution from sparse temporal snapshots is a key challenge in generative modeling and single-cell omics. While Optimal Transport (OT) is popular, existing frameworks are largely restricted to first-order dynamics, assuming memoryless velocity fields. This limits expressiveness, as first-order systems fail to account for regulatory momentum and time-delayed responses inherent in processes like cell differentiation. Here, we introduce TracingFlow, a simulation-free Flow Matching framework generalizing to second-order dynamics. By using neural networks to regress the acceleration field, TracingFlow provides an exact, efficient solution to the Dynamical Optimal Acceleration Transport (DOAT) problem. Unlike first-order methods yielding over-smoothed trajectories, our second-order formulation captures high-curvature transitions and nonlinear evolutions by learning the underlying force fields. Evaluated on complex synthetic and large-scale scRNA-seq datasets, TracingFlow achieves superior accuracy in distributional reconstruction and trajectory faithfulness. Moreover, by integrating lineage tracing priors, it recovers dynamical structures that are both mathematically optimal and biologically plausible.

</details>


### [239] [Causal Modeling of Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes via Adaptive LLM Proposals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21079)
*Kavimayil P. Komarasamy,Saurabh Mathur,Ameet Soni,David M. Haas,Kristian Kersting,Sriraam Natarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a neurosymbolic framework that combines Large Language Model (LLM) prior knowledge with empirical data scoring to generate plausible causal hypotheses for Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes (APOs). The method iteratively updates LLM context based on high-scoring graphs to explore promising hypothesis spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding causes of APOs like preterm birth and gestational diabetes remains challenging due to limited data and incomplete domain knowledge. Existing methods (pure data-driven or LLM-only) fail to produce consistent causal findings.

Method: The framework treats the LLM as an adaptive proposal distribution that generates causal hypotheses, which are then scored against empirical data. High-scoring graphs update the LLM's context to guide subsequent generations toward better hypothesis regions. Evaluated on real-world clinical data for APOs.

Result: The method recovers all expert-validated edges and identifies additional plausible causal relations not previously listed by experts.

Conclusion: This neurosymbolic approach successfully integrates LLM knowledge with empirical validation, providing a promising direction for causal discovery in data-sparse domains and potentially offering new insights for targeted interventions in maternal-child health.

Abstract: Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes (APOs) such as preterm birth and gestational diabetes can have long-term consequences for both the mother and child, yet an understanding of their causes remains elusive. Causal discovery in this domain is especially challenging due to a paucity of data and incomplete domain knowledge. As a result, pure data-driven methods fail, and Large Language Model (LLM) outputs remain inconsistent or contradictory. We introduce a neurosymbolic framework for generating plausible causal hypotheses that iteratively combines the broad prior knowledge of LLMs with empirical scoring on data. Our method treats the LLM as an adaptive proposal distribution, generating hypotheses that are scored against empirical data; the resulting high-scoring graphs are then used to update the LLM's context, steering subsequent generations toward more promising regions of the hypothesis space. We evaluate our approach on a real-world clinical dataset for modeling APOs and their risk factors, comparing our results against an expert-constructed causal graph. Our method recovers all expert-validated edges and identifies additional plausible causal relations not previously listed by experts, potentially providing new insights for targeted interventions.

</details>


### [240] [FlatLand: Personalized Graph Federated Learning via Tailored Lorentz Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21096)
*Jiahong Liu,Ram Samarth B B,Xinyu Fu,Menglin Yang,Weixi Zhang,Rex Ying,Irwin King*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlatLand is a personalized federated learning method for graph data that uses hyperbolic geometry (Lorentz space) to handle structurally diverse graphs across clients. It decouples heterogeneous and common knowledge into time-like and space-like parameters for direct aggregation without requiring client similarity estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing personalized federated learning methods ignore the intrinsic geometric properties of diverse graph structures, and highly heterogeneous client data remain challenging in federated learning, especially in graph federated learning where clients possess structurally diverse graphs.

Method: The paper proposes embedding different clients' data in tailored Lorentz space of hyperbolic geometry, leveraging the negative curvature prevalent in real-world graphs and the time-like dimension to encode client-specific heterogeneity. A parameter decoupling strategy separates heterogeneous information (time-like parameters) from common knowledge (space-like parameters), enabling direct aggregation.

Result: FlatLand achieves superior performance on diverse federated graph learning tasks, particularly in low-dimensional settings.

Conclusion: Hyperbolic geometry naturally accommodates the intrinsic negative curvature in real-world graphs, and the parameter decoupling approach eliminates the need for client similarity estimation and extra calculation modules, providing an effective solution for personalized federated graph learning.

Abstract: Federated learning enables privacy-preserving collaborative training, but highly heterogeneous client data remain challenging, especially in graph federated learning where clients possess structurally diverse graphs. Existing personalized federated learning (PFL) methods ignore the intrinsic geometric properties of diverse graph structures. We propose FlatLand, a novel personalized federated learning method that embeds different clients' data in tailored Lorentz space of hyperbolic geometry. Our key insight is that hyperbolic geometry naturally accommodates the intrinsic negative curvature prevalent in real-world graphs, while the time-like dimension in Lorentz space provides a principled way to encode client-specific heterogeneity. We develop a parameter decoupling strategy that separates heterogeneous information (captured in time-like parameters) from common knowledge (preserved in space-like parameters), enabling direct aggregation without requiring client similarity estimation and extra calculation modules. Empirical results on diverse federated graph learning tasks demonstrate that FlatLand achieves superior performance, particularly in low-dimensional settings.

</details>


### [241] [BackDFL: A Unified Benchmark For Backdoor Attacks and Defenses In Decentralized Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21137)
*Mouhamed Amine Bouchiha,Gregory Blanc,Yufei Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents BackDFL, a unified benchmark for evaluating Decentralized Federated Learning (DFL) under realistic adaptive backdoor attacks. The authors argue that DFL robustness has been overestimated due to simplified threat models and fragmented evaluations, and demonstrate that existing defenses fail even with modest (15%) malicious participation rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from the fundamental security vulnerabilities introduced by the architectural shift from centralized to peer-to-peer model exchange in DFL. Without globally coordinated aggregation, DFL becomes particularly susceptible to backdoor attacks. The authors identified that existing studies on DFL security suffer from multiple limitations: simplified threat models, non-adaptive adversaries, fragmented evaluation protocols, inconsistent communication topologies, and ad hoc training configurations, leading to an incomplete understanding of DFL security.

Method: The authors developed BackDFL, a unified benchmark that enables systematic evaluation of DFL under realistic and adaptive backdoor attacks. They conducted extensive experiments across different communication graph topologies to expose critical failure modes of decentralized learning.

Result: The experiments reveal that both state-of-the-art Byzantine-robust DFL methods and adapted FL backdoor defenses fail under modest malicious participation rates as low as 15%. This vulnerability is especially pronounced in heterogeneous settings, and the robustness of defenses varies substantially across different communication graph topologies.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that the robustness of DFL has been significantly overestimated. There is a critical need for more robust defense mechanisms that can handle adaptive adversaries and function effectively across diverse network topologies, particularly in heterogeneous environments.

Abstract: Decentralized Federated Learning (DFL) promises trust-free collaborative learning by replacing the centralized parameter server with peer-to-peer model exchange. However, this architectural shift fundamentally reshapes the threat landscape. Without globally coordinated aggregation, DFL becomes particularly susceptible to backdoor attacks, in which malicious participants implant persistent hidden behaviors while maintaining high clean-task performance. In this paper, we argue that the robustness of DFL has been significantly overestimated. Existing studies rely on simplified threat models, non-adaptive adversaries, fragmented evaluation protocols, inconsistent communication topologies, and ad hoc training configurations, leading to an incomplete understanding of DFL security. To address these limitations, we present BackDFL, a unified benchmark for systematically evaluating DFL under realistic and adaptive backdoor attacks. Through extensive experiments, BackDFL exposes critical failure modes of decentralized learning. Our results demonstrate that both state-of-the-art Byzantine-robust DFL methods and adapted FL backdoor defenses fail under modest malicious participation rates (as low as 15%), especially in heterogeneous settings, while their robustness varies substantially across communication graph topologies.

</details>


### [242] [COEC: Calibrated Orthogonal-Equivalence Compensation for Structured Pruning of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21142)
*Peiqi Yu,Nam Ling,Wei Wang,Wei Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: COEC is a training-free compensation framework for structured pruning in LLMs that applies left and right orthogonal rotations with generalized cross-validation for rescaling, Gram matrix calibration, and alignment penalties to recover performance lost from column removal without requiring backpropagation or retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing training-free compensation methods (additive bias or single orthogonal rotation) only modify the output side of retained weights, leaving the input singular frame unchanged. This limits how retained weights can adapt after column removal, restricting performance recovery.

Method: COEC applies alternating left and right orthogonal rotations to retained weights. The right rotation is optimized on a reduced Stiefel manifold. Singular values are rescaled using generalized cross-validation to select regularization strength per layer. A calibration Gram matrix tempers high-energy activation directions, while an alignment penalty preserves geometric relations between adjacent attention projections. All components use second-order statistics from a small calibration set.

Result: Experiments on Llama-3, Llama-3.1, and Qwen2.5 across multiple sparsity levels show COEC improves perplexity on every model and zero-shot accuracy in most settings over existing compensation methods, with larger gains at higher sparsity.

Conclusion: Post-pruning compensation can recover significant performance lost to column removal. COEC is independent of the column pruning criterion and applicable to multiple structured pruning methods, offering a general framework for training-free LLM compression.

Abstract: Structured pruning reduces the size and inference cost of large language models (LLMs) by removing weight columns, but the resulting output error can degrade accuracy. Existing training-free compensation methods use an additive bias or a single orthogonal rotation on the output side of the retained weight. These corrections leave its input singular frame unchanged and therefore limit how the retained weight can adapt after column removal. We propose COEC (Calibrated Orthogonal-Equivalence Compensation), a training-free compensation framework that applies alternating left and right orthogonal rotations to the retained weight. The right rotation is optimized on a reduced Stiefel manifold, while singular values are rescaled using generalized cross-validation to select the regularization strength for each layer. COEC further tempers the calibration Gram matrix to reduce the dominance of high-energy activation directions and introduces an alignment penalty that preserves the geometric relation between adjacent attention projections.All components use second-order statistics from a small calibration set and require neither backpropagation through the LLM nor retraining of the model parameters. COEC is independent of the column pruning criterion and can be applied to multiple structured pruning methods. Experiments on the Llama-3, Llama-3.1, and Qwen2.5 model families across multiple structured sparsity levels show that COEC improves perplexity on every model and zero-shot accuracy in most settings over existing compensation methods, with larger gains at higher sparsity. These results show that post-pruning compensation can recover part of the performance lost to column removal.

</details>


### [243] [Capturing Cardiac Cyclicity through Phase-Equivariant Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21147)
*Blaise Delaney,Dominic Dootson,Juan Jose Juan Castella,Salil Patel,Andrew Pfaff,Yuji Xing,Jonny Hancox,Karin Sevegnani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Winder is a self-supervised learning method for ECG data that exploits the cyclic nature of cardiac cycles by encoding phase symmetry into the model architecture. It uses a fixed, closed-form transport operator (no learned parameters) to organize representations into phase-invariant coordinates and phase-rotating harmonic subspaces, achieving competitive diagnostic accuracy with ~1M parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The cardiac cycle provides a well-defined cyclic structure that can serve as a natural prior for self-supervised representation learning. The authors argue that explicitly encoding cardiac-phase symmetry can preserve diagnostically useful information while yielding a latent geometry that is legible and tied to measurable physiological quantities.

Method: The paper derives a phase-equivariant self-supervised objective and introduces Winder, a joint-embedding architecture that organizes representations into two components: (1) phase-invariant coordinates and (2) phase-rotating harmonic subspaces. The key innovation is a fixed, closed-form transport operator derived from the cycle's geometry rather than learned, which adds no parameters to the model.

Result: Evaluated on PTB-XL under a frozen linear-probe protocol, Winder achieves diagnostic accuracy within the range of state-of-the-art self-supervised methods while using only ~1M parameters. The model exhibits phase-equivariant latent geometry.

Conclusion: Explicitly encoding cardiac-phase symmetry can preserve diagnostically useful information while yielding a latent geometry that is legible, parameter-efficient, and directly tied to a measurable physiological quantity (cardiac phase).

Abstract: The cyclic structure of physiological processes offers a natural prior for self-supervised representation learning, and the cardiac cycle provides a particularly well-defined setting in which to exploit it. We derive a phase-equivariant self-supervised objective and introduce Winder, a joint-embedding architecture that organises representations into phase-invariant coordinates and phase-rotating harmonic subspaces. Its transport operator is fixed and closed-form, derived from the cycle's geometry rather than learned, and adds no parameters. Evaluated on PTB-XL under a frozen linear-probe protocol, Winder attains diagnostic accuracy within the range reported by state-of-the-art self-supervised methods at a ~1 M parameter footprint, while exhibiting phase-equivariant latent geometry. These findings demonstrate that explicitly encoding cardiac-phase symmetry can preserve diagnostically useful information while yielding a latent geometry that is legible, parameter-efficient, and directly tied to a measurable physiological quantity.

</details>


### [244] [Thermo-FL: Thermal-Aware Robust Federated Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models for Edge AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21172)
*Shiva Shrestha,Kazi Shaharair Sharif,Zongxing Xie,Jiajing Huang,Anhao Xiang,Honghui Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Thermo-FL is a thermal-aware federated LoRA fine-tuning framework that jointly addresses hardware instability (device temperature) and adversarial update corruption in edge LLM adaptation. It adjusts client workload and update density based on thermal state, and uses a robust aggregation pipeline (TERRA) with multiple filtering mechanisms for sparse updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical federated learning deployments face two critical challenges: (1) hardware instability due to thermal constraints causing client throttling, slow training, or delayed aggregation, and (2) adversarial threats from Byzantine clients or communication-layer attackers corrupting updates. Existing approaches address these issues in isolation, but real-world edge LLM adaptation requires joint consideration.

Method: Thermo-FL employs a dual-layer approach: (1) Client-side thermal-aware adaptation - dynamically adjusts active LoRA-layer fraction and transmitted update density based on device temperature; (2) Server-side TERRA aggregation pipeline combining norm filtering, mask-aware directional validation, adaptive active-coordinate clipping, and mask-aware aggregation for robust handling of dynamically sparse LoRA updates. Evaluation uses both a large-scale emulator and Jetson-based physical testbed.

Result: In emulator experiments, Thermo-FL achieves strongest BoolQ accuracy across clean and attack settings while remaining competitive on GSM8K. In physical prototype, it stabilizes device temperature, reduces compressed upload size via bitmap sparse encoding, and preserves GSM8K utility under sign-flip/scale and MITM perturbations.

Conclusion: Secure edge LLM adaptation must jointly consider hardware behavior, workload regulation, sparse communication, and aggregation robustness. The integration of thermal awareness with robust aggregation provides a practical solution for deploying federated LLM fine-tuning on resource-constrained edge devices under adversarial conditions.

Abstract: Federated fine-tuning enables large language models to adapt on edge devices without centralizing private data, but practical deployments must address hardware instability and adversarial update corruption together. Thermally constrained clients may throttle, slow local training, or delay synchronous aggregation, while Byzantine clients and communication-layer adversaries can corrupt the updates used to form the global model. To address these challenges, we present Thermo-FL, a thermal-aware federated LoRA fine-tuning framework that uses device temperature as an active control signal for local adapter training and sparse update transmission. On the client side, Thermo-FL adjusts the active LoRA-layer fraction and transmitted update density as devices heat or cool, reducing workload under thermal stress. On the server side, Thermo-FL introduces TERRA, a robust aggregation pipeline for dynamically sparse LoRA updates that combines norm filtering, mask-aware directional validation, adaptive active-coordinate clipping, and mask-aware aggregation. We evaluate Thermo-FL using both a large-scale emulator and a Jetson-based physical testbed. In the emulator, Thermo-FL improves robustness under adversarial sparse aggregation and achieves the strongest BoolQ accuracy across clean and attack settings while remaining competitive on GSM8K. In the physical prototype, Thermo-FL stabilizes device temperature, reduces compressed upload size through bitmap sparse encoding, and preserves GSM8K utility under sign-flip/scale and MITM perturbations. These results show that secure edge LLM adaptation should jointly consider hardware behavior, workload regulation, sparse communication, and aggregation robustness.

</details>


### [245] [A Neurosymbolic Approach for Constructing Planning Domain Models from Clinical Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21186)
*Ranveer Singh,Saurabh Mathur,Michael Skinner,Prasad Tadepalli,Kristian Kersting,Sriraam Natarajan*

Abstract: Surgical procedures such as laparoscopic appendectomy are complex, high-stakes processes, yet formalizing their workflows for decision support remains a significant challenge. Inducing probabilistic planning domain models in this setting is particularly difficult due to the lack of structured event data and the prevalence of implicit actions in clinical narratives, which neither empirical symbolic methods nor Large Language Models (LLMs) can adequately address on their own. We introduce NSPIN, a neurosymbolic framework for inducing probabilistic planning domain models from unstructured clinical narratives. Our method extracts and imputes structured event sequences from raw text using a pretrained LLM, then induces a PPDDL model and refines its preconditions with LLM-proposed revisions, guided by empirical validation. We evaluate the approach on 2,660 laparoscopic appendectomy notes written by 9 surgeons. NSPIN yields models that generalize to unseen notes, and expert clinical review indicates its induced knowledge is largely consistent with surgical practice.

### [246] [Tydra: An Efficient Hybrid Model for Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21199)
