Abstract: Transformer-based tabular foundation models such as TabPFN achieve strong predictive performance but incur quadratic computational cost with context length. On the other hand, subquadratic SSM-based alternatives such as Hydra trade away accuracy for efficiency. To balance both, we introduce Tydra, a hybrid Transformer-State Space Model (SSM) architecture for tabular in-context learning that interleaves attention and SSM layers. Across 30 OpenML datasets, Tydra reduces inference time by 30% relative to TabPFN while retaining much of its predictive performance. Tydra also outperforms an approximately ten-times-larger Hydra model while providing faster inference. The results indicate that hybrid architectures are a promising direction for tabular foundation models.

</details>


### [247] [Curriculum-Aware Interpolate-then-Refine: Learned Physiological Time-Series Imputation under Realistic Missingness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21207)
*Yu-Chao Huang,Haochen Zhang,Nicholas Konz,Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the problem of imputing missing physiological time series data (e.g., arterial blood pressure, blood glucose). The authors identify that existing imputation methods fail on clinical data because they ignore two key properties: gaps often occur during clinically extreme states, and gap lengths vary across orders of magnitude. They propose CAIR (Curriculum-Aware Interpolate-then-Refine), a two-stage framework combining a bidirectional-GRU interpolator with a Transformer refiner that iteratively corrects estimates over three passes. CAIR outperforms all baselines across different missingness mechanisms (MCAR, MAR, NMAR), with performance margins increasing from 9% to 19% under more challenging conditions. A key finding is that low reconstruction error alone does not guarantee preservation of clinically relevant burden metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern imputation methods perform poorly on physiological time series. A recent benchmark found that simple linear interpolation outperformed every learned imputer on real-world clinical signals. This poor performance stems from two overlooked properties of physiological missingness: (1) gaps may occur when the signal is clinically extreme rather than typical, and (2) gap lengths can easily span orders of magnitude. The authors argue that generic imputers fail because they don't account for these domain-specific characteristics.

Method: The authors propose Curriculum-Aware Interpolate-then-Refine (CAIR), a two-stage framework for physiological time-series imputation. The key idea is to learn a coarse base curve and then repeatedly correct it toward physiological realism, rather than predicting a gap in a single pass. CAIR consists of: (1) a bidirectional-GRU interpolator that generates initial estimates, and (2) a Transformer refiner that corrects its own estimate over three successive passes. The model is trained jointly under a broad, signal-agnostic random-gap curriculum. Evaluation is stratified by gap length and missingness mechanism (MCAR, MAR, NMAR) rather than using a single average metric.

Result: CAIR achieves the most accurate imputation under every missingness mechanism on both datasets (AI-READI for continuous glucose monitoring and MIMIC-III for arterial pressure in intensive care). The performance margin over the strongest baseline grows with difficulty: 9% under MCAR (most random) to 19% under value-dependent dropout (NMAR), where generic learned imputers are weakest. Additionally, the authors demonstrate that low reconstruction error alone does not recover burden metrics clinicians act on: interpolants matching CAIR's error fail to preserve those metrics, imputers that recover them are far less accurate, and CAIR alone ranks among the best on both axes.

Conclusion: CAIR successfully addresses the unique challenges of physiological time-series imputation by using a two-stage interpolate-then-refine approach with iterative correction. By accounting for the specific properties of clinical missingness, it outperforms all baselines across different missingness mechanisms, with increasing advantages under more challenging conditions. The paper also highlights the importance of evaluating imputers on clinically relevant metrics beyond simple reconstruction error, as CAIR uniquely balances both accuracy and burden metric preservation.

Abstract: Imputing physiological time series (arterial blood pressure, blood glucose, etc.) is essential for addressing the missingness that pervades clinical data. Yet modern imputation methods perform poorly in this domain: a recent benchmark found that simple linear interpolation outperformed every learned imputer on real-world clinical signals with realistic gaps. We show that this reflects two properties of physiological missingness that generic imputers ignore: gaps may occur when the signal is clinically extreme rather than typical, and gap lengths can easily span orders of magnitude. To this end, we introduce Curriculum-Aware Interpolate-then-Refine (CAIR), a two-stage framework for physiological time-series imputation. Our key motivation is to learn a coarse base curve and then repeatedly correct it toward physiological realism, rather than predict a gap in a single pass. Consequently, CAIR couples a bidirectional-GRU interpolator with a Transformer refiner that corrects its own estimate over three successive passes, trained jointly under a broad, signal-agnostic random-gap curriculum. We evaluate imputers stratified by gap length and missingness mechanism (MCAR, MAR, NMAR) rather than by a single average, and CAIR is the most accurate under every mechanism on continuous glucose monitoring (AI-READI) and arterial pressure in intensive care (MIMIC-III). Its margin over the strongest baseline grows with difficulty, from 9% under MCAR to 19% under value-dependent dropout, where generic learned imputers are weakest. We further show low reconstruction error alone does not recover the burden metrics clinicians act on: interpolants matching CAIR's error fail to preserve those metrics, imputers that recover them are far less accurate, and CAIR alone ranks among the best on both axes.

</details>


### [248] [TRACE-C: Rank-Calibrated Relational Anomaly Detection for Multi-Stream Operational Telemetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21251)
*Matthew Faucher*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRACE-C is an auditable strictly-prior rank-calibrated detector for aligned multi-stream telemetry that uses rolling median/MAD residuals fed through three window channels (maximum normalized local sum, Gaussian copula-form dependence contrast, and worst standardized AR(1) innovation), with channel ranks Fisher-aggregated and ranked against earlier aggregates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Detect joint anomalies in operational telemetry where individual streams remain within familiar ranges but exhibit anomalous behavior collectively. Existing methods may miss such multi-stream anomalies.

Method: Uses same-regime rolling median/MAD residuals feeding three window channels: (1) maximum normalized local sum, (2) Gaussian copula-form dependence contrast on robust-z residuals, and (3) worst standardized AR(1) innovation. Channel ranks are Fisher-aggregated and ranked against earlier aggregates. Applied to six Great Britain grid streams with 2019 fit, July-December 2019 development, and 2020 hold-out validation.

Result: TRACE-C ranked Storm Atiyah first among 2019 test windows, but channel ablation revealed this was attributable to the local channel (copula-only ranked it 59th). The 9 August frequency event ranked 143 by fused detector vs 40 by temporal channel alone. In 2020, no window was selected; the highest-ranked frozen window was later identified as Storm Ellen.

Conclusion: Three interpretive limits apply: p-values are selection quantities not event probabilities; the copula-form channel is not a literal copula density (no probability-integral or normal-score transform applied); empirical rank counts are diagnostics, not coverage or false-discovery proofs. The method shows that joint anomaly detection requires careful channel analysis, as single-channel performance may not reflect fused detector behavior.

Abstract: Operational telemetry can be jointly anomalous while every individual stream stays inside its familiar range. TRACE-C is an auditable strictly-prior rank-calibrated detector for aligned multi-stream telemetry: same-regime rolling median/MAD residuals feed three window channels -- a maximum normalized local sum, a Gaussian copula-form dependence contrast on robust-z residuals, and a worst standardized AR(1) innovation -- whose channel ranks are Fisher-aggregated and ranked against earlier aggregates.
  We evaluate six Great Britain grid streams with a January-April 2019 fit, July-December 2019 development evidence, and a 2020 hold-out frozen before inspection. TRACE-C ranks Storm Atiyah first among 2019 test windows, but a disclosed channel ablation attributes that rank to the local channel, not the copula-form channel: copula-only ranks Atiyah 59th. The short 9 August frequency event is ranked far lower by the fused detector (143) than by the temporal channel alone (40), and reconstruction baselines rank it first. In 2020 no window is selected, which is consistent with record-rule saturation rather than an uneventful year; the highest-ranked frozen window was later interpreted as Storm Ellen.
  Three interpretive limits carry throughout. The resulting p-values are selection quantities, not event probabilities. The copula-form channel is not a literal copula density: the method applies no probability-integral or normal-score transform. Empirical rank counts are diagnostics, not coverage or false-discovery proofs. Every table and figure in this paper is generated from committed machine-readable reports.

</details>


### [249] [ConceptTS: LLM-Guided Concept Bottlenecks for Interpretable Multivariate Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21277)
*Yichen Jiang,Yueqiao Chen,Dongyu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ConceptTS is an interpretable multivariate time-series forecasting framework that uses LLMs to generate human-readable concepts organized in three bottlenecks, achieving competitive accuracy while providing transparent predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-of-the-art multivariate time-series forecasters lack transparency and interpretability, restricting their use in practical applications where understanding prediction factors is essential.

Method: ConceptTS uses a large language model to propose task-relevant concepts and generate executable labeling rules without manual annotation. Concepts are organized into three complementary bottlenecks: historical context, local forecast intervals, and full forecast horizon. A shared decoder combines representations from predicted concept activations to produce forecasts.

Result: On the Beijing Multi-Site Air Quality dataset, ConceptTS achieves accuracy competitive with strong black-box baselines while producing semantically meaningful concept activations.

Conclusion: ConceptTS successfully provides interpretable predictions without sacrificing accuracy, making the model's decision process explicit and supporting concept-level interventions.

Abstract: State-of-the-art multivariate time-series forecasters can model complex temporal and cross-variable dependencies, yet their opaque representations provide limited insight into why a particular forecast is produced. This lack of transparency restricts their use in settings where practitioners must understand and assess the factors underlying a prediction. We introduce ConceptTS, an interpretable forecasting framework that organizes its predictions around named, human-readable concepts. ConceptTS uses a large language model to propose task-relevant concepts and generate executable labeling rules, translating the language model's domain knowledge into direct supervision without costly manual concept annotation. The proposed concepts are organized into three complementary bottlenecks that describe the historical context, local forecast intervals, and the full forecast horizon. A shared decoder combines representations derived from their predicted activations to construct the forecast, making the model's decision process explicit and supporting direct concept-level interventions. Experiments on the Beijing Multi-Site Air Quality dataset show that ConceptTS achieves accuracy competitive with strong black-box baselines while producing semantically meaningful concept activations.

</details>


### [250] [SPARCL: Spectral Partitioned Analytic Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21307)
*James Hartley,Zeropy Surio,Daniel Whitmore,Hannah Clarke,Thomas Reed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Analytic continual learning has emerged as a strong exemplar-free alternative to gradient-based class-incremental learning because it replaces iterative optimization with closed-form ridge updates. Yet the usual forgetting narrative, centered on stochastic gradient overwriting, does not explain why analytic methods still drift on old classes despite exact recursive solvers. We identify the culprit as spectral interference: the joint ridge classifier for all tasks shares the inverse autocorrelation operator $(R+λI)^{-1}$, so incoming task samples that load onto old dominant eigendirections dilute the spectrum and perturb old-class logits even when old labels are never revisited. Based on this view, we propose SPARCL, a spectral partitioned analytic continual learner that decomposes the running autocorrelation into a high-energy core and a residual complement, freezes old-class classifier components in the core subspace, and updates only the residual block through recursive least squares with an optional residual random-projection expansion. This yields a simple closed-form update with a provable invariance guarantee for the core contribution of old logits. Across CIFAR-100, CUB-200, ImageNet-R, and ImageNet-A under a frozen ViT-B/16 protocol, SPARCL closes most of the gap from classical analytic learners to strong representation matchers, while remaining complementary to sparse feature-decorrelation approaches such as Fly-CL.

</details>


### [251] [Rethinking Expressivity and Efficiency in Test-Time Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21308)
*Zeyun Zhong,Joya Chen,Manuel Martin,Frederik Diederichs,Juergen Gall,Juergen Beyerer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: E²-TTT is a novel method that enables expressive per-token update dynamics during Test-Time Training while maintaining hardware efficiency through closed-form state transitions, achieving strong performance on long-context tasks with 1.3B parameter models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Test-Time Training methods struggle to balance the expressivity of per-token update dynamics with the hardware efficiency of chunk-wise approximations, limiting their applicability for long-context processing.

Method: Under the standard approximation of taking gradients at chunk-start weights, derive a closed-form state transition that exactly reproduces the chunk-end fast-weight and momentum states of the per-token recurrence, enabling fully parallelized chunk-level training while preserving temporal structure.

Result: E²-TTT performs on par with previous TTT and hybrid attention baselines in language modeling while outperforming them on in-context retrieval. It retains over 90% accuracy at 8× training context length on the Needle in a Haystack passkey test, while matching the training throughput of efficient chunk-wise methods.

Conclusion: E²-TTT effectively reconciles expressivity with efficiency in Test-Time Training, providing a practical solution for long-context processing that maintains the temporal structure of update rules while achieving hardware-efficient parallelization.

Abstract: Test-Time Training (TTT) enables long-context processing via continuous weight updates during inference, but current methods struggle to balance the expressivity of per-token update dynamics with the hardware efficiency of chunk-wise approximations. We propose E$^2$-TTT (Expressive and Efficient TTT) to bridge this gap. Under the standard approximation of taking gradients at the chunk-start weights, we derive a closed-form state transition that exactly reproduces the chunk-end fast-weight and momentum states of the per-token recurrence. This enables fully parallelized chunk-level training while preserving the temporal structure of the update rule that prior chunk-wise methods discard. We validate E$^2$-TTT by training models up to 1.3B parameters from scratch. It performs on par with previous TTT and hybrid attention baselines in language modeling while outperforming them on in-context retrieval. Its advantage is most pronounced in length extrapolation: on the standard ``Needle in a Haystack'' passkey test, it retains over 90% accuracy at $8\times$ the training context length. Meanwhile, E$^2$-TTT can match the training throughput of efficient chunk-wise methods, demonstrating that it effectively reconciles expressivity with efficiency. The code is available at https://github.com/zeyun-zhong/E2-TTT.

</details>


### [252] [Time-Aware Tranformer-Based Prediction Model for AECOPD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21324)
*Weihao Qu,Ling Zheng,Dongyang Wang,Jiacun Wang,Haowen Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Time-Aware transformer-based model to predict Acute Exacerbation of COPD (AECOPD) using only respiratory data from home ventilators, addressing the latency issue in current ML-based prediction methods that rely on clinical/lab data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ML models for AECOPD prediction rely on clinical and laboratory data, which introduces latency. The rapid symptom changes in AECOPD require time-sensitive prediction. This paper addresses the need for timely detection by focusing on home monitoring scenarios using only daily-use ventilator data.

Method: The proposed Time-Aware transformer-based AECOPD prediction model uses a Time-Aware transformer architecture to generate patient representations that capture both symptoms and their temporal progression from ventilator data. The approach is designed for home monitoring scenarios where only respiratory data is available.

Result: The experimental results demonstrate that the Time-Aware transformer-based approach outperforms traditional methods in multiple classification tasks for AECOPD prediction.

Conclusion: The Time-Aware transformer-based approach shows potential to enhance AECOPD prediction accuracy by effectively capturing temporal patterns in ventilator respiratory data, offering a promising solution for timely AECOPD detection in home monitoring settings.

Abstract: The rapid symptom change of Acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (AECOPD) makes it critical to have time-sensitive prediction models. However, most current machine learning models studying AECOPD use clinical and laboratory data, which will inevitably cause latency. To ensure timely detection of AECOPD and minimize latency, this paper focuses on home monitoring scenarios where only respiratory data from daily-use ventilators is available. We introduce a Time-Aware transformer-based AECOPD prediction model, which generates meaningful patient representations using the Time-Aware transformer to capture the symptoms and their temporal progression in ventilator data. Our experimental results demonstrate that our Time-Aware transformer-based approach outperforms traditional methods in multiple classification tasks, highlighting its potential to enhance AECOPD prediction accuracy.

</details>


### [253] [Across-Design Uncertainty in Short Pricing Panels: Evidence from Simulated Price Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21334)
*Pedro Cadahia Delgado*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper examines the inferential consequences of sparse pricing in short observational panels—where many observations yield only a small number of distinct price movements. Using synthetic data calibrated to sparse pricing regimes, the study separates uncertainty conditional on realized price trajectories from variation across alternative trajectories. The key finding shows that across-trajectory variation accounts for 97.6% of estimation error variance, which standard within-panel resampling fails to capture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from a practical problem in pricing panel analysis: short observational panels often contain many observations but offer only limited distinct price movements. This sparsity creates inferential challenges that standard methods fail to address, particularly because within-panel resampling cannot capture the across-design variation in estimation error.

Method: The authors use a synthetic data-generating process calibrated to sparse pricing regimes. They employ gradient-boosted specifications and separate uncertainty conditional on realized price trajectories from variation in estimation error across alternative trajectories. They examine within-panel resampling procedures and test the empirical relation σ_hat ≈ 0.182 V^(-0.271), where V = moves × magnitude squared. They also explore adding regions with common price paths and averaging across units with independent design-specific errors. Finally, they apply Paule-Mandel variance component estimation across independently priced units.

Result: The across-design component accounts for 97.6% of the variance of estimation error in baseline simulations. The empirical relation σ_hat ≈ 0.182 V^(-0.271) provides a good description of across-design dispersion. Adding regions with common price paths reduces outcome noise but doesn't create independent price trajectories, while averaging across units with independent design-specific errors reduces dispersion at the standard square root rate. The Paule-Mandel variance component substantially increases empirical coverage from 0.469 to 0.931.

Conclusion: The broader implication is a methodological shift: rather than relying solely on fixed passive panels, researchers should design data-generating processes that create independent identifying variation. Standard within-panel resampling procedures are insufficient for capturing the full extent of estimation uncertainty in sparse pricing regimes.

Abstract: Short observational pricing panels can contain many observations while offering only a small number of distinct price movements. This paper studies the inferential consequences of that distinction in a synthetic data-generating process calibrated to a sparse pricing regime. We separate uncertainty conditional on a realised price trajectory from variation in estimation error across alternative trajectories generated by the same pricing process. In the baseline simulations, the latter component accounts for 97.6% of the variance of estimation error for the gradient-boosted specification. Within-panel resampling procedures use the information of one realised trajectory and do not identify this across-design component.
  Three results organise the analysis. First, across-design dispersion is well described by the empirical relation sigma_hat approx 0.182 V^(-0.271), where V equals moves times magnitude squared. Second, adding regions sharing a common price path reduces outcome noise but does not create independent price trajectories; conversely, averaging across units with independent design-specific errors reduces dispersion at the standard square root rate. Third, a Paule-Mandel variance component estimated across independently priced units substantially increases empirical coverage in homogeneous simulations, from 0.469 to 0.931. The broader implication is a shift toward designing data-generating processes that create independent identifying variation rather than relying solely on fixed passive panels.

</details>


### [254] [Asymmetric Capacity Allocation in Self-Refinement Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21345)
*Zhuoyi Yang,Ian G. Harris,Salar Hashemitaheri,Cassie Huang,Yuangang Li,Hyunwoo Oh,Paul Dourish,Tony Givargis,Mohsen Imani,Li Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic study of how model size affects each stage (generation, critique, revision) of the self-refinement pipeline in LLMs. Through experiments on 5 benchmarks using Qwen3 (6 sizes) and Gemma 3 (4 sizes), the authors find that larger generators and refiners improve performance, but critic size has minimal impact. They conclude that model capacity should be allocated non-uniformly across stages for computational efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing approaches treat model size as an implementation detail rather than studying its effects on each stage of self-refinement. This may lead to resource waste since different stages (generation, critique, revision) involve different cognitive demands. The authors aim to determine whether effective self-refinement requires equally capable models for all three stages.

Method: The authors conducted a comprehensive stage-wise model size study using 6 model sizes of Qwen3 and 4 model sizes of Gemma 3 across 5 benchmarks from different domains. They systematically varied the model size for each stage (generator, critic, refiner) independently to examine how size affects performance at each stage.

Result: Key findings: (1) Larger generators and refiners generally improve pipeline performance, while an undersized refiner can actually harm performance; (2) Performance is highly insensitive to critic size, though including even a small critic consistently outperforms omitting critique altogether.

Conclusion: Model capacity should not be allocated uniformly across self-refinement pipelines. Different stages exhibit distinct size scaling characteristics. This provides practical guidance for designing more computationally efficient multi-stage language model systems by appropriately sizing each component.

Abstract: Self-refinement, typically structured as generation, critique, and revision, is a widely adopted paradigm for improving LLM generation and serves as a core mechanism in many LLM agents. While the three stages involve different cognitive demands, most existing approaches conveniently treat the model size as an implementation detail rather than a subject of study, which may lead to a waste of resources. Little work has systematically examined how model size affects each stage or whether effective self-refinement requires equally capable models for generation, critique, and revision. We present the first stage-wise model size study of the self-refinement pipeline on 5 benchmarks from different domains using 6 model sizes of Qwen3 and 4 model sizes of Gemma 3. We conclude that larger generators and refiners generally improve the pipeline, whereas an undersized refiner can even harm performance. Second, performance is highly insensitive to the size of the critic, although including even a small critic consistently outperforms omitting critique altogether. Our findings demonstrate that model capacity should not be allocated uniformly across self-refinement pipelines. Instead, different stages exhibit distinct size scaling characteristics, providing practical guidance for designing more computationally efficient multi-stage language model systems.

</details>
